may switch from important to unimportant over the course of the algorithm, but it may never switch in the opposite direction. This allows us to guarantee that the set
V^{F}(C)
of vertices remains decremental, which is crucial since the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
data structure does not support edge insertions, except in the case of supernode splitting. We defer the specific definition of important supernodes for later, but they are defined so that, on the one hand, we can control the cardinalities of the sets
V^{F}(C)\cap S^{L}_{k}
of vertices (which is sufficient in order to make our construction efficient), while, on the other hand, still allowing us to support
short-path
queries.

We now proceed to formally define the level-
0
data structure. We then define the data structures for levels
1\leq L\leq q
. Finally, we provide an algorithm for responding to
short-path
queries.

Throughout, we use the following parameters.
We let
\Delta=\hat{m}^{16\epsilon^{3}}
, and, for all
0\leq L\leq q
, we let
\mu_{L}=(4\Delta)^{L+1}
.

We will ensure that, for every level
0\leq L\leq q
, the dynamic degree bound of graph
H^{L}
is at most
\mu_{L}
, and every regular vertex of
H^{L}
may lie in at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
over the course of a single level-
L
phase.

We denote
\mu^{\prime}=\mu_{q}=(4\Delta)^{q+1}
, so the dynamic degree bounds of all graphs
H^{L}
that we maintain are bounded by
\mu^{\prime}
. Notice that:

\mu^{\prime}\leq(4\hat{m}^{16\epsilon^{3}})^{\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil+1}\leq\hat{m}^{64\epsilon^{2}}.

(6)

4.1
Level-
0
Data Structure

Recall that there is a single level-
0
phase, that lasts for the whole duration of the time interval
{\mathcal{T}}
– the time horizon for
\Sigma
.
The level-
0
data structure is initialized once at the beginning of the algorithm. We construct a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{0}=\left(H^{0}=(V^{0},U^{0},\tilde{E}^{0}),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in\tilde{E}^{0}},\hat{D}\right)
, that, over the course of the time interval
{\mathcal{T}}
, undergoes a sequence of valid update operations. Additionally, for all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, we will maintain data structure
DS^{0}_{i}
, that, intuitively, solves the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on
{\mathcal{J}}^{0}
with distance parameter
D_{i}
.
We now define the data structures that the algorithm maintains more formally.
We start by providing an algorithm for initializing the level-
0
data structure, and then provide an algorithm for updating it. Lastly, we show that the resulting level-
0
data structure can support approximate short-path queries between pairs of vertices
x,y\in V(G)
, such that there is a level-
0
path of length at most
D^{*}
connecting
x
to
y
in the current graph
G
.

Initialization.

We initialize a bipartite level-
0
graph
H^{0}=(V^{0},U^{0},\tilde{E}^{0})
, where vertices of
V^{0}
are called regular vertices, and vertices of
U^{0}
are called supernodes.
The initial graph
H^{0}
is constructed as follows. For every vertex
x\in V(G)
, we introduce a regular vertex
v^{0}(x)
, that is added to
V^{0}
, and a supernode
u^{0}(x)
, that is added to
U^{0}
. We also add an edge
(v^{0}(x),u^{0}(x))
, whose length is
1
. We refer to vertices in
\left\{v^{0}(x)\mid x\in V(G)\right\}
as
type-1 regular vertices
, and to supernodes in set
\left\{u^{0}(x)\mid x\in V(G)\right\}
as
type-1 supernodes
.

For every edge
e=(x,y)\in E(G)
, we include a regular vertex
v^{0}(e)
in
V^{0}
. The vertex connects to supernodes
u^{0}(x)
and
u^{0}(y)
with edges, whose lengths are
\ell(e)
each. We refer to the set
\left\{v^{0}(e)\mid e\in E(G)\right\}
of vertices as
type-2 regular vertices
. Level-
0
graph
H^{0}
does not contain type-2 supernodes, unlike graphs from higher levels.
This completes the definition of the initial graph
H^{0}
.

For all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, we also define a graph
H^{0}_{i}
, that is defined exactly like
H^{0}
, except that it does not include edges of
H^{0}
whose length is greater than
D_{i}
.

For all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, we have now defined a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{0}_{i}=\left(H^{0}_{i},\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H^{0}_{i})},D_{i}\right)
.
Below, we define an online sequence of valid update operations for each such graph
H^{0}_{i}
. We can therefore view
{\mathcal{J}}^{0}_{i}
as an instance of the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem. The number of regular vertices in graph
H^{0}_{i}
is bounded by
|V(G^{0})|+|E(G^{0})|\leq 4m
. We let
\hat{W}=\max\left\{4m\cdot\mu_{0},\hat{m}\right\}\leq\max\left\{m\cdot\hat{m}^{64\epsilon^{2}},\hat{m}\right\}\leq n^{3}
.
We initialize the data structure associated with algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem from
1.1

on this instance of
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
with the parameter
\hat{W}
that we defined. We denote the corresponding data structure by
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i}
, and we denote by
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}_{i}
the collection of clusters (the weak neighborhood cover) that the algorithm maintains. For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}_{i}
, we say that the
scale
of
C
is
i
, and we denote
\operatorname{scale}(C)=i
. Recall that the approximation factor that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
achieves is
\alpha(\hat{W})\leq\alpha
.

Updates.

As graph
G
undergoes a sequence
\Sigma
of edge insertions and deletions, we update graph
H^{0}
as follows. Consider any update to graph
G
that appears in
\Sigma
. If the update is the insertion of an edge into
G
, then we ignore it. Assume now that the update is the deletion of some edge
e
from
G
. If edge
e
is not an original edge of
G
(that is, it was inserted at some point into
G
), then we also ignore it. Otherwise, we delete both edges that are incident to supernode
v^{0}(e)
from
H^{0}
.
This concludes the description of an algorithm that, given the online sequence
\Sigma
of edge insertions and deletions for graph
G
, produces an update sequence for graph
H^{0}
. Notice that we only employ edge-deletion operations to graph
H^{0}
. We never perform vertex-deletion or supernode-splitting. It is therefore immediate to verify that the dynamic degree bound of graph
H^{0}
is at most
2\leq\mu_{0}
. It is immediate to verify that the time required to initialize graph
H^{0}
, and to produce the update sequence for it, given
\Sigma
, is bounded by
O(m)
. Notice also that the number of regular vertices in graph
H_{0}
is bounded by
4m
. Therefore, for all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, the time that is required in order to maintain data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i}
is bounded by:

O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\mu_{0}^{O(1/\epsilon)}\right)\leq O\left(m^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\right)\leq O\left(m\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\right).

The total update time that is required in order to maintain the whole level-
0
data structure, that we denote by
\mathsf{DS}^{0}
, is then bounded by:

T^{0}\leq O\left(m\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot\log\hat{D}\right)\leq O\left(m\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\right),

since
\log\hat{D}\leq\hat{m}^{\epsilon^{12}}
from Inequality
5
.

Throughout the algorithm, we denote
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\log\hat{D}}{\mathcal{C}}^{0}_{i}
. Since, for all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, a regular vertex of
H^{0}
may belong to at most
\hat{W}^{4\epsilon^{3}}\leq\hat{m}^{8\epsilon^{3}}
clusters over the course of the algorithm,
we get that every regular vertex of
H^{0}
may belong to at most
2\hat{m}^{8\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\log\hat{D}\leq\hat{m}^{16\epsilon^{3}}=\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
over the course of the algorithm.

Recall that we denote by
\mathsf{DS}^{0}
all level-
0
data structures that we maintain. For convenience, for all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, for every regular vertex
v\in V(H^{0})
, we denote the cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
that data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i}
maintains by
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{0}_{i}(v)
. Similarly, we denote the list
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}(v)
of all clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}_{i}
containing
v
that data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i}
maintains by
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{0}_{i}(v)
. We then let
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{0}(v)=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\log\hat{D}}\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{0}_{i}(v)
. Lastly, for all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, if
C
is a cluster that currently lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}_{i}
, and
v,v^{\prime}
are two regular vertices of
H^{0}
that currently lie in
C
, we denote by
\mbox{\sf{short-path}}^{0}_{i}(C,v,v^{\prime})
a query
\mbox{\sf{short-path}}(C,v,v^{\prime})
to data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i}
. Recall that the data structure must return a path
P\subseteq H^{0}
of length at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i}
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
H^{0}_{i}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. For convenience, we will think of
\mbox{\sf{short-path}}^{0}_{i}(C,v,v^{\prime})
as a query that is supported by data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}
.

Supporting short-path queries.

Next, we show an algorithm that supports short-path queries between pairs of vertices
x,y\in V(G)
, provided that there is a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in the current graph
G
, whose length is at most
D^{*}
, and level is
0
.
We start with the following simple claim.

Claim 4.1
.

Let
x,y
be any pair of vertices of
G
, and assume that, at some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, there is a level-
0
path
P
in graph
G
, that connects
x
to
y
, such that the length of
P
is at most
D\leq D^{*}
. Then there is a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H^{0}
, connecting
v^{0}(x)
to
v^{0}(y)
, whose length is at most
2D+2
.

Proof.

Let
x=z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}=y
be the sequence of vertices on path
P
. For
1\leq j\leq r-1
, denote by
e_{j}=(z_{j},z_{j+1})
the
j
th edge on the path. Consider the following sequence of vertices in graph
H^{0}
:

\left(v^{0}(z_{1}),u^{0}(z_{1}),v^{0}(e_{1}),u^{0}(z_{2}),v^{0}(e_{2}),\ldots,v^{0}(e_{r-1}),u^{0}(z_{r}),v^{0}(z_{r})\right).

It is immediate to verify that this sequence defines a valid path in graph
H^{0}
, and the length of the path is
2+\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}2\ell(e_{j})\leq 2+2D
.

We then obtain the following easy corollary.

Corollary 4.2
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, at any time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, given a pair
x,y
of vertices of
G
, and a distance parameter
D\leq D^{*}
that is an integral power of
2
, responds ”YES” or ”NO”, in time
O(\log\hat{m})
. If the algorithm responds ”NO”, then
G
does not contain a path connecting
x
to
y
of length at most
D
that belongs to level
0
. If the algorithm responds ”YES”, then it can, additionally, compute a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
G
, connecting
x
to
y
, whose length is at most
4D\cdot\alpha
, in time
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)
.

We note that, once the algorithm from
Corollary

4.2
responds “YES” or ”NO”, we may choose to terminate it. Alternatively, if it responds “YES”, we may choose to continue the algorithm to obtain the desired path
P^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose we are given a pair
x,y
of vertices of
G
and a distance parameter
D
, that is an integral power of
2
. We let
i=\log D
. Notice that, from
4.1
, if there exists a level-
0
path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in
G
, whose length is at most
D
, such that all edges of
P
are original edges, then there must be a path
\tilde{P}
in graph
H^{0}
, of length at most
4D
, connecting
v^{0}(x)
to
v^{0}(y)
. We use data structure
DS^{0}_{i+2}
to compute a cluster
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{0}_{i+2}(v^{0}(x))
. We then check whether
v^{0}(y)\in v^{0}(C)
. If this is not the case, then we are guaranteed that there is no level-
0
path in
G
of length at most
D
that connects
x
to
y
. Therefore, if
v^{0}(y)\not\in V(C)
, we return “NO”, and otherwise we return “YES”. Since the vertices of
V(C)
are maintained in a sorted list, and
|V(H^{0})|\leq\hat{m}
, the running time of the algorithm so far is bounded by
O(\log\hat{m})
.

If the algorithm responds “YES”, then we are guaranteed that
v^{0}(x),v^{0}(y)\in V(C)
. We can then execute query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}^{0}_{i+2}(C,v^{0}(x),v^{0}(y))
in data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i+2}
. The data structure must return a path
\tilde{P}^{\prime}
in the current graph
H^{0}
, of length at most
\alpha\cdot 4D_{i}
, connecting
v^{0}(x)
to
v^{0}(y)
, in time
O(|E(\tilde{P}^{\prime})|)
.
Since each type-1 regular vertex in
H^{0}
has degree
1
, we can assume that no such vertex serves as an inner vertex on path
\tilde{P}^{\prime}
.

We now transform path
\tilde{P}^{\prime}
in graph
H^{0}
into a path
P^{\prime}
, connecting
x
to
y
, in graph
G
. In order to do so, we delete the first and the last vertices of
\tilde{P}^{\prime}
, suppress all type-2 regular vertices, and replace every supernode
u^{0}(z)
on the path with the corresponding vertex
z\in V(G)
. It is easy to verify that the resulting path
P^{\prime}
connects
x
to
y
in the current graph
G
, and its length remains bounded by
4\alpha\cdot D_{i}
. The running time of this part of the algorithm is
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)
.

Flattened Sets of Vertices.

For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
, we now define a flattened set
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices. We do not maintain these sets of vertices explicitly, but we will use this definition in order to maintain data structures from higher levels. Consider some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, and a cluster
C
that belonged to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
, at time
\tau
. We let the flattened set
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices contain every vertex
x\in V(G)
, such that the corresponding regular vertex
v^{0}(x)
lies in
V(C)
. Note that, from the definition of allowed updates to the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
of clusters in the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, regular vertices may not join a cluster after it is created. Therefore, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
, the corresponding flattened set
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices is decremental: once cluster
C
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
and set
V^{F}(C)
is initialized, vertices may leave it but they cannot join it.

4.2
Level-
L
Data Structure

We now consider an integer
0<L\leq q
, and provide the description of level-
L
data structure. Recall that the purpose of the data structure is to support approximate
short-path
queries between pairs of vertices
x,y\in V(G)
, such that there is a level-
L
path in
G
connecting
x
to
y
, whose length is at most
D^{*}
.

We assume that for all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, the level-
L^{\prime}
data structure is defined already, and in particular for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, the flattened set
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices is already defined (recall that we do not maintain these vertex sets explicitly).

Recall that the timeline is partitioned into
M^{L}
level-
L
phases, each of which spans a contiguous sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
of updates to graph
G
, that contains at most
M^{q-L}
edge insertions. For all
1\leq k\leq M^{L}
, the
k
th level-
L
phase is denoted by
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, the time interval corresponding to phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
is denoted by
{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
, and the sequence of updates that graph
G
undergoes during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
is denoted by
\Sigma^{L}_{k}\subseteq\Sigma
. From the definition of the hierarchical partition of the time line into phases, for all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
is completely contained in some level-
L^{\prime}
phase.
Let
\Phi^{L-1}_{k^{\prime}}
be the level-
(L-1)
phase that contains
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
Recall that we have denoted by
A^{L}_{k}
the collection of all edges that were inserted into graph
G
between time
\tau^{\prime}
– the beginning of level-
(L-1)
phase
\Phi^{L-1}_{k^{\prime}}
, and time
\tau
– the beginning of level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
(including
\tau^{\prime}
and excluding
\tau
). Clearly,
|A^{L}_{k}|\leq M^{q-L+1}
holds.
We also denoted by
S^{L}_{k}
the collection of vertices of
G
that serve as endpoints of the edges of
A^{L}_{k}
. The set
A^{L}_{k}
of edges remains unchanged over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, even if some edges of
A^{L}_{k}
are deleted from graph
G
. Similarly, the set
S^{L}_{k}
of vertices does not change over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

At the beginning of every level-
L
phase, we initialize the level-
L
data structures from scratch. Notice that, if
\tau
is the time when some level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
starts, it is possible that
\tau
is also the starting time of some level-
L^{\prime}
phase, for
1\leq L^{\prime}<L
. However, in this case, time
\tau
must also be the start of a level-
(L-1)
phase, and so
A^{L}_{k}=\emptyset
must hold. In this case, the level-
L
data structure, graph
H^{L}
, and the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
of clusters remain empty over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. From now on we assume that, when a level-
L
data structure is being initialized at the beginning of some level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
have already been initialized.

We now consider some integer
1\leq k\leq M^{L}
, and provide a description of the level-
L
data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}
that is maintained over the course of level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
As mentioned already, we assume that data structures for levels
0,\ldots,L-1
are already defined and initialized.

Recall that, for all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, for each cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, the level-
L^{\prime}
data structure defines a flattened set
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices. This set is initialized once cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
.
Notice that, once a cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, it remains in this set for the duration of the current level-
L^{\prime}
phase (though the cluster may eventually become empty), and hence for the duration of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
For all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, for every level-
L^{\prime}
phase
\Phi^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
, we assume that the following properties hold:

P1.

For every cluster
C
that lied in
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
at any time during Phase
\Phi^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
, the flattened set
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices is initialized when cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, and after that vertices may leave
V^{F}(C)
but not join it;

P2.

For every vertex
x\in V(G)
, there are at most
(4\Delta)^{L^{\prime}+1}
clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, such that
x
ever belonged to
V^{F}(C)
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
; and

P3.

If a new cluster
C
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
at some time
\tau
during Phase
\Phi^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
, due to a cluster splitting operation that is applied to a cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
with
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
, then, at time
\tau
,
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V^{F}(C^{\prime})
holds.

Notice that all these properties hold for the level-
0
data structure. We assume that they hold for data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
, and we describe a construction of the level-
L
data structure that will ensure these properties for level
L
.

The next observation will be useful for us later.

Observation 4.3
.

Let
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
be a level, and assume that the level-
L^{\prime}
data structure ensures properties
P1
and
P3
. Consider any level-
L^{\prime}
phase
\Phi^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
, and a cluster
C
that belonged to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
at time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
. Consider some time
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
with
\tau^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
, and let
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
. Denote by
S
the set
V^{F}(C)
at time
\tau
, and by
S^{\prime}
the set
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
at time
\tau^{\prime}
. Then
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
must hold.

Proof.

Consider a level
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, a level-
L^{\prime}
phase
\Phi^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
, and a cluster
C
that lied in set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
at some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
. We define a dynamic set of vertices
Q\subseteq V(G)
, for all time points
\tau^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
with
\tau^{\prime\prime}\leq\tau
. For each such time point
\tau^{\prime\prime}
, set
Q^{(\tau^{\prime\prime})}
is equal to the set
V^{F}(\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime\prime})}(C))
at time
\tau^{\prime\prime}
. From properties
P1
and
P3
it is immediate to verify that set
Q
of vertices is decremental. The observation then follows since
Q^{(\tau)}=S
and
Q^{(\tau^{\prime})}=S^{\prime}
.

We now consider the current level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
For convenience, for all
0<L^{\prime}\leq L-1
, we denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
the collection of all clusters
C
that ever belonged to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
over the course of the current level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Notice that, once cluster
C
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, it remains in
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
until the end of the current level-
L^{\prime}
phase, so in particular, it remains in
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
until the end of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. We also denote
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}=\bigcup_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
.

As mentioned already, the level-
L
data structure maintains a graph
H^{L}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. The definition of the graph depends on the flattened sets
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices, for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
. As mentioned already, the sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices are not maintained explicitly, since this may be too costly, as their cardinalities may be quite large. However, in order to maintain graph
H^{L}
, we only need to maintain, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, the set
V^{F}(C)\cap S^{L}_{k}
of vertices, whose cardinality is significantly smaller. For convenience, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, we denote by
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=V^{F}(C)\cap S^{L}_{k}
. The set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices is only maintained over the course of the current level-
L
phase, by our level-
L
data structure. Once the level-
L
phase terminates, we recompute all such sets of vertices from scratch. Observe however that vertex sets
\left\{Z^{L}_{k}(C)\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}}
are completely determined by the data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
, and by the set
S^{L}_{k}
of vertices, which is fixed throughout Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. In particular, the definitions of the sets
\left\{Z^{L}_{k}(C)\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}}
of vertices do not depend on the data structures that we will maintain at level
L
.

The following properties of the sets
\left\{Z^{L}_{k}(C)\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}}
of vertices follow immediately from Properties
P1
–
P3

P’1.

For a cluster
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, vertex set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\subseteq V(G)
is initialized when cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
or at the start of phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
– whatever happens later, and after that, over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, vertices may leave
V^{F}(C)
but not join it;

P’2.

For every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, there are at most
(4\Delta)^{L+1}\leq\mu_{L}
clusters
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, such that
x
ever belongs to
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
; and

P’3.

If a new cluster
C
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
at some time
\tau
during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
because of a cluster splitting operation applied to a cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, then, at time
\tau
,
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\subseteq Z_{k}^{L}(C^{\prime})
holds.

The data structure at level
L
consists of three components. The first component, that we refer to as
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
, maintains the sets
\left\{Z^{L}_{k}(C)\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}}
of vertices. The second data structure, that we refer to as
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
, maintains the level-
L
graph
H^{L}
, and the corresponding neighborhood cover data structures, that include the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
of level-
L
clusters. The third data structure, that we refer to as
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
maintains, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
and supernode
u\in V(C)
, an
importance status
of the supernode: whether the supernode is marked as important for cluster
C
. The importance status of the supernodes will in turn be used in order to define the flattened sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
will be exploited by data structures
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
from higher levels
L^{\prime\prime}>L
, in order to maintain the sets
Z^{L^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L^{\prime\prime}}
. We start by providing a high-level definition of each of these three data structures, and state the invariants that the data structures maintain. We then provide a more detailed description of the implementation of each of the data structures.

Data Structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
.

Intuitively, the purpose of the
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
data structure is to maintain the sets
\left\{Z^{L}_{k}(C)\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}}
of vertices over the course of level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. However, the number of clusters in set
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
may be prohibitively large, and for many of these clusters,
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=\emptyset
may hold. Instead, the data structure will maintain a set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
of clusters, and it will only explicitly maintain the sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
. The data structure ensures that the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters has the following properties:

F1.

the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters is initialized at the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, and, over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, clusters may join it but they may not leave it;

F2.

at the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
,
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
holds, and whenever a new cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
,
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
holds at that time;

F3.

throughout Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, if a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
does not lie in
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, then
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=\emptyset
holds; and

F4.

if
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
is a time that is not the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, then a cluster
C
may be added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
at time
\tau
only if cluster
C
was added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
at time
\tau
, due to a cluster splitting operation that was applied to some cluster
C^{\prime}
, and
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
at time
\tau
.

We note that, if a new cluster
C
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
at some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
due to a cluster splitting operation that was applied to cluster
C^{\prime}
, and
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
, then, from Property
P’3
,
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
holds at time
\tau
, and so
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
must hold.

We ensure that the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters that data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
maintains over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
obeys the above invariants. For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, the data structure also maintains the corresponding set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices. We denote by
\mathsf{T_{FS}}
the total update time that data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
requires over the course of a single phase. We emphasize that data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
only depends on data structures maintained at levels
0,\ldots,L-1
, and the set
S^{L}_{k}
of vertices that is fixed throughout Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, so it does not depend on any other data structures maintained at level
L
.

Data Structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
.

Data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
is responsible for maintaining the level-
L
a bipartite graph
H^{L}=(V^{L},U^{L},\tilde{E}^{L})
, over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. The graph is initialized at the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, and after that it undergoes a sequence of online valid update operations, that is computed by data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
, based on the sequence
\Sigma
of updates that graph
G
undergoes, changes to the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters, and the sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
that data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
maintains. For all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, we also maintain a graph
H^{L}_{i}
, that is obtained from
H^{L}
by deleting from it all edges whose length is greater than
D_{i}
. We then obtain a corresponding valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{L}_{i}
, that undergoes a sequence of valid update operations. We apply the algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem from
1.1
to this input structure, and parameter
\hat{W}=\max\left\{4m\cdot\mu_{L},\hat{m}\right\}
, and obtain a set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
of clusters that the algorithm maintains over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. We then denote
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\log\hat{D}}{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
. We ensure that every regular vertex of
H^{L}
may belong to at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
We now formally define dynamic graph
H^{L}
.

Definition 4.4
(Graph
H^{L}
).

Dynamic graph
H^{L}=(V^{L},U^{L},\tilde{E}^{L})
is defined as follows.

•

The set
V^{L}
of regular vertices of graph
H^{L}
is the union of two subsets: the set of type-1 regular vertices
\left\{v^{L}(x)\mid x\in S^{L}_{k}\right\}
, and the set of type-2 regular vertices
\left\{v^{L}(e)\mid e\in A^{L}_{k}\right\}
.

•

The set
U^{L}
of supernodes of graph
H^{L}
is the union of two subsets – the set of type-1 supernodes
\left\{u^{L}(x)\mid x\in S^{L}_{k}\right\}
, and the set of type-2 supernodes
\left\{u^{L}(C)\mid C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\right\}
.

•

The edges of graph
H^{L}
are defined as follows. Let
e=(x,y)\in A^{L}_{k}
be a an edge. If edge
e
was already deleted from
G
, then vertex
v^{L}(e)
is isolated in graph
H^{L}
. Otherwise, it is connected to supernodes
u^{L}(x)
and
u^{L}(y)
with edges of length
\ell(e)
. Consider now a vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
. We add an edge
(v^{L}(x),u^{L}(x))
to graph
H^{L}
of length
1
. Additionally, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
with
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, we add an edge
(v^{L}(x),u^{L}(C))
, whose length is
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C)}
.

Note that, from the above definition, the set
V^{L}
of regular vertices, and the set of type-1 supernodes remains unchanged over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

Notice also that, for every type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C)
, all edges that are incident to the supernode have the same length –
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C)}
, and all neighbors of
u^{L}(C)
are type-1 regular vertices, that represent the vertices of
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
. We denote by
N(u^{L}(C))
the collection of all vertices of
H^{L}
that are neighbors of supernode
u^{L}(C)
. Since the set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices is decremental, once supernode
u^{L}(C)
is added to graph
H^{L}
, the set
N(u^{L}(C))
of its neighbors is also decremental.

We show below an algorithm that maintains the dynamic graph
H^{L}
. After the graph
H^{L}
is initialized at the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, it will only undergo a sequence of valid update operations. We show that the resulting dynamic graph is consistent with the definition of graph
H^{L}
listed above. We need one more detail regarding graph
H^{L}
that will be useful for us. As mentioned already, once we initialize graph
H^{L}
, it will only undergo valid update operations: edge-deletion, isolated vertex deletion, and supernode-splitting. The supernode splitting operation will only be applied to type-2 supernodes, and it will mirror cluster splitting from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
. In other words, supernode
u^{L}(C)
may only undergo a supernode splitting operation at time
\tau
, if the corresponding cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
underwent a cluster-splitting operation at time
\tau
. If
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
is the new resulting cluster, then the new supernode that is obtained by splitting
u^{L}(C)
is
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
– that is, it represents cluster
C^{\prime}
. It is then easy to verify that, for every type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C)
that ever lies in graph
H^{L}
, for every time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
, if we denote
\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau)}(u^{L}(C))
by
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
, then
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau)}(C)
(if
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}
, then the ancestor-cluster of
C
is defined with respect to
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}
). In particular, both
C
and
C^{\prime}
must have the same scale
i
.

For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, if supernode
u^{L}(C)
belongs to graph
H^{L}
, and it is not an isolated vertex of
H^{L}
, then we will designate some vertex
x\in V(G)
to be the
representative
vertex of cluster
C
at level
L
, and denote
\sigma_{L}(C)=x
. Vertex
x
may only be a representative of cluster
C
only if
x\in V^{F}(C)
, and
v^{L}(x)
is a vertex of
H^{L}
. Note that, if
x
is a representative of
u^{L}(C)
, then edge
(v^{L}(x),u^{L}(C))
belongs to graph
H^{L}
. For convenience, we summarize these requirements in the following rules.

B1.

for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, if
u^{L}(C)
is a vertex of
H^{L}
, and it is not an isolated vertex, then a
level-
L
representative

\sigma_{L}(C)\in V(G)
of cluster
C
must be maintained by the data structure.

B2.

if
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, and
x=\sigma^{L}(C)
, then
x\in V^{F}(C)
and
v^{L}(x)\in V(H^{L})
must hold.

Generally, we will let
\sigma_{L}(C)
be any vertex that satisfies these properties. If
x=\sigma_{L}(C)
, and, after some time, vertex
x
is deleted from set
V^{F}(C)
, then we select any other vertex
y\in V^{F}(C)
with
v^{L}(y)\in V(H^{L})
to be a new representative of cluster
C
at level
L
.

Data Structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
.

The purpose of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
is to maintain, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, for every type-2 supernode
u\in V(C)
, the
importance status
of supernode
u
. In other words, supernode
u
is marked as either important or unimportant for cluster
C
. If a type-2 supernode
u
does not belong to cluster
C
, then we will say that
u
is
unimportant
for cluster
C
. However, data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
only maintains explicitly the importance status of a type-2 supernode
u
for a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
if
u\in V(C)
. In addition to maintaining the importance status of type-2 supernodes for clusters, the data structure maintains, for every type-2 supernode
u\in V(H^{L})
, the list of all clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, for which
u
is marked as important supernode, together with pointers to these clusters.

The definition of important supernodes completely determines the definition of the flattened sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
. For a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, the set
V^{F}(C)
of vertices is the union of two subsets. The first subset is
\left\{x\in S^{L}_{k}\mid v^{L}(x)\in V(C)\right\}
. The second subset contains, for every type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
that is marked as important for cluster
C
, all vertices of
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
(recall that
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
must hold, so set
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
is already defined).

On the one hand, we would like to mark supernodes of clusters as important quite generously, in order to ensure that we can support short-path queries. For example, marking all type-2 supernodes of every cluster
C
as important for
C
would accomplish this. But on the other hand, we need to limit the sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices, in order to ensure that the cardinalities of the sets
Z^{L^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}(C)
of vertices, for levels
L^{\prime\prime}>L
are not too large, as we need to maintain them explicitly.

A reasonable compromise between these two goals is the following: we would like to ensure that a type-2 supernode
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as important for cluster
C
if and only if all these conditions hold: (i)
u\in V(C)
; (ii)
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{scale}(C)
; and (iii)
N(u)\subseteq V(C)
. Defining the importance status of supernodes
u
in this way would indeed allow us to accomplish the goals stated above, but it would introduce a significant problem: the status of a supernode
u
for a cluster
C
may flip from being unimportant to being important. We cannot allow this to happen, since this may cause vertices to join the set
V^{F}(C)
, which is unacceptable (see Condition
P1
, which is crucial in order to ensure that no edges are inserted into graph
H^{L}
except via supernode splitting operations). Therefore, we modify the above definition to ensure that the status of a supernode may never switch from unimportant to important.

For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, for every type-2 supernode
u=u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
, data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
will maintain the
importance status
of supernode
u
, that is, the supernode will be marked as either being
important
or
unimportant
for
C
. This marking will obey the following rule.

R1.

Consider some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
, a cluster
C
that belongs to
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
at time
\tau
, and a type-2 supernode
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})
that belongs to
V(C)
at time
\tau
(so
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
holds). Supernode
u
is marked as
important
for
C
at time
\tau
if and only if all of the following hold:

•

\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{scale}(C)
;

•

at time
\tau
,
N(u)\neq\emptyset
and
N(u)\subseteq V(C)
hold;
and

•

if
\tau
is not the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, then for every time
\tau^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
with
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
, if we denote
\tilde{C}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
, and
\tilde{u}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u)
, then supernode
\tilde{u}
was marked as important for cluster
\tilde{C}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
.

It is easy to see that the following rule is equivalent to Rule
R1
.

R’1.

Consider some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
, a cluster
C
that belongs to
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
at time
\tau
, and a type-2 supernode
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})
that belongs to
V(C)
at time
\tau
. Supernode
u
is marked as
important
for
C
at time
\tau
if and only if
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{scale}(C)
, and for every time
\tau^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
with
\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau
, if we denote
\tilde{C}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
and
\tilde{u}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u)
, then, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
N(\tilde{u})\neq\emptyset
and
N(\tilde{u})\subseteq V(\tilde{C})
held.

The equivalence easily follows since, if we denote by
\tilde{C}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
and
u^{L}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{L}(C^{\prime}))
, then
\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C})=\operatorname{scale}(C)
, and
\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})=\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})
.
Additionally, from
3.6
,
u^{L}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})\in V(\tilde{C})
holds at time
\tau^{\prime}
.
We will use the two rules interchangeably, as in some proofs one of them is more convenient to use than the other.

We now proceed to describe algorithms that maintain level-
L
data structures in more detail. We note that, while data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
only depends on data structures maintained at levels
0,\ldots,L-1
, it exploits data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
maintained at those levels. Because of this, we describe the data structures in a somewhat different order. First, we assume that data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
with properties that are defined above exists, and describe data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
that relies on it. Next, we describe data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
, that uses data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
. Only then we describe data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
, that relies on data structures
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
from lower levels.

4.2.1
Data Structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}

We fix a level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. We assume that data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
are now defined, and we assume that there exists data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
, that, over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, maintains a collection
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
of clusters, for which Properties
F1
–
F4
hold. The data structure additionally maintains the sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices, for all clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
.
From Property
P’1
, once cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, vertices may leave set
Z^{L}_{k}
, but they may not join it.
Recall that we have denoted by
\mathsf{T_{FS}}
the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

At the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, we are given a set
A^{L}_{k}
of edges of
G
, with
|A^{L}_{k}|\leq M^{q-L+1}
, and the set
S^{L}_{k}
of vertices of
G
, that serve as endpoints of the edges of
A^{L}_{k}
. Both sets remain unchanged over the course of the phase. At the beginning of the phase, data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
produces the initial set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters that contains, from Property
F3
, all clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
with
Z^{L}_{k}\neq\emptyset
, and only such clusters. For each such cluster, the data structure also produces the initial set
Z^{L}_{k}\subseteq V(G)
of vertices. It is now immediate to initialize graph
H^{L}
, so that it matches Definition
4.4
. From Property
P’2
, for every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, there are at most
\mu_{L}=(4\Delta)^{L+1}
clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
with
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, so the degree of every regular vertex in
H^{L}
is at most
\mu_{L}
.
For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, we let
\sigma_{L}(C)\in V(G)
be an arbitrary vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, that becomes the representative of
C
at level
L
.
Clearly, graph
H^{L}
can be initialized in time
O(|A^{L}_{k}|\cdot\mu_{L})\leq O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\mu_{L})\leq O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)})
, since
\mu_{L}\leq\mu^{\prime}\leq\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}
from Inequality
6
.

Next, we describe an algorithm that correctly maintains graph
H^{L}
, by only applying valid update operations to it. Updates to graph
H^{L}
are triggered by the arrival of new updates from sequence
\Sigma
to graph
G
. Consider any such update
\sigma_{\tau}
, which must be either the deletion of an edge from
G
, or the insertion of an edge into
G
.

If an edge
e
is inserted into
G
, then we simply ignore this update. Assume now that an edge
e
is deleted from
G
. If edge
e
was inserted into
G
during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, then we ignore this update. Otherwise, if
e\in A^{L}_{k}
, then we delete the two edges that are incident to vertex
v^{L}(e)
from graph
H^{L}
. No further updates to
H^{L}
or data structures from lower levels are required. Otherwise, we update data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
, and data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
, with the deletion of the edge
e
.
As the result of this update, the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters may change, and additionally, for some clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, the set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices may change. Each of such changes may require updating graph
H^{L}
.

Assume first that, for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, set
Z^{L}_{k}
of vertices has changed. From Property
P’1
, vertices may leave set
Z^{L}_{k}
, but they may not join it. Therefore, the change must be the deletion of some vertices from
Z^{L}_{k}
. For each such vertex
x
, we delete the edge
(v^{L}(x),u^{L}(C))
from graph
H^{L}
.
If vertex
x
is the representative vertex of cluster
C
at level
L
, then, if vertex
u^{L}(C)
is not an isolated vertex in
H^{L}
, there must be another vertex
y\in V(G)
with
v^{L}(y)\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
. We let any such vertex
y
become the new representative vertex for
C
at level
L
.

Assume now that a new cluster
C
just jointed the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
. From Property
F4
, this can only happen if cluster
C
was just added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, due to a cluster splitting operation that was applied to some cluster
C^{\prime}
, that currently belongs to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
.
Notice that, if
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}
holds for some
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
and
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, then
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}
must also hold, since the cluster splitting operation was executed by data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}
. So in particular,
\operatorname{scale}(C)=\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})
. From Property
P’3
,
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\subseteq Z_{k}^{L}(C^{\prime})
currently holds. Therefore, for every vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, edge
(v^{L}(x),u^{L}(C^{\prime}))
currently lies in graph
H^{L}
. We let
E^{\prime}=\left\{(v^{L}(x),u^{L}(C^{\prime}))\mid x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)\right\}
be the collection of all such edges. We apply the supernode splitting operation to supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
, with the set
E^{\prime}
of edges, to obtain a new supernode
u^{L}(C)
, that represents the new cluster
C
. Note that, from Property
F2
,
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
must hold, so
E^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
as well. It is easy to verify that, at the end of this supernode splitting operation, the new supernode
u^{L}(C)
is connected with an edge of length
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C)}=2^{\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})}
to every regular vertex
v^{L}(x)
with
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, and only to such vertices. We select any such vertex
x\in V^{L}_{k}(C)
to be the representative vertex of cluster
C
for level
L
, setting
\sigma_{L}(C)=x
.

This completes the algorithm for maintaining graph
H^{L}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. The running time required for updating graph
H^{L}_{k}
is asymptotically bounded by the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
. Therefore, the running time of the algorithm so far is bounded by
O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(1)}+\mathsf{T_{FS}})
.
Notice that, from Property
P’2
, for every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, there are at most
\mu_{L}
clusters
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, such that
x
ever belongs to
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. It is then easy to verify that the dynamic degree bound of graph
H^{L}
is bounded by
\mu_{L}
. It is also easy to see that the data structure ensures Properties
B1
and
B2
for representative vertices of clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
at level
L
.

For all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, we also obtain dynamic graph
H^{L}_{i}
, that is identical to
H^{L}
, except that it excludes all edges whose length is greater than
D_{i}
. Once graph
H^{L}
is initialized, it is easy to initialize each such graph
H^{L}_{i}
. When an update operation is applied to graph
H^{L}
, then we apply the same update operation to graph
H^{L}_{i}
, except that we ignore edges whose length is greater than
D_{i}
. In particular, supernodes
u^{L}(C)
with
\operatorname{scale}(C)>i
are never split, as all their incident edges have length greater than
D_{i}
. The running time that is required in order to maintain all graphs
H^{L}_{i}
, for
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
is bounded by
O\left((M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+\mathsf{T_{FS}})\cdot\log\hat{D}\right)\leq O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+\mathsf{T_{FS}}\cdot\log\hat{D}\right)
, since
\log\hat{D}\leq\hat{m}^{\epsilon^{12}}
from Inequality
5
.

Consider now some scale
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
. Notice that the number of regular vertices in graph
H^{L}_{i}
is
|A^{L}_{k}|+|S^{L}_{k}|\leq 3M^{q-L+1}<m
.

We have now defined a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{L}_{i}=\left(H^{L}_{i},\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H^{L}_{i})},D_{i}\right)
, that undergoes a sequence of valid update operations. We view it as an input to the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, with distance parameter
D_{i}
and parameter
\hat{W}=\max\left\{4m\mu_{L},\hat{m}\right\}\leq\hat{m}^{2}
. We apply Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem from
1.1

to this instance of
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
. We denote the corresponding data structure by
\mathsf{DS}^{L}_{i}
, and we denote by
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
the collection of clusters (the weak neighborhood cover) that the algorithm maintains. For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
, we say that the
scale
of
C
is
i
, and we denote
\operatorname{scale}(C)=i
. Recall that the algorithm achieves approximation factor
\alpha(\hat{W})\leq\alpha(n^{3})=\alpha
.
Since the number of regular vertices in
H^{L}_{i}
is at most
3M^{q-L+1}
, and the dynamic degree bound of graph
H^{L}_{i}
is
\mu_{L}\leq\mu^{\prime}\leq\hat{m}^{64\epsilon^{2}}
, from

1.1
, the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}_{i}
is bounded by:

O\left(M^{(q-L+1)(1+O(\epsilon))}\cdot(\mu_{L})^{O(1/\epsilon)}\right)\leq O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\right).

Recall that algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
also guarantees that every regular vertex of
H^{L}_{i}
may belong to at most
\hat{W}^{4\epsilon^{3}}\leq\hat{m}^{8\epsilon^{3}}
different clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

Lastly, we denote
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\log\hat{D}}{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
. From the above discussion, every regular vertex of
H^{L}
may belong to at most
2\hat{m}^{8\epsilon^{3}}\log\hat{D}\leq\hat{m}^{16\epsilon^{3}}=\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
(we have used the fact that
\log\hat{D}\leq\hat{m}^{\epsilon^{12}}
from Inequality
5
.).

This completes the description of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
. We denote by
\mathsf{T_{basic}}
that total update time of this data structure over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. From our discussion:

\mathsf{T_{basic}}\leq O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+\mathsf{T_{FS}}\cdot\log\hat{D}\right)\leq O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+\mathsf{T_{FS}}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{3})}\right).

For convenience, for all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, for every regular vertex
v\in V(H^{L})
, we denote the cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
that data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}_{i}
maintains by
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i}(v)
. Similarly, we denote the list
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}(v)
of all clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
containing
v
that data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}_{i}
maintains by
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{L}_{i}(v)
. We then let
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{L}(v)=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\log\hat{D}}\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{L}_{i}(v)
. Lastly, for all
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
, if
C
is a cluster that currently lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
, and
v,v^{\prime}
are two regular vertices of
H^{L}
that currently lie in
C
, we denote by
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}^{L}_{i}(C,v,v^{\prime})
a query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,v,v^{\prime})
to data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}_{i}
. Recall that the data structure must return a path
P\subseteq H^{L}
of length at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i}
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
H^{L}_{i}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. For convenience, we will think of
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}^{L}_{i}(C,v,v^{\prime})
as a query that is supported by data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}
.

4.2.2
Data Structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}

In this subsection we describe data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
. Recall that, over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, the data structure must maintain, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, for every type-2 supernode
u\in V(C)
, the importance status of supernode
u
, which is a single bit indicating whether supernode
u
is important for cluster
C
. For brevity, if a type-2 supernode
u
does not belong to cluster
C
, then we say that it is unimportant for
C
, though there is no need to maintain the importance status in such a case explicitly. The importance status of every type-2 supernode in a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
is completely determined by Rule
R1
(or its equivalent rule
R’1
). Our goal is to implement a data structure that maintains the importance status of every type-2 supernode in every cluster of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
efficiently. In addition to maintaining the importance status of supernodes in clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, we maintain, for every type-2 supernode
u\in V(H^{L})
, an ordered list of all clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, such that
u
is important for
C
, together with pointers to each such cluster. We now describe initialization of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
, and the algorithm for updating it.

Initialization.

Consider the initial collection
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
of clusters that is constructed at the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
According to Rule
R1
,
for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and every type-2 supernode
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
, supernode
u
is important for cluster
C
if and only if the following three conditions hold: (i)
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{scale}(C)
; (ii)
N(u)\neq\emptyset
; and (iii)
N(u)\subseteq V(C)
. Here,
N(u)
is the set of all vertices that are neighbors of
u
in graph
H^{L}
. The most natural way to implement the initialization is then to consider every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and every type-2 supernode
u\in V(C)
one by one, and then establish whether
u
is important for cluster
C
. Unfortunately, this algorithm may be inefficient, since, unlike regular vertices, supernodes may belong to a large number of clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and spending
N(u)
time on each such occurrence of a supernode in a cluster may be too expensive.

In order to initialize the data structure efficiently, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
and type-2 supernode
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
, we create a counter
\rho(C,C^{\prime})
. The counter will count the number of vertices of
N(u)
that lie in
V(C)
. Initially, we set the counter
\rho(C,C^{\prime})
to
0
.

Next, we consider every type-1 regular vertex
v^{L}(x)\in V^{L}
one by one. Recall that data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
ensures that vertex
v^{L}(x)
may belong to at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and it may be a neighbor of at most
\mu_{L}
supernodes of
H^{L}
. When vertex
v^{L}(x)\in V^{L}
is processed, we consider every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
with
v^{L}(x)\in V(C)
. For each such cluster, we consider every type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
that is a neighbor of
v^{L}(x)
. If
u^{L}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
, then we increase the counter
\rho(C,C^{\prime})
by
1
.

From the above discussion, the time required to process a single regular type-1 vertex of
V^{L}
is
O(\mu_{L}\cdot\Delta)
, and the time to process all type-1 vertices of
V^{L}
is bounded by
O(|A^{L}_{k}|\cdot\mu_{L}\cdot\Delta)\leq O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\mu_{L}\cdot\Delta)
.

Once all type-1 regular vertices of
H^{L}
are processed, we consider every type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})\in V(H^{L})
, and we compute
|N(u)|
– the number of neighbors of
u
in
H^{L}
. This calculation can be performed for all supernodes of
H^{L}
in time
O(|E(H^{L})|)\leq O(|A^{L}_{k}|\cdot\mu_{L})\leq O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\mu_{L})
.

Lastly, we consider every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and every type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
. If
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{scale}(C)
,
N(u^{L}(C^{\prime}))\neq\emptyset
, and
\rho(C,C^{\prime})=|N(u^{L}(C^{\prime}))|
, then we mark supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
as important for
C
. We then add cluster
C
to the list of clusters that supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
maintains, together with a pointer to cluster
C
. Otherwise, we mark
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
it as unimportant for
C
.
The time that is required in order to perform this last step is bounded by
O\left(\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}}|V(C)|\right)
, which, in turn, is bounded by the time that is required in order to initialize the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
of clusters, that is bounded by
O(\mathsf{T_{basic}})
.

Overall, the running time of the algorithm for initializing the data structure is bounded by:

O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\mu_{L}\cdot\Delta+\mathsf{T_{basic}})\leq O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}+\mathsf{T_{basic}})

Updates.

We now provide an algorithm that maintains, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, the importance status of every type-2 supernode
u\in V(C)
, over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, so that Rule
R1
is obeyed.
The importance status of a supernode may change either due to the valid update operations that graph
H^{L}
undergoes (that includes an insertion of a new type-2 supernode into a cluster
C
due to the supernode-splitting operation), or due to the allowed updates that clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
undergo. We now consider each of these possible changes in turn, starting with the valid update operations that are applied to graph
H^{L}
.

First, if an isolated vertex is deleted from graph
H^{L}
, then no further updates to data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
are necessary. Assume now that an edge
(u,v)
is deleted from graph
H^{L}
, where
u
is a supernode. If
u
becomes an isolated vertex in
H^{L}
, then we consider every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
for which
u
is marked as an important supernode (using the list maintained by vertex
u
), and we mark
u
as an unimportant supernode for each such cluster, setting the list of clusters maintained by vertex
u
to
\emptyset
.

Assume now that graph
H^{L}
undergoes a supernode-splitting operation, applied to some supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
, that creates a new supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
. We consider every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, to which the new supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
was added. From the definition of allowed changes to the clusters in the definition of the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, it must be the case that
u^{L}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
holds. If
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as unimportant for
C
, then we mark
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
as unimportant for
C
as well. Assume now that
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as important for
C
. In this case, from Rule
R1
, every vertex in
N(u^{L}(C^{\prime}))
lies in
C
. From the definition of the supernode-splitting operation,
N(u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime}))\neq\emptyset
and
N(u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime}))\subseteq N(u^{L}(C^{\prime}))
must hold. Furthermore, since such a supernode splitting operation may only occur if cluster
C^{\prime}
just underwent a cluster-splitting operation that created a new cluster
C^{\prime\prime}
, it must be the case that
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime\prime})=\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{scale}(C)
. Therefore, if
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as important for
C
, we mark
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
as important for
C
as well, and add cluster
C
to the list of clusters that supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
maintains.

As graph
H^{L}
undergoes valid update operations, the collection
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
of clusters also undergoes a sequence of allowed changes. We have already discussed how the insertion of a new supernode into a cluster is handled (recall that a new supernode
u^{\prime}
may only be inserted into a cluster
C
if
u^{\prime}
was just created via a supernode-splitting operation applied another supernode
u\in V(C)
). It remains to consider the
\operatorname{DeleteVertex}
and
\operatorname{ClusterSplit}
operations.

Consider first a
\operatorname{DeleteVertex}
update, when some vertex
z
is deleted from some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. If the vertex is a supernode, and it is marked as important for
C
, then we delete
C
from the list of clusters maintained by supernode
z
. Assume now that
z
is a regular vertex. We only need to update the data structure if it is a type-1 regular vertex, that is,
z=v^{L}(x)
for some vertex
x\in V(G)
. In this case, we consider every supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
that is a neighbor of
z
in
H^{L}
one by one. For each such supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
, if it is currently marked as important for
C
, we mark it as unimportant for
C
. Note that, since the degree of
v^{L}(x)
is bounded by
\mu_{L}
, the time required to update the data structure due to the deletion of
v^{L}(x)
from
C
is bounded by
O(\mu_{L})
.

Lastly, consider a
\operatorname{ClusterSplit}
update, where a new cluster
C^{\prime}
is created, by splitting a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
. We consider every supernode
u\in V(C^{\prime})
one by one. If
u
is marked as unimportant for
C
, then we mark it as unimportant for
C^{\prime}
. Otherwise, we check whether every neighbor vertex of
u
in
H^{L}
also lies in
V(C^{\prime})
. This can be done in time
O(|\delta_{C^{\prime}}(u)|)
. If this is the case, we mark
u
as important for
C^{\prime}
, and otherwise we mark it as unimportant for
C^{\prime}
. Note that the total time required to process this update is bounded by
O(|E(C^{\prime})|)
.

This concludes our algorithm for maintaining the importance status of the supernodes in the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
. It is immediate to verify that the resulting data structure obeys Rule
R1
.

Recall that the total time required to initialize data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
is bounded by
O(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}+\mathsf{T_{basic}})
.
Since data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
needs to maintain both the graph
H^{L}
and all clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
explicitly,
from the above discussion, it is easy to verify that the additional time that is required in order to maintain the importance status of the supernodes in each cluster of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
is bounded by
O(\mathsf{T_{basic}}\cdot\mu_{L})\leq O(\mathsf{T_{basic}}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})})
. Overall, the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
over the course of a single level-
L
phase is bounded by:

O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}+\mathsf{T_{basic}}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right).

We will need the following observation regarding the importance status of supernodes.

Observation 4.5
.

Let
u
be a type-2 supernode that belonged to graph
H^{L}
at some time during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Then the total number of clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, such that
u
was ever marked as an important supernode for
C
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, is at most
\Delta
.

Proof.

Let
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})
be a type-2 supernode that belonged to graph
H^{L}
at some time during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Assume that
u
was added to graph
H^{L}
at time
\tau
. If
\tau
is not the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, then node
u
was added to
H^{L}
due to the supernode-splitting operation, and, from the definition of the operation,
N(u)\neq\emptyset
holds at time
\tau
. Otherwise, from the definition of the initial graph
H^{L}
, it is easy to verify that
N(u)\neq\emptyset
must hold at time
\tau
as well. Note that, from the definition of valid update operations, the set
N(u)
of the neighbors of vertex
u
in
H^{L}
is decremental.

Among all vertices that lie in
N(u)
at time
\tau
, we choose a single vertex
v^{L}(x)
, as follows. If, at the end of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
,
N(u)\neq\emptyset
holds, then we let
v^{L}(x)
be any vertex lying in set
N(u)
at the end of the phase. Otherwise, we let
v^{L}(x)
be the vertex of
H^{L}
, that belonged to set
N(u)
at time
\tau
, and left this set last.

Consider now some cluster
C
, such that, at some time
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau
during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, supernode
u
was marked as an important supernode for
C
. Then at time
\tau^{\prime}
, it must be the case that
N(u)\subseteq V(C)
, and, in particular,
v^{L}(x)\in V(C)
. Since every regular vertex of
H^{L}
may belong to at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, we conclude that supernode
u
may be marked as an important supernode for at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

We can now define the flattened sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
. Consider a cluster
C
that belonged to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
at some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{L}_{k}
. Then, at time
\tau
, the flattened set
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V(G)
of vertices is the union of two subsets. The first subset is
\left\{x\in V(G)\mid v^{L}(x)\in V(C)\right\}
, and the second subset is the union of sets
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
for clusters
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, for which supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
lies in graph
H^{L}
, and is marked as an important supernode for
C
at time
\tau
:

V^{F}(C)=\left\{x\in V(G)\mid v^{L}(x)\in V(C)\right\}\cup\left(\bigcup_{u^{L}(C^{\prime})\mbox{ important for }C}V^{F}(C^{\prime})\right).

In the next subsection, we analyze the flattened sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and in particular we show that Properties
P1
–
P3
hold for them.

4.2.3
Flattened Vertex Sets – Analysis

We start by establishing that Property
P3
holds for the flattened sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
.

Observation 4.6
.

If a new cluster
C
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
at some time
\tau
during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, due to a cluster splitting operation that is applied to a cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
with
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
, then, at time
\tau
,
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V^{F}(C^{\prime})
holds.

Proof.

Since, at time
\tau
,
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
holds, every type-1 regular vertex
v^{L}(x)
that belongs to
V(C)
must also lie in
V(C^{\prime})
. Furthermore, from Rule
R1
, a supernode
u\in V(C^{\prime})
may only be marked as important for
C
at time
\tau
, if
u
is marked as important for
C^{\prime}
at time
\tau
. From the definition of of the flattened set of vertices, it is now immediate that
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V^{F}(C^{\prime})
holds at time
\tau
.

Next, we establish Property
P1
in the following observation.

Observation 4.7
.

For every cluster
C
that lied in
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, the set
V^{F}(C)
of vertices is decremental. In other words, after
V^{F}(C)
is initialized (when cluster
C
joins
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
), vertices may leave
V^{F}(C)
but not join it.

Proof.

Consider a cluster
C
that was added to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
at some time
\tau
during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Recall that:

V^{F}(C)=\left\{x\in V(G)\mid v^{L}(x)\in V(C)\right\}\cup\left(\bigcup_{u^{L}(C^{\prime})\mbox{ important for }C}V^{F}(C^{\prime})\right).

Consider first the collection
\left\{x\in V(G)\mid v^{L}(x)\in V(C)\right\}
of vertices of
G
. Once cluster
C
is created, it may only undergo allowed updates. From the definition of the allowed updates, no new regular vertices may join
C
. Therefore, vertices may leave set
\left\{x\in V(G)\mid v^{L}(x)\in V(C)\right\}
, but they may not join it.

Consider now some type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
. Recall that
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
must hold, and, from our assumption, Property
P1
holds for all levels
L^{\prime}<L
. Since level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
is contained in some level-
L^{\prime}
phase, we conclude that the set
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
of vertices is decremental over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

Assume first that supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
belonged to cluster
C
at time
\tau
when cluster
C
was created. Note that, from Rule
R1
, the importance status of
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
for cluster
C
may switch from important to unimportant, but not in the other direction.

Therefore, from the above discussion, the only way that new vertices may join set
V^{F}(C)
is when a new supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is inserted into cluster
C
, that is marked as important for
C
, and so the vertices of
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
are added to set
V^{F}(C)
. Recall that a new supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
may only be added to cluster
C
if some type-2 supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
, that currently lies in cluster
C
, just underwent supernode-splitting operation, and supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
was created as the result of this operation. From Rule
R1
, supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
may only be marked as important for
C
, if supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
is currently marked as such. We now show that
V^{F}(C^{\prime})\subseteq V^{F}(C^{\prime\prime})
holds, and so the vertices of
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
already lie in set
V^{F}(C)
.

Indeed, let
L^{\prime}<L
be the level for which
C^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
. Recall that supernode-splitting operations at level
L
mirror cluster-splitting operations at lower levels. In other words, a supernode-splitting operation that creates a new supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
may only be applied to supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime\prime})
in graph
H^{L}
, if cluster
C^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
just underwent a cluster-splitting operation, that resulted in the creation of a new cluster
C^{\prime}\subseteq C^{\prime\prime}
. However, in this case, since we have assumed that Property
P3
holds for all levels below
L
, we get that, when cluster
C^{\prime}
was created,
V^{F}(C^{\prime})\subseteq V^{F}(C^{\prime\prime})
held. Therefore, no new vertices may be added to set
V^{F}(C)
due to the supernode-splitting operation.

Lastly, we establish Property
P2
.

Observation 4.8
.

For every vertex
x\in V(G)
, there are at most
(4\Delta)^{L+1}
clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, such that
x
ever belonged to set
V^{F}(C)
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

Proof.

Consider a level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, and some vertex
x\in V(G)
.

For convenience, for all
0\leq L^{\prime}\leq L
, we denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
the collection that contains all clusters
C
that ever belonged to
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
over the course of the level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Recall that, for all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, current level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k^{\prime}}
is contained in the current level-
L^{\prime}
phase.

Recall first data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
ensures that every regular vertex may belong to at most
\Delta
clusters in
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Therefore, the number of clusters
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, for which
v^{L}(x)\in V(C)
ever held over the course of the phase is at most
\Delta
.

Consider now some level
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
.
Since we have assumed that Property
P2
holds for all levels below
L
, there are at most
(4\Delta)^{L^{\prime}+1}
clusters
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, such that
x\in V^{F}(C)
ever held over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Let
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)
denote the set of all clusters
C\in\bigcup_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, such that
x\in V^{F}(C)
held at any time during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. From the above discussion,
|{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)|\leq\sum_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}(4\Delta)^{L^{\prime}+1}\leq 2\cdot(4\Delta)^{L}
.
From
4.5
, every supernode of
H^{L}
may be marked as important for at most
\Delta
clusters over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Therefore, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)
, there are at most
\Delta
clusters
C^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, for which
u^{L}(C)
was ever marked as important for
C^{\prime}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. We conclude that the number of clusters
C^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, for which
x\in V^{F}(C)
ever held over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
is bounded by:

\Delta+|{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)|\cdot\Delta\leq\Delta+2\cdot(4\Delta)^{L}\cdot\Delta\leq(4\Delta)^{L+1}.

To summarize, we have now established that Properties
P1
–
P3
hold for level
L
and the sets
\left\{V^{F}(C)\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}}
of vertices.

4.2.4
Data Structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}

In this subsection we describe data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
, that is maintained as part of the level-
L
data structure, over the course of a single level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. The purpose of the data structure is to efficiently maintain the sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=V^{F}(C)\cap S^{L}_{k}
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
. This data structure only depends on data structures maintained at levels
0,\ldots,(L-1)
, and is independent of other level-
L
data structures, except that it uses the set
S^{L}_{k}
of vertices (that is computed at the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, and does not change over the course of the phase).

We now fix a level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. For convenience, we denote by
\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}
the total update time of data structures from levels
0,\ldots,(L-1)
during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. We will use
\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}
in order to bound the total update time of
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
We start by describing the algorithm for initializing data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
, and then provide an algorithm for updating it.

Initialization

At the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, we construct a collection
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
of clusters
C
, that contains every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, for which
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
holds, and only such clusters. For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, we also construct the corresponding set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=V^{F}(C)\cap S^{L}_{k}
of vertices. We emphasize that the sets
V^{F}(C)
of vertices are not maintained explicitly.

Consider now some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
. In order to efficiently maintain the sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices as the phase progresses, we will store, together with every vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, some additional information. Specifically, assume that
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
for some level
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
. We will store, together with every vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, a list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
, that contains every type-2 supernode
u=u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
, such that
u
is marked as important for
C
, and
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
holds. Intuitively, these are the supernodes that are responsible for including vertex
x
in set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, and this additional data structure will allow us to maintain the set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
efficiently, as these supernodes may become unimportant for
C
over the course of the phase.

For every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, we also maintain pointers from
x
to every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
with
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
.

In order to initialize the
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
data structure, we start by setting
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}=\emptyset
, and then process all levels
L^{\prime}=0,\ldots,L-1
one by one.

Processing Level
0
.

We first describe an algorithm for processing level
0
. For every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, we use data structure
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{0}(v^{0}(x))
to compute all clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
with
v^{0}(x)\in V(C)
. For each such cluster
C
, if
C
does not belong to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
yet, we add
C
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
and initialize
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=\left\{x\right\}
. Otherwise, if
C
already lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, we add vertex
x
to set
Z^{L}_{k}
. Since every regular vertex may lie in at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
, the running time required to process level
0
is bounded by
O(\Delta\cdot|S^{L}_{k}|)
.

Processing Level
L^{\prime}
.

Consider now some integer
0<L^{\prime}\leq L-1
, and assume that levels
0,\ldots,L^{\prime}-1
were already processed. We now show an algorithm to process level
L^{\prime}
.
The algorithm for processing level
L^{\prime}
consists of two steps.

In the first step, we process every cluster
C
that lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
one by one. Each such cluster
C
must belong to
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L^{\prime}}
. In order to process cluster
C
, we check whether
u^{L^{\prime}}(C)
is a vertex of
H^{L^{\prime}}
. If this is not the case, then no further processing of
C
is required. Assume now that
u^{L^{\prime}}(C)
is a vertex of
H^{L^{\prime}}
. Then we consider every cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, such that
u^{L^{\prime}}(C)
was marked as an important supernode for
C^{\prime}
(in which case
u^{L^{\prime}}(C)\in V(C^{\prime})
must hold). Recall that data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
for level
L^{\prime}
maintains a pointer from
u^{L^{\prime}}(C)
to every such cluster
C^{\prime}
.
For each such cluster
C^{\prime}
, if

C^{\prime}
does not currently lie in
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, then we add it to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, and initialize
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
. If
C^{\prime}
currently lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, then set
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
is already initialized.
Next, we consider every vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
one by one. For each such vertex
x
, if
x
already lies in set
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
, then we add supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C)
to the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
. Otherwise, we add
x
to
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
, and we initialize the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
to contain supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C)
. This completes the algorithm for processing a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, and the algoritm for the first step of processing level
L^{\prime}
.

Recall that, from Property
P2
, for every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, there are at most
(4\Delta)^{L^{\prime}+1}
clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
with
x\in V^{F}(C)
before level
L^{\prime}
is processed. In particular,
|{\mathcal{C}}^{*}|\leq|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot(4\Delta)^{L^{\prime}+1}
must hold then. Moreover, from
4.5
, every supernode of
H^{L^{\prime}}
may be marked as important for at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
. It is then easy to verify that the running time of the first step is bounded by
O\left(|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot(4\Delta)^{L^{\prime}+2}\right)
.

In the second step, we consider every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
one by one.
For each such vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, if
v^{L^{\prime}}(x)\in V(H^{L^{\prime}})
, then we use data structure
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}^{L^{\prime}}(v^{L^{\prime}}(x))
to compute all clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
with
v^{L^{\prime}}(x)\in V(C)
. For each such cluster
C
, if
C
does not belong to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
yet, we add
C
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
and initialize
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=\left\{x\right\}
and
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)=\emptyset
. Otherwise, we add vertex
x
to
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
if it does not lie in this set already. Since every regular vertex may lie in at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, the running time of this second step is
O(|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot\Delta)
.

This completes the algorithm for processing level
L^{\prime}
. Once all levels
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
are processed, we add, for every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, pointers from
x
to every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
with
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
. This completes the algorithm for initializing the
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
data structure.
From the discussion so far, the running time required to initialize the data structure is bounded by
O\left(|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot(4\Delta)^{L+1}\right)\leq O\left(|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right)
. Clearly, set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
contains all clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
with
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
, and only such clusters. Therefore, Properties
F2
and
F3
hold after the initialization of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
.

Updating the
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
Data Structure.

We now describe an algorithm for maintaining the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, so that Properties
F1
–
F4
hold. For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, we will also maintain the set
Z^{L}_{k}
of vertices, and, for every vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
, containing every supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
that is marked as important for
C
, for which
x\in V^{F}(C^{\prime})
holds.

It is easy to verify that we only need to update the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters if a new cluster
C^{\prime}
is created via the cluster splitting operation of some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
(if a cluster splitting operation is applied to a cluster
C\not\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, then
Z^{L}_{k}(C)=\emptyset
must hold, and, from Property
P3
,
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
holds as well, so we do not need to add
C^{\prime}
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
). Additionally, we may need to update the sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, if one of the following happen: (i) a regular vertex is deleted from a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
; or (ii) a supernode that was marked as important for a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
becomes unimportant for
C
. Notice also that, if some vertex
x
is deleted from a set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
with
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, then this change may propagate to higher levels: if, for some level
L^{\prime\prime}>L^{\prime}
, supernode
u^{L^{\prime\prime}}(C)
lies in
H^{L^{\prime\prime}}
, and it is an important supernode for some cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime\prime}}\cap{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, then we may need to delete
x
from
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
as well. We also need to update the data structure if a supernode splitting operation occurs, for some supernode
u\in V(C)
, where
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
. In this case, the set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
may not change, but we may need to update the lists
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
of some vertices
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
