. We now consider each of these updates one by one.

Deletion of a regular vertex from a cluster.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
be a cluster that currently lies in set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, for some
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, and assume that some type-1 regular vertex
v^{L^{\prime}}(x)
was just deleted from
C
(if the deleted vertex is a type-2 regular vertex, then no update is needed). If
x\not\in S^{L}_{k}
, then no other updates are needed. Assume now that
x\in S^{L}_{k}
. If the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
is empty, then we delete
x
from
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
. Otherwise, no update is needed to the data structure.
Note that the running time required to update the data structure is asymptotically bounded by the number of clusters containing vertices
v^{L^{\prime}}(x)
, for
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
. Therefore, the total time required in order to update data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
due to the deletions of regular vertices from clusters is asymptotically bounded by the total update time of data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

A supernode becomes unimportant for a cluster.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
be a cluster that currently lies in set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, for some
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, and assume that some type-2 supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
that was previously marked as important for
C
, is now marked as unimportant (this includes the case when
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
is deleted from cluster
C
). We consider every vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
one by one. For each such vertex
x
, we delete supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
from the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
. If the list becomes empty, and there is no type-1 regular vertex
v^{L^{\prime}}(x)
in
V(C)
, then we delete
x
from set
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
. The total time required in order to update the data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
is asymptotically bounded by the number of deletions performed in the lists
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
for vertices
x\in S^{L}_{k}
.

A new cluster is created.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
be a cluster, with
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, for some
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, and assume that a new cluster
C^{\prime}
was just created added to
{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
via a cluster-splitting operation applied to cluster
C
.

We initialize
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})=\emptyset
, and then consider every type-1 regular vertex
v^{L^{\prime}}(x)\in V(C^{\prime})
one by one. For each such vertex, if
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, then we add
x
to
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
. Next, we process every type-2 supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})\in V(C^{\prime})
. For each such supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
, if it is marked as important for
C^{\prime}
, then we consider every vertex
y\in Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime\prime})
. If
y\in Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
holds, then we add supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
to the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C^{\prime},y)
. Otherwise, we add
y
to
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
, and we initialize the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C^{\prime},y)
to contain the supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
.
Once all regular vertices and type-2 supernodes of
C^{\prime}
are processed, if
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
, then we add
C^{\prime}
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
.
The time required in order to update data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
due to the creation of a new cluster
C^{\prime}
is asymptotically bounded by
|V(C^{\prime})|
plus the total number of entries that were added to lists
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C^{\prime},x)
for vertices
x\in S^{L}_{k}
.

Note that the splitting of cluster
C
may lead to supernode splitting of the supernode
u^{L^{\prime\prime}}(C)
in graphs
H^{L^{\prime\prime}}
with
L^{\prime\prime}\geq L^{\prime}
, to which such a supernode belongs. We now provide an algorithm that updates the data structure due to supernode splitting.

Supernode splitting.

Consider some level
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, and assume that some supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
just underwent a supernode-splitting operation, through which a new supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
was created, where
C^{\prime\prime}\subseteq C^{\prime}
is a cluster that was just split off from cluster
C^{\prime}
. As a result, the newly created supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
may have been added into some clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
. Let
C
be any such cluster, and recall that
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})\in V(C)
must hold (due to the definition of allowed updates to clusters maintained by the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
data structure). If supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
is not marked as an important cluster for
C
, then no further updates are necessary. Assume now that
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
is marked as important for
C
. In this case,
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
must also be important for
C
, and so
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})\subseteq Z^{L}_{k}(C)
currently holds. Since cluster
C^{\prime\prime}
was just split off from cluster
C^{\prime}
, from
Property
P3
,

V^{F}(C^{\prime\prime})\subseteq V^{F}(C^{\prime})
currently holds, and so
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime\prime})\subseteq Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
. In particular,
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime\prime})\subseteq Z^{L}_{k}(C)
must hold. For every vertex
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime\prime})
, we add supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime\prime})
to list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
.
The time required in order to update data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
due to the supernode splitting operation is asymptotically bounded by the total number of entries that were added to lists
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
for vertices
x\in S^{L}_{k}
and clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, plus the number of clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, into which the new supernode was inserted. The latter can be charged to the total update time of data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
.

Propagated updates.

Assume that some vertex
x
was just deleted from set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of any cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, and assume that
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
. For every level
L^{\prime}<L^{\prime\prime}<L
, we check whether
u^{L^{\prime\prime}}(C)
is a supernode of
H^{L^{\prime\prime}}
, and if so, we consider every cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime\prime}}
for which
u^{L^{\prime\prime}}(C)
is an important supernode, using the list that the supernode maintains. Consider any such cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime\prime}}
, and notice that
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
and
x\in V^{F}(C^{\prime})
must hold. We delete
u^{L^{\prime\prime}}(C)
from the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C^{\prime},x)
. If the list becomes empty, and
v^{L^{\prime\prime}}(x)\not\in V(C)
, then we delete
x
from
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
(which, in turn, may trigger updates in higher levels). Notice however that the running time of all such updates can be charged to the number of supernodes deleted from lists
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C^{\prime},x)
, for vertices
x\in S^{L}_{k}
and clusters
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
.

This completes the algorithm for maintaining the collection
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters, and sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices for clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
We claim that the algorithm guarantees Properties
F1
–
F4
. Indeed, it is immediate to see that, once the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters is initialized, we never delete clusters from it, so Property
F1
holds. It is also easy to verify that, at the beginning of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, we correctly initialize the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters to contain every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
with
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
, and only such clusters. As the phase progresses, we only add new clusters
C
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
if
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\neq\emptyset
holds. Therefore, Property
F2
is maintained.

As noted already, Property
F3
holds at the beginning of the phase. Since vertex sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
are decremental, the only way that the set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices is non-empty for a cluster
C\not\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, is if
C
is a newly created cluster. However, in this case, cluster
C
must have been created via a cluster-splitting operation of some cluster
C^{\prime}
, and, from Property
P3
,
V^{F}(C)\subseteq V^{F}(C^{\prime})
must hold. But then
Z^{L}_{k}(C)\subseteq Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})
, and so
Z^{L}_{k}(C^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
. Therefore,
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
holds, and our algorithm will add cluster
C
to set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
the moment it is created. This establishes Property
F3
. Property
F4
follows immediately from the algorithm.

We conclude that the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
of clusters that we maintain satisfies Properties
F1
–
F4
. It is then easy to verify that the sets
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of vertices for clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
are maintained correctly by our algorithm.

We now analyze the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.
Recall that we have already bounded the running time of the algorithm that initializes the data structure by
O\left(|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot(4\Delta)^{L+1}\right)
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
denote the collection
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
at the end of the phase. Since set
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
is incremental, set
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
maintains every cluster that ever belonged to
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
over the course of the phase.

For every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, we denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)
the collection of all clusters
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, for which
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
ever held during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. Let
\Pi(x)
be the collection of all pairs
(C,u)
, where
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)
, and supernode
u
ever belonged to list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
. We bound the total update time of the algorithm for maintaining data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
(excluding the initialization) as follows. For every vertex
x\in S^{L}_{k}
, we let the budget of
x
be
\beta(x)=|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)|+|\Pi(x)|
. Whenever vertex
x
is added to a set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
of a newly created cluster
C
, or whenever the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
is modified, we can charge the time required to process these changes to the budget of
x
. This is because, for every cluster
C
with
x\in Z^{L}_{k}(C)
, every supernode can be added to the list
\mbox{\sf{supernodes}}(C,x)
at most once, and then deleted from the list at most once (because the status of a supernode
u
for cluster
C
may never switch from unimportant to important, and because set
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
of vertices is decremental for all clusters
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
). Furthermore, vertex
x
may only be added to set
Z^{L}_{k}(C)
once when cluster
C
is created, and after that it may only be deleted from the set. The additional running time that is required by the algorithm for maintaining data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
is asymptotically bounded by the time that is required in order to maintained data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, that we denoted by
\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}
. Overall, the total update time that is required in order to maintain data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
(excluding initialization) is bounded by:

O\left(\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}+\sum_{x\in S^{L}_{k}}\beta(x)\right)

.

Next, we bound
\sum_{x\in S^{L}_{k}}\beta(x)
. First, from Property
P2
,
|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)|\leq\sum_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}(4\Delta)^{L^{\prime}+1}\leq(4\Delta)^{L+1}
. Next, we bound
|\Pi(x)|
. Consider a pair
(C,u)\in\Pi(x)
, and assume that
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, for some
0<L^{\prime}<L
, and
u=u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
. Then
C^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)
must hold, and supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
is important for cluster
C
.

Consider now some level
0\leq L^{\prime\prime}<L
, and a cluster
C^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)\cap\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime\prime}}
. Let
L^{\prime\prime}<L^{\prime}<L
be any level, such that supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
belongs to graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
at any time during Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
. From
4.5
, the total number of clusters
C\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
, such that supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
was ever marked as important for
C
over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
is at most
\Delta
. Therefore, there are at most
\Delta
pairs
(C,u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime}))\in\Pi(x)
, where
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
ever held. We conclude that for every cluster
C^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)
, there are at most
L\cdot\Delta
pairs
(C,u^{L^{\prime}}(C^{\prime}))\in\Pi(x)
, where
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
. Therefore,
|\Pi(x)|\leq|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}(x)|\cdot L\cdot\Delta\leq L\cdot(4\Delta)^{L+1}\cdot\Delta
. Overall, we get that:

\sum_{x\in S^{L}_{k}}\beta(x)\leq|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot\left((4\Delta)^{L+1}+L\cdot(4\Delta)^{L+1}\cdot\Delta\right)\leq|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot(4\Delta)^{2L}.

Overall, the total update time that is required in order to maintain the flat vertex sets (including initialization) is bounded by
O\left(\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}+|S^{L}_{k}|\cdot(4\Delta)^{2L}\right)\leq O\left(\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}+M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right)
, since
|S^{L}_{k}|\leq 2|A^{L}_{k}|\leq 2M^{q-L+1}
,
\Delta=\hat{m}^{16\epsilon^{3}}
, and
L\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
.

4.2.5
Analysis of Total Update Time

We now bound the total update time of the level-
L
data structure. We first fix a single level-
L
phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
, and analyze the total update time of the level-
L
data structure over the course of the phase. Recall that the total update time required by data structure
\mathsf{DS_{FlatSets}}
is bounded by:

\mathsf{T_{FS}}\leq O\left(\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}+M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right),

where
\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}
is total update time of data structures from levels
0,\ldots,L-1
during phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
.

The total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Basic}}
is:

\mathsf{T_{basic}}\leq O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+\mathsf{T_{FS}}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{3})}\right).

and the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS_{Imp}}
is bounded by:

O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}+\mathsf{T_{basic}}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right).

Overall, we get that the total update time of the level-
L
data structure over the course of Phase
\Phi^{L}_{k}
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}+\mathsf{T_{basic}}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}+M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+\mathsf{T_{FS}}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(M^{q-L+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+\tilde{T}^{L}_{k}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right).\end{split}

Recall that the number of level-
L
phases is
M^{L}
. Let
T^{<L}
denote the total update time of data structures for levels
0,\ldots,L-1
over the course of the whole algorithm. Then the total update time of the level-
L
data structure over the course of the whole algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}O\left(M^{q+1}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+T^{<L}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right)&\leq O\left(m\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+T^{<L}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right).\end{split}

(We have used the fact that
M^{q}<m^{1+\epsilon}\leq m\cdot\hat{m}^{\epsilon}
.)

Let
T^{0}
denote the total update time of the level-
0
data structure over the course of the algorithm. We then get the following recursion:

T^{<1}=T^{0}\leq O\left(m\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\right),

and for
1<L\leq q+1
:

T^{<L}\leq O\left(m\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}+T^{<(L-1)}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right).

Since
q=\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, it is then easy to see that:

T^{<q+1}\leq T^{0}\cdot\left(\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon^{2})}\right)^{q+1}\leq T^{0}\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\leq O\left(m\cdot\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}\right).

The amortized update time of the algorithm is bounded by
\hat{m}^{O(\epsilon)}
per operation, as required.

4.3
Responding to Short-Path Queries

In this section we provide an algorithm to support
short-path
queries. We start by showing that, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<q+1}
, all vertices lying in the flattened set
V^{F}(C)
are close to each other in the current graph
G
. We also show an algorithm that, for all
0\leq L\leq q
, given a path
P
in graph
H^{L}
between a pair
v^{L}(x),v^{L}(y)
of type-1 regular vertices, transforms it into a path connecting
x
to
y
in
G
, while approximately preserving its length. We will exploit this path-transformation algorithm in order to support
short-path
queries.

4.3.1
Paths between Vertices in Flattened Sets, and Path Transformation

Consider some level
0\leq L\leq q
, and some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
during the time horizon. Let
C
be a cluster that lies in set
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
at time
\tau
. Recall that we have defined a dynamic set
V^{F}(C)
of vertices associated with cluster
C
, that is used in order to construct and maintain graphs at higher levels. We show that every pair of vertices in set
V^{F}(C)
is connected by a short path in the current graph
G
, and we further show an efficient algorithm that computes such a path. Recall that
\alpha=\alpha(n^{3})
is the approximation factor that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
from
5.1
achieves.

Claim 4.9
.

There is a large enough constant
\tilde{c}
, and a deterministic algorithm that, at any time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, given a level
0\leq L\leq q
, a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}
, and a pair of vertices
x,y\in V^{F}(C)
, returns a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in the current graph
G
, whose length is at most
(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L+1}\cdot D_{i}
, where
i=\operatorname{scale}(C)
. The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E(P)|)
.

Proof.

Throughout, we denote
i=\operatorname{scale}(C)
.
The proof is by induction on the level
L
. The base case is when
L=0
. From the definition of the flattened sets of vertices, if
x,y\in V^{F}(C)
, then
v^{0}(x),v^{0}(y)\in V(C)
must hold. We execute query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}^{0}_{i}(C,x,y)
in data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i}
, that must return a path
Q
connecting vertex
v^{0}(x)
to vertex
v^{0}(y)
in graph
H^{0}
, whose length is at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i}
. We can assume w.l.o.g. that the path contains no type-1 regular vertices except for its endpoints, since every type-1 regular vertex has degree
1
in
H^{0}
. By suppressing all type-2 regular vertices on path
P
, deleting the first and the last vertex on the path, and replacing each supernode
u(z)
with the corresponding vertex
z\in V(G)
, we obtain a path
P
connecting
x
and
y
in the current graph
G
, whose length is at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i}
. The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E(P)|)
.

For the step of the induction, we consider some level
0<L\leq q
, and assume that the claim holds for all levels
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
. In the remainder of the proof, whenever we refer to dynamic graphs or data structures that our algorithm maintains, we refer to them at time
\tau
, unless stated otherwise.
Let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
be the given cluster, and let
x,y\in V^{F}(C)
be a pair of vertices.

From the definition of the flattened set
V^{F}(C)
of vertices, either (i)
v^{L}(x)\in V(H^{L})
and
v^{L}(x)\in V(C)
; or (ii) there is a supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
, that is marked as important for
C
, and
v^{L}(x)\in V^{F}(C^{\prime})
. Assume first that
v^{L}(x)\in V(H^{L})
. Then we define a vertex
z=x
, and we let
Q
be a path in graph
G
, that only consists of vertex
x
.
Assume now that the latter holds, that is, there is a supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
, which is marked as important for
C
, and
v^{L}(x)\in V^{F}(C^{\prime})
.
Let
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
be the level with
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
.
Since supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as important for
C
,
N(u^{L}(C^{\prime}))\neq\emptyset
(from Rule
R1
), and so
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is not an isolated vertex in
H^{L}
. Therefore, our data structure maintains a representative vertex from cluster
C^{\prime}
at level
L
, that we denote by
z=\sigma_{L}(C^{\prime})
. From Property
B2
,
z\in V^{F}(C^{\prime})
must hold, and vertex
v^{L}(z)
lies in graph
H^{L}
. From the definition of graph
H^{L}
, edge
(v^{L}(z),u^{L}(C^{\prime}))
belongs to
H^{L}
. From the definition of important supernodes, since supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as important for cluster
C
, all regular vertices that are neighbors of
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
in
H^{L}
belong to
C
, so in particular
v^{L}(z)\in V(C)
. Lastly, since supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as important for cluster
C
,
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{scale}(C)
must hold. We denote
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})=i^{\prime}
; recall that
\operatorname{scale}(C)
is denoted by
i
.
We apply the algorithm from the induction hypothesis to level
L^{\prime}
, cluster
C^{\prime}
, and vertices
x
and
z
, that both belong to
V^{F}(C^{\prime})
. We then obtain a path
Q
in graph
G
, that connects
x
to
z
, whose length is bounded by
(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L^{\prime}+1}\cdot D_{i^{\prime}}\leq(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L}\cdot D_{i}
. The time required to compute the path is bounded by
O(|E(Q)|)
.

To summarize, so far we have defined a vertex
z\in V(G)
, with
v^{L}(z)\in V(C)
, and we have computed a path
Q
in graph
G
, connecting
x
to
z
, whose length is at most
(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L}\cdot D_{i}
.

We repeat the same procedure with vertex
y
, to obtain a vertex
z^{\prime}\in V(G)
, with
v^{L}(z^{\prime})\in V(H^{L})
and
v^{L}(z^{\prime})\in V(C)
.
We also compute a path
Q^{\prime}
connecting
z^{\prime}
to
y
in graph
G
, whose length is at most
(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L}\cdot D_{i}
, in time
O(|E(Q^{\prime})|)
.

Next, we perform query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}^{L}_{i}(C,v^{L}(z),v^{L}(z^{\prime}))
in data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}_{i}
, to obtain a path
\tilde{P}
in graph
H^{L}
, that connects vertices
v^{L}(z)
and
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
, whose length is at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i}
. The query time is bounded by
O(|E(\tilde{P})|)
.

Let
u^{L}(C_{1}),u^{L}(C_{2}),\ldots,u^{L}(C_{r})
denote the sequence of type-2 supernodes on path
\tilde{P}
, as we traverse it from vertex
v^{L}(z)
to vertex
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
. For all
1\leq j\leq r
, let
i_{j}=\operatorname{scale}(C_{j})
. For
1\leq j\leq r
, we denote by
v^{L}(z_{j}),v^{L}(z^{\prime}_{j})
the two vertices that immediately precede and immediately follow supernode
u^{L}(C_{j})
on path
\tilde{P}
.
We will use the following observation in order to transform path
\tilde{P}
in graph
H^{L}
into a path in graph
G
that connects
z
to
z^{\prime}
, while approximately preserving the path length.

Observation 4.10
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given an index
1\leq j\leq r
, computes a path
\tilde{Q}_{j}
in graph
G
, that connects
z_{j}
to
z^{\prime}_{j}
, whose length is at most
(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L}\cdot D_{i_{j}}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(\tilde{Q}_{j})|)
.

Proof.

We fix an index
1\leq j\leq r
. From the construction of graph
H^{L}
, since edges
(v^{L}(z_{j}),u^{L}(C_{j}))
,
(v^{L}(z^{\prime}_{j}),u^{L}(C_{j}))
are present in graph
H^{L}
, it must be the case that
z_{j}\in V^{F}(C_{j})
and
z^{\prime}_{j}\in V^{F}(C_{j})
. Let
L^{\prime}<L
be the level with
C_{j}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}
. We apply the algorithm from induction hypothesis to level
L^{\prime}
, cluster
C_{j}
, and vertices
z_{j},z^{\prime}_{j}
, to obtain a path
\tilde{Q}_{j}
connecting
z_{j}
to
z^{\prime}_{j}
in graph
G
, whose length is at most
(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L^{\prime}+1}\cdot D_{i_{j}}\leq(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L}\cdot D_{i_{j}}
. The time required to compute the path is bounded by
O(|E(\tilde{Q}_{j})|)
.

Consider now some index
1\leq j<r
, and let
P_{j}
be the subpath of path
\tilde{P}
between
v^{L}(z^{\prime}_{j})
and
v^{L}(z_{j+1})
. We denote the length of
P_{j}
by
\ell^{\prime}_{j}
. We now define a path
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}_{j}
connecting
z^{\prime}_{j}
to
z_{j+1}
in graph
G
, such that the length of
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}_{j}
is at most
\ell^{\prime}_{j}
. If
z^{\prime}_{j}=z_{j+1}
, then
\tilde{Q}_{j}=(z^{\prime}_{j})
. Otherwise, we denote the sequence of supernodes on path
\tilde{Q}_{j}
(which must all be type-1 supernodes) by
u^{L}(a_{1}),u^{L}(a_{2}),\ldots,u^{L}(a_{r_{j}})
. From the definition of graph
H^{L}
,
a_{1}=z^{\prime}_{j}
, and
a_{r_{j}}=z_{j+1}
must hold. We can also assume that every consecutive pair of supernodes on the path are distinct (since otherwise the path traverses the same edge back and forth). It is then easy to verify that, for all
1\leq b<r_{j}
, the regular vertex that appears between
u^{L}(a_{b})
and
u^{L}(a_{b+1})
on path
P_{j}
must be a type-2 regular vertex, that we denote by
v^{L}(e_{b})
, and moreover,
a_{b},a_{b+1}
are the endpoints of edge
e_{b}
. Therefore, the sequence
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r_{j}})
of edges in graph
G
define a path that connects
z^{\prime}_{j}
to
z_{j+1}
. It is immediate to verify that the length of the path is bounded by
\ell^{\prime}_{j}
.

We also consider the subpath
P_{0}
of
\tilde{P}
between
v^{L}(z)
and
v^{L}(z_{1})
, whose length is denoted by
\ell^{\prime}_{0}
, and the subpath
P_{r}
of
\tilde{P}
between
v^{L}(z_{r})
and
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
, whose length is denoted by
\ell^{\prime}_{r}
. Using exactly the same algorithm, we compute a path
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}_{0}
connecting
z
to
z_{1}
in
G
of length at most
\ell^{\prime}_{0}
, and a path
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}_{r}
connecting
z_{r}
to
z^{\prime}
in
G
, whose length is at most
\ell^{\prime}_{r}
.

It is immediate to verify, from the construction of graph
H^{L}
, that the length of path
\tilde{P}
in
H^{L}
is at least
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\ell^{\prime}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}D_{i_{j}}
. Therefore,
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\ell^{\prime}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}D_{i_{j}}\leq\alpha\cdot D_{i}
.

Consider now a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
G
, that is obtained by concatenating the paths
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}_{0},\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Q}_{r},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}_{r}
. Then path
P^{\prime}
connects
z
to
z^{\prime}
in
G
, and its length is bounded by
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\ell^{\prime}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L}\cdot D_{i_{j}}\leq\tilde{c}^{L}\alpha^{L+1}\cdot D_{i}
. Lastly, we let
P
be the path obtained by concatenating paths
Q,P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
. Then
P
is a path in graph
G
that connects
x
to
y
, and its length is bounded by
2(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L}\cdot D_{i}+\tilde{c}^{L}\alpha^{L+1}\cdot D_{i}\leq(\tilde{c}\cdot\alpha)^{L+1}\cdot D_{i}
. It is easy to verify that the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E(P)|)
.

The following corollary of
4.9
allows us to efficiently transform paths in graph
H^{L}
, for all
1\leq L\leq q
, into paths in graph
G
, while approximately preserving the path length.

Corollary 4.11
.

There is a large enough constant
c^{\prime}
, and a deterministic algorithm, that, at any time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, given a level
0\leq L\leq q
, a pair
v^{L}(z),v^{L}(z^{\prime})\in H^{L}
of type-1 regular vertices, and a path
P
connecting
v^{L}(z)
to
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
in
H^{L}
, whose length is denoted by
D^{\prime}
, computes a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
G
, connecting
z
to
z^{\prime}
, so that the length of
P^{\prime}
is bounded by
(c^{\prime}\alpha)^{L+1}\cdot D^{\prime}
. The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)
.

Proof.

The proof of the corollary uses arguments similar to those that appeared in the proof of
4.9
.

Let
u^{L}(C_{1}),u^{L}(C_{2}),\ldots,u^{L}(C_{r})
denote the sequence of type-2 supernodes on path
P
, as we traverse it from
v^{L}(z)
to
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
. For all
1\leq j\leq r
, let
i_{j}=\operatorname{scale}(C_{j})
. As in the proof of
4.9
, we denote, for all
1\leq j\leq r
, by
v^{L}(z_{j})
and
v^{L}(z^{\prime}_{j})
the two vertices that immediately precede and immediately follow supernode
u^{L}(C_{j})
on path
P
. For
1\leq j<r
, we also let
P_{j}
be the subpath of
P
between vertices
v^{L}(z^{\prime}_{j})
and
v^{L}(z_{j+1})
, We also let
P_{0}
be the subpath of
P
between
v^{L}(z)
and
v^{L}(z_{1})
, and
P_{r}
the subpath of
P
between
v^{L}(z^{\prime}_{r})
and
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
. For
0\leq j\leq r
, let
\ell_{j}
be the length of path
P_{j}
in graph
H^{L}
. For convenience, we denote
z=z^{\prime}_{0}
and
z^{\prime}=z_{r+1}
. Notice that the length of path
P
in
H^{L}
is
D^{\prime}\geq\sum_{j=0}^{r}\ell_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}D_{i_{j}}
.

Using the same algorithm as in the proof of
4.9
, for all
0\leq j\leq r
, we compute a path
P^{\prime}_{j}
in graph
G
, that connects vertices
z^{\prime}_{j}
and
z_{j+1}
, whose length is at most
\ell_{j}
, in time
O(|E(P^{\prime}_{j})|)
.

Next, we consider each index
1\leq j\leq r
one by one. From the definition of graph
H^{L}
,
z_{j},z^{\prime}_{j}\in V^{F}(C_{j})
must hold. We now use the algorithm from
4.9
to compute a path
P^{\prime\prime}_{j}
in graph
G
, connecting vertices
z_{j}
and
z^{\prime}_{j}
, whose length is at most
(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L+1}\cdot D_{i_{j}}
, in time
O(|E(P^{\prime\prime}_{j})|)
.

Lastly, we obtain the final path
P^{\prime}
connecting
z
to
z^{\prime}
in graph
G
by concatenating the paths
P^{\prime}_{0},P^{\prime\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,P^{\prime\prime}_{r},P^{\prime}_{r}
. The length of the resulting path is bounded by:

\sum_{j=0}^{r}\ell_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L+1}D_{i_{j}}\leq(\tilde{c}\alpha)^{L+1}\cdot D^{\prime}.

Next, we define and analyze the central notion that we will use in order to support short-path queries, namely, covering chains.

4.3.2
Covering Chains

The notion of covering chains is central to our algorithm for supporting
short-path
queries.
In order to define covering chains, we first need to define the notion of a
covering quadruple
.

Definition 4.12
(Covering Quadruple).

Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
be any time during the time horizon, let
0\leq L\leq q
be a level,
0\leq i\leq\log\hat{D}
a scale,
C
a cluster that lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{L}_{i}
at time
\tau
, and
x\in V(G)
a vertex. We say that
(L,i,C,x)
is a
valid covering quadruple
at time
\tau
if vertex
v^{L}(x)
lies in graph
H^{L}
, and moreover,
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i}(v^{L}(x))
at time
\tau
.

Note that, if
(L,i,C,x)
is a valid covering quadruple, then it must be the case that
v^{L}(x)\in V(C)
, and, from the definition of the flattened vertex sets,
x\in V^{F}(C)
must also hold.
We are now ready to define the notion of covering chains.

Definition 4.13
(Covering Chain).

Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
be any time during the time horizon, and let
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))
be a sequence of valid covering quadruples. We say that
J
is a
valid covering chain for vertex
x\in V(G)
at time
\tau
, if the following hold:

•

0=L_{1}<L_{2}<\cdots<L_{r}\leq q
;

•

for all
1<j\leq r
,
i_{j}=i_{1}+10(L_{j}+1)
;

•

x_{1}=x
; and

•

for all
1\leq j<r
, supernode
u^{L_{j+1}}(C_{j})
lies in graph
H^{L_{j+1}}
at time
\tau
, and moreover,
x_{j+1}
is the representative vertex of cluster
C_{j}
at level
L_{j+1}
at time
\tau
.

We say that the length of the chain
J
is
r
. We also say that the level of chain
J
is
L_{r}
, and its scale is
i_{1}
. We may sometimes say that
J
lies at level
L_{r}
and scale
i_{1}
. Lastly, we say that vertex
x

owns
chain
J
.

Notice that, if
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))
is a valid covering chain for some vertex
x\in V(G)
, then for all
0\leq j<r
, it must be the case that
x_{j},x_{j+1}\in V^{F}(C_{j})
. Indeed, vertex
x_{j+1}
may only be a representative of cluster
C_{j}
at level
L_{j+1}
if
x_{j+1}\in V^{F}(C_{j})
holds, and, since
(L_{j},i_{j},C_{j},x_{j})
is a valid covering quadruple, as established above,
x_{j}\in V^{F}(C_{j})
must also hold.

For a vertex
x\in V(G)
, a level
0\leq L\leq q
, and a scale
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, we denote by
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
the set of all valid level-
L
chains at scale
i
that vertex
x
owns. Note that the set
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
of covering chains may change over time. We also denote

{\mathcal{K}}^{L}(x)=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\log D^{*}}{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
, and

{\mathcal{K}}(x)=\bigcup_{L=0}^{q}{\mathcal{K}}^{L}(x)
– the set of all covering chains owned by
x
.

Covering chains will be used in order to respond to
short-path
queries. Specifically, given a
short-path
query between a pair
x,y
of vertices of
G
, we start by computing, for every level
0\leq L\leq q
, the collections
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}(x)
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}(y)
of covering chains. For every pair
J\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L}(x)
,
J^{\prime}={\mathcal{K}}^{L}(y)
of such chains, we then perform a distance query between vertices
v^{L}(x_{r})
,
v^{L}(x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})
in
\mathsf{DS}^{L}
, where
x_{r}
is the vertex that appears in the last quadruple of
J
, and
x^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}
is the vertex that appears in the last quadruple of
J^{\prime}
. The responses to these distance queries will then guide the algorithm for computing a short path between the input vertices
x
and
y
.

We start by showing that, for every vertex
x
and level
L
, we can compute the set
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}(x)
of covering chains efficiently. We then explore several properties of covering chains that will be useful for us later.
Recall that we have denoted
\mu^{\prime}=(4\Delta)^{q+1}
, and that the dynamic degree bounds in all graphs
\left\{H^{L}\right\}_{L=0}^{q}
are at most
\mu^{\prime}
.

Claim 4.14
.

There is a large enough constant
\hat{c}
and a deterministic algorithm that, at any time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
during the time horizon, given a vertex
x\in V(G)
, a level
0\leq L\leq q
, and a scale
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, computes the current set
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
of covering chains at level
L
and scale
i
that
x
owns. The running time of the algorithm is at most
O(\hat{c}^{L+1}\cdot L^{2})
. Moreover, at any time
\tau
during the time horizon
{\mathcal{T}}
,
|{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)|\leq\hat{c}^{L+1}
holds.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the level
L
. The base of the induction is when
L=0
. Given a scale
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
and a vertex
x\in V(G)
, we compute cluster
C_{1}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{0}_{i}(v^{0}(x))
in time
O(1)
using data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{0}_{i}
. We then obtain a covering chain that consists of a single covering quadruple
J=(0,i,C_{1},x)
. It is easy to verify that
J
is the only level-
0
covering chain at scale
i
that vertex
x
owns. We then set
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)=\left\{J\right\}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(1)\leq\hat{c}
, if
\hat{c}
is a large enough constant.

Consider now some integer
0<L\leq q
, and assume that the claim holds for all levels
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
.
We also assume that, for all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, the time that is required in order to compute set
{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(x)
, given the sets
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{i}(x),\ldots,{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}-1}_{i}(x)
, is bounded by
L^{\prime}\cdot(\hat{c})^{L^{\prime}+1}
.

We start by computing, for all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
, the corresponding collection
{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(x)
of covering chains, and we denote
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(x)
. We then gradually construct the collection
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
of covering chains. Notice that, if
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))
is a covering chain that
x
owns at level
L
and scale
i
, then
r>1
must hold, and furthermore,
J^{\prime}=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r-1},i_{r-1},C_{r-1},x_{r-1}))
is a valid covering chain that
x
owns at level
L_{r-1}
and scale
i
. In particular,
J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
must hold. we say that
J^{\prime}
is the
prefix
of chain
J
.
Clearly, for every chain
J\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
, there is a chain
J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
that is the prefix of
J
.
Therefore, we will consider every chain in
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
one by one, and for each such chain
J^{\prime}
, we will attempt to extend it by a single quadruple in order to obtain a chain in
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
.

Consider any chain
J^{\prime}=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r-1},i_{r-1},C_{r-1},x_{r-1}))\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, and recall that
L_{1}=0
,
i_{1}=i
, and
x_{1}=x
must hold. Let
i_{r}=i+10L+10
. We check, in time
O(1)
, whether supernode
u^{L}(C_{r-1})
lies in graph
H^{L}
. If so, and if it is not an isolated vertex of
H^{L}
, then we let
x_{r}
be the unique representative of cluster
C_{r-1}
at level
L
, and we let
C_{r}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i_{r}}(x_{r})
.
Vertex
x_{r}
and cluster
C_{r}
can be computed in time
O(1)
using data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{L}
.
Let
J
be a chain that is obtained from
J^{\prime}
by appending the quadruple
(L,i_{r},C_{r},x_{r})
at the end of
J^{\prime}
. Clearly,
J\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
holds, and moreover, it is easy to verify that
J
is the only covering chain in
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
, such that
J^{\prime}
is a prefix of
J
. We then add
J
to
{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}
and continue to the next iteration. Notice that processing chain
J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
takes time
O(r)\leq O(L)
, where
r
is the length of the chain.

It is easy to verify that
|{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)|\leq|{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}|=\bigcup_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}|{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(x)|
. From the induction hypothesis, for all
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
,
|{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(x)|\leq\hat{c}^{L^{\prime}+1}
holds. Therefore,
|{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)|\leq\sum_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}\hat{c}^{L^{\prime}+1}\leq\hat{c}^{L+1}
.

The running time that is required in order to compute set
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
, once the sets
\left\{{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(x)\right\}_{L^{\prime}=1}^{L-1}
have been computed, is bounded by
O(L\cdot|{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}|)\leq O(L\cdot\hat{c}^{L+1})
. From the induction hypothesis, the time required to process levels
L^{\prime}=0,\ldots,L-1
is bounded by
\sum_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L-1}O(L^{\prime}\cdot\hat{c}^{L^{\prime}+1})\leq O(L^{2}\cdot(\hat{c}^{L+1}))
. Therefore, the total time that is required in order to construct the set
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}
of covering chains is bounded by
O(L^{2}\cdot(\hat{c}^{L+1}))
.

In the next crucial claim we show that, if
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))
is a covering chain that vertex
x
owns at time
\tau
, then
x\in V^{F}(C_{r})
must hold.

Claim 4.15
.

Let
x\in V(G)
be any vertex, and let
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))
be a covering chain that
x
owns at some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Then at time
\tau
,
x\in V^{F}(C_{r})
holds.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the length
r
of the chain
J
. The base of the induction is when the length of
J
is
1
, so
J=(L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1})
. Recall that
L_{1}=0
and
x_{1}=x
must hold. Furthermore, at time
\tau
,
C_{1}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{0}_{i_{1}}(v^{0}(x))
. In particular, regular vertex
v^{0}(x)
must lie in cluster
C_{1}
at time
\tau
. From the definition of set
V^{F}(C_{1})
, it must be the case that
x\in V^{F}(C_{1})
at time
\tau
.

Assume now that we are given an integer
0<r\leq q
, and that the claim holds for all integers
0\leq r^{\prime}<r
. Let
x\in V(G)
be a vertex, and let
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))
be a covering chain that
x
owns at some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Denote
J^{\prime}=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r-1},i_{r-1},C_{r-1},x_{r-1}))
. Clearly,
J^{\prime}
is a valid covering chain of length
r-1
that
x
owns at time
\tau
, so, from the induction hypothesis, at time
\tau
,
x\in V^{F}(C_{r-1})
holds. For convenience, we denote
C^{\prime}=C_{r-1}
,
L^{\prime}=L_{r-1}
,
C=C_{r}
, and
L=L_{r}
. We also denote
i^{\prime}=i_{r-1}
and
i=i_{r}
. Recall that
i\geq i^{\prime}+10
, from the definition of a covering chain. Our goal is to prove that, at time
\tau
,
x\in V^{F}(C)
holds.

From the definition of a covering chain, supernode
u^{L}(C^{\prime})
lies in graph
H^{L}
, and
x_{r}
is the representative vertex of cluster
C^{\prime}
at level
L
. In particular, it must be the case that
x_{r}\in V^{F}(C^{\prime})
(see Property
B2
of representative vertices).
Therefore, graph
H^{L}
contains an edge
(v^{L}(x_{r}),u^{L}(C^{\prime}))
, whose length is
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})}=D_{i^{\prime}}
.
For convenience, denote
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})
.
Recall that we denoted by
N(u)
the collection of neighbors of vertex
u
. From the definition of graph
H^{L}
, for every vertex
v\in N(u)
, the length of the edge
(u,v)
in
H^{L}
is
D_{i^{\prime}}
, and so
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{L}}(v,v^{L}(x_{r}))\leq 2D_{i^{\prime}}
. Recall that
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i}(v^{L}(x_{r}))
. Therefore,
u\in V(C)
, and
N(u)\subseteq V(C)
holds at time
\tau
.

Notice that, if supernode
u
is marked as important for cluster
C
at time
\tau
, then
V^{F}(C^{\prime})\subseteq V^{F}(C)
holds at time
\tau
, and so
x\in V^{F}(C)
must hold. Therefore, it is now enough to prove that supernode
u
is marked as important for cluster
C
at time
\tau
. The following observation will then finish the proof of
4.15
.

Observation 4.16
.

Supernode
u=u^{L}(C^{\prime})
is marked as important for cluster
C
at time
\tau
.

Proof.

Since vertex
x_{r}
is a representative vertex of cluster
C^{\prime}
at time
\tau
,
N(u)\neq\emptyset
at time
\tau
, and, as we have established already,
N(u)\subseteq V(C)
holds at time
\tau
. Moreover,
\operatorname{scale}(C^{\prime})<\operatorname{scale}(C)
. Therefore, according to Rule
R’1
, in order to prove that
u
is marked as an important for
C
at time
\tau
, it is enough to prove that,
for every time
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
during the current level-
L
phase, if
C^{*}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
and
u^{*}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u)
, then
N^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{*})\neq\emptyset
and
N^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{*})\subseteq V(C^{*})
holds at time
\tau^{\prime}
(here,
N^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{*})
is the set of all vertices that are neighbors of
u^{*}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
).

We now fix some time
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
during the current level-
L
phase.
Denote
C^{*}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
and
u^{*}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u)
.
Since
C_{r}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i}(v^{L}(x_{r}))
holds at time
\tau
, and
C^{*}=\operatorname{Ancestor}_{\tau^{\prime}}(C)
, from the Consistent Covering property of the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
data structure, at time
\tau^{\prime}
, it must be the case that
B_{H^{L}}(v^{L}(x_{r}),2^{i})\subseteq V(C^{*})
.

Since, at time
\tau
, edge
(v^{L}(x_{r}),u)
lies in graph
H^{L}
(as
x_{r}\in V^{F}(C^{\prime}))
, from
3.4
,
edge
(v^{L}(x_{r}),u^{*})
belongs to graph
H^{L}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
, so in particular,
N^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{*})\neq\emptyset
. Furthermore, from the same observation, the lengths of the edges incident to
u^{*}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
are equal to the lengths of the edges incident to
u
at time
\tau
, which, in turn, are all equal to
D_{i^{\prime}}
. Therefore,
the length of every edge that connects a vertex of
N^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{*})
to
u^{*}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
is
D_{i^{\prime}}
.

We conclude that, for each vertex
v\in N^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{*})
, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{L}}(v,v^{L}(x_{r}))\leq 2D_{i^{\prime}}
must hold. Since, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H^{L}}(v^{L}(x_{r}),2^{i})\subseteq V(C^{*})
, and
i\geq i^{\prime}+10
, we get that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
N_{\tau^{\prime}}(u^{*})\subseteq V(C^{*})
held.

From Rule
R’1
, we conclude that supernode
u
is marked as important for cluster
C
at time
\tau
.

4.3.3
A Central Claim

In this subsection we state and prove the central claim that allows us to support
short-path
queries.
The section uses the notion of levels of edges and of paths of graph
G
, that was defined at the beginning of
Section

4
.
We also need one additional definition.

Consider some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, a vertex
x\in V(G)
, a scale
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, and a level
0\leq L\leq q
. Recall that we have defined a collection
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
of covering chains at level
L
and scale
i
that
x
owns at time
\tau
. We define a set
R^{L}_{i}(x)
of vertices of
G
, as follows. Vertex
x^{\prime}
lies in
R^{L}_{i}(x)
if and only if there exists a covering chain
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
, such that
x^{\prime}=x_{r}
. Recall that chain
J=((L_{1},i_{1},C_{1},x_{1}),\ldots,(L_{r},i_{r},C_{r},x_{r}))
has level
L
if
L_{r}=L
, and it has scale
i
if
i_{1}=i
.
We are now ready to state the central claim.

Claim 4.17
.

Consider any time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, level
0\leq L\leq q
, and scale
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
. Let
x,y\in V(G)
be any pair of vertices, such that, at time
\tau
, there is a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in
G
, whose length is at most
D_{i}\leq D^{*}
, and
\operatorname{Level}(P)=L
. Then
R^{L}_{i}(x),R^{L}_{i}(y)\neq\emptyset
, and moreover, at time
\tau
, there is a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H^{L}
, connecting a vertex
v^{L}(x^{\prime})
, with
x^{\prime}\in R_{i}^{L}(x)
to a vertex
v^{L}(y^{\prime})
, with
y^{\prime}\in R_{i}^{L}(y)
, whose length is at most
2^{10L+4}\cdot D_{i}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the level
L
.

Induction Base.

The base case is when
L=0
. Consider a pair
x,y
of vertices of
G
, and a path
P\subseteq G
connecting
x
to
y
, whose length is at most
D_{i}
, such that path
P
lies at level
0
. In other words, every edge of
P
is an original edge of graph
G
.

From the definition of covering chains, the set
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{i}(x)
of level-
0
scale-
i
covering chains that vertex
x
owns at time
\tau
only contains a single chain
J=(0,i,C,x)
, where
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{0}_{i}(v^{0}(x))
. Therefore,
R^{0}_{i}(x)=\left\{x\right\}
. Similarly, the set
{\mathcal{K}}^{0}_{i}(y)
of level-
0
scale-
i
covering chains that vertex
y
owns at time
\tau
only contains a single chain
J^{\prime}=(0,i,C^{\prime},y)
, where
C^{\prime}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{0}_{i}(v^{0}(y))
, and so
R^{0}_{i}(y)=\left\{y\right\}
.

We now show that there exists a path
P^{\prime}
, connecting vertices
v^{0}(x)
and
v^{0}(y)
in graph
H^{0}
, whose length is at most
4D_{i}
.
We denote the sequence of the sequence of vertices on path
P
by
(x=z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{r}=y)
. For
1\leq j<r
, we denote
e_{j}=(z_{j},z_{j+1})
. Consider the following sequence of vertices in graph
H^{0}
:

v^{0}(z_{1}),u^{0}(z_{1}),v^{0}(e_{1}),u^{0}(z_{2}),v^{0}(e_{2}),\ldots,v^{0}(e_{r-1}),u^{0}(z_{r}),v^{0}(z_{r}).

Recall that graph
H^{0}
contains edges
(v^{0}(z_{1}),u^{0}(z_{1}))
and
(u^{0}(z_{r}),v^{0}(z_{r}))
of length
1
each. Additionally, for all
1\leq j<r
, since
e_{j}=(z_{j},z_{j+1})
, edges
(v^{0}(e_{j}),u^{0}(z_{j}))
and
(v^{0}(e_{j}),u^{0}(z_{j+1}))
are both present in
H^{0}
, and have length
\ell(e_{j})
each. It is the easy to see that the above sequence of vertices defines a path in graph
H^{0}
, that connects
v^{0}(x)
to
v^{0}(y)
, and that the length of the path is at most
2+2\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}\ell_{G}(e_{j})\leq 4\ell_{G}(P)\leq 4D_{i}
.

Induction Step.

We now consider a level
0<L\leq q
, and we assume that the claim holds for all levels
0\leq L^{\prime}<L
. Let
x,y\in V(G)
be a pair of vertices, and let
P
be a level-
L
path in graph
G^{(\tau)}
that connects
x
to
y
, so that the length of the path is at most
D_{i}
. Recall that
\hat{D}=D^{*}\cdot 2^{10q+10}
,
Since
D_{i}\leq D^{*}
, we get that
D_{i}\cdot 2^{10q+10}\leq\hat{D}
.

We denote by
\Phi^{L}_{k}
the current level-
L
phase. From the definition of a level-
L
path, there must be at least one edge
e\in E(P)
, so that
e\in A^{L}_{k}
. We denote
E(P)\cap A^{L}_{k}=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r}\right\}
, and we assume that the edges are indexed in the order of their appearance on path
P
, as we traverse it from
x
to
y
. For all
1\leq j\leq r
, we denote
e_{j}=(x_{j},y_{j})
, and we assume that
x_{j}
appears before
y_{j}
on path
P
, as we traverse it from
x
to
y
. Note that it is possible that
x=x_{1}
or
y=y_{r}
(or both).

Let
{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{P_{0},P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}\right\}
be the collection of paths that is obtained from
P
once we delete the edges of
E(P)\cap A^{L}_{k}
from it. For convenience, we denote
x=y_{0}
and
y=x_{r+1}
. Then for all
0\leq j\leq r
, path
P_{j}
has endpoints
y_{j}
and
x_{j+1}
. Some of the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
may consist of a single vertex. For example, if
x=x_{1}
, then
P_{0}=(x)
, and if, for some
0<j\leq r
,
y_{j}=x_{j+1}
, then
P_{j}=(y_{j})
.
For all
0\leq j\leq r
, we denote the length of path
P_{j}
in graph
G
by
\ell_{j}
. We also let
i_{j}
be the smallest integer, such that
\ell_{j}\leq 2^{i_{j}}
holds, so, if
\ell_{j}\neq 0
, then
\ell_{j}\leq 2^{i_{j}}<2\ell_{j}
.

Observe first that every vertex of
\left\{x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{r}\right\}
lies in
S^{L}_{k}
, and so for each such vertex
z
, there is a corresponding type-1 regular vertex
v^{L}(z)
in graph
H^{L}
. We start by showing that, for all
1\leq j<r
, there is a path
Q_{j}
in graph
H^{L}
, that connects
v^{L}(y_{j})
to
v^{L}(x_{j+1})
, whose length is comparable to
\ell_{j}
. In fact this path will only contain two edges.

Claim 4.18
.

For all
1\leq j<r
, there is a path
Q_{j}
in graph
H^{L}
, connecting
v^{L}(y_{j})
to
v^{L}(x_{j+1})
, such that the length of the path is at most
2^{i_{j}+10L+1}
.

Proof.

We fix an index
1\leq j<r
.
If
y_{j}=x_{j+1}
, then we let
Q_{j}
be a path that consists of a single vertex – vertex
v^{L}(y_{j})
(that must belong to
H^{L}
from our discussion above). From now on we assume that
y_{j}\neq x_{j+1}
.

For ease of notation, we denote
z=y_{j}
and
z^{\prime}=x_{j+1}
. We also denote
P^{\prime}=P_{j}
, and
i^{\prime}=i_{j}
. Therefore,
P^{\prime}
is a path in graph
G
, connecting vertex
z
to vertex
z^{\prime}
. The length of
P^{\prime}
is at most
2^{i^{\prime}}
, and
\operatorname{Level}(P^{\prime})<L
.

Throughout the proof, whenever we refer to graph
G
, data structures that we maintain, or any other dynamic objects, by default we refer to all these objects at time
\tau
(unless stated otherwise). The proof of
4.18
easily follows from the following observation.

Observation 4.19
.

There is a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{<L}
, with
\operatorname{scale}(C)\leq i^{\prime}+10L
, such that
z,z^{\prime}\in V^{F}(C)
.

Indeed, assume that
4.19
holds. Then, from the definition of graph
H^{L}
, it must be the case that
u^{L}(C)
is a vertex of
H^{L}
(as
z,z^{\prime}\in S^{L}_{k}
), and moreover, edges
(v^{L}(z),u^{L}(C))
and
(u^{L}(C),v^{L}(z^{\prime}))
are present in
H^{L}
. Since the length of each such edge is
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C)}\leq 2^{i_{j}+10L}
, we obtain the desired path
Q_{j}
connecting
v^{L}(z)
to
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
in
H^{L}
by simply concatenating these two edges. The length of the path is bounded by
2^{i_{j}+10L+1}
. In order to complete the proof of
4.18
, it is now enough to prove
4.19
.

Proof of
4.19
.

Let
L^{\prime}=\operatorname{Level}(P^{\prime})
, so
L^{\prime}<L
. From the induction hypothesis, there is a path
\tilde{Q}
in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
, of length at most
2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+4}
, connecting some vertices
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}),v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime})
, where
\tilde{z}\in R^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(z)
and
\tilde{z}^{\prime}\in R^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})
.

From the definition of set
R^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(z)
of vertices, there is a covering chain
J\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(z)
, whose last quadruple is
(L^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},C,\tilde{z})
, where
i^{\prime\prime}={i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+10}
. From the definition of a covering quadruple,
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
holds, and
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{L^{\prime}}(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}))
. Note that
\operatorname{scale}(C)=i^{\prime\prime}={i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+10}\leq i^{\prime}+10L
.
From
4.15
,
z\in V^{F}(C)
holds. From now on, it remains to show that
z^{\prime}\in V^{F}(C)
holds as well.

As before, from the definition of the set
R^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})
of vertices, there is a covering chain
J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})
, whose last quadruple is
(L^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime},\tilde{z}^{\prime})
, where
i^{\prime\prime}={i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+10}
as before. We consider the penultimate quadruple of the chain
J^{\prime}
, that we denote by
(L^{\prime\prime},\tilde{i},\tilde{C},\tilde{z}^{\prime\prime})
. Recall that
\tilde{i}=\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C})={i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime\prime}+10}\leq{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}}
, since
L^{\prime\prime}<L^{\prime}
must hold.

From the definition of a covering chain, supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C})
lies in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
, and vertex
\tilde{z}^{\prime}
is its representative in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
.

Note that, if we let
J^{\prime\prime}
be the chain obtained from
J^{\prime}
by deleting its last quadruple, then we obtain a valid containment chain that vertex
z^{\prime}
owns. The scale of the chain remains
i^{\prime}
, and its level is
L^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore, from
4.15
,
z^{\prime}\in V^{F}(\tilde{C})
holds. We need the following observation to complete the proof of
4.19

Observation 4.20
.

At time
\tau
, supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C})
lies in cluster
C
, and it is marked as an important supernode for cluster
C
.

Assume first that the observation is correct. We now get that, at time
\tau
,
V^{F}(\tilde{C})\subseteq V^{F}(C)
holds, and so
z^{\prime}\in V^{F}(C)
must hold, completing the proof of
4.19
. It now remains to prove
4.20
, which we do next.

Proof of
4.20
.

Recall that there is a path
\tilde{Q}
in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
, whose length is at most

2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+4}
, connecting
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z})
to
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime})
. On the other hand, edge
(u^{L}(\tilde{C}),v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}))
lies in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
, and its length is
2^{\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C})}\leq 2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the distance between vertices
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z})
and
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C})
in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
at time
\tau
is bounded by
2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+4}+2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}}\leq 2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+5}
.

Recall that
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{L^{\prime}}(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}))
, and that

i^{\prime\prime}={i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+10}
. Therefore, cluster
C
contains all vertices of
B_{H^{L^{\prime}}}(u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}),2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}})
. Since every edge incident to
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C})
has length
2^{\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C})}\leq 2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}}
, we get that, at time
\tau
,
N(u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}))\subseteq V(C)
.
Notice that, from the above discussion, at time
\tau
, supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C})
had at least one neighbor, and
\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C})<\operatorname{scale}(C)
holds.

From Rule
R’1
regarding important supernodes, it is now enough to prove the following: for every time
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
during the current level-
L^{\prime}
phase, if we denote by
N_{\tau^{\prime}}
the set of all neighbors of supernode
\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}))
at time
\tau^{\prime}
, then, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
N_{\tau^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
held, and every vertex of
N_{\tau^{\prime}}
belonged to cluster
\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
. If this condition holds, then, from Rule
R’1
, supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C})
is marked as important for
C
at time
\tau
.
The following observation will then complete the proof of
4.20
.

Observation 4.21
.

Let
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
be any time during the current level-
L^{\prime}
phase. Denote
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}))
,
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
, and let
N_{\tau^{\prime}}
be the set of all vertices of
H^{L^{\prime}}
that were neighbors of supernode
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})
at time
\tau^{\prime}
. Then, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
N_{\tau^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
, and
N_{\tau^{\prime}}\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
hold.

Proof.

We fix some time
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
during the current level-
L^{\prime}
phase. In this proof, whenever we refer to graphs, data structures, and other dynamic objects that our algorithm maintains, we refer to these objects at time
\tau^{\prime}
, unless stated otherwise.
From the Consistent Covering Property, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H^{L^{\prime}}}(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}),2^{i^{\prime\prime}})\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
held.
Since
i^{\prime\prime}={i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+10}
, cluster
C^{\prime}
contains all vertices of
B_{H^{L^{\prime}}}(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}),2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+10})
.

Recall that we have established that, at time
\tau
, the distance between
vertices
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z})
and
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime})
in
H^{L^{\prime}}
was at most
2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+4}
(due to path
\tilde{Q}
).
Since, from
3.3
, distances between regular vertices of
H^{L^{\prime}}
may only grow overtime, at time
\tau^{\prime}
, the distance between
vertices
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z})
and
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime})
was also at most
2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+4}
.

From
3.4
, since edge
(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}),u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}))
lies in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
at time
\tau
, it must be the case that edge
(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime}),u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}^{\prime}))
lies in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
. Moreover, the lengths of all edges incident to
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})
at time
\tau^{\prime}
are equal to the lengths of the edges incident to
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C})
at time
\tau
; equivalently,
\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})=\operatorname{scale}(\tilde{C})\leq i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}
must hold. We conclude that the length of every edge connecting vertex
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})
to a vertex of
N_{\tau^{\prime}}
is at most
2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
, and that the distance between vertices
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z})
and
u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}^{\prime})
in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
at time
\tau^{\prime}
is at most
2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+4}+2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}}\leq 2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+5}
. Since cluster
C^{\prime}
contains all vertices of
B_{H^{L^{\prime}}}(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}),2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}+10})
, we get that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
, every vertex in
B_{H^{L^{\prime}}}(u^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{C}^{\prime}),2^{i^{\prime}+10L^{\prime}})
belonged to cluster
C^{\prime}
. In particular,
N_{\tau^{\prime}}\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
holds at time
\tau^{\prime}
.

So far
4.18
allows us to transform each path
P_{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}
, for
0<j<r
into a corresponding path in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
. We use the next claim to deal with path
P_{0}
.

Claim 4.22
.

There is a path
Q_{0}
in graph
H^{L}
, connecting some vertex
v^{L}(y^{\prime}_{0})
with
y^{\prime}_{0}\in R^{L}_{i}(x)
to vertex
v^{L}(x_{1})
, such that the length of the path is at most
2^{i+10L+1}
.

Proof.

For convenience, we denote
y_{0}
by
z
and
x_{1}
by
z^{\prime}
. We also denote
P_{0}
by
P^{\prime}
. Recall that path
P^{\prime}
connects
z
to
z^{\prime}
in graph
G
, and its length is bounded by
D_{i}=2^{i}
.

Assume first that
z=z^{\prime}
, and so
P^{\prime}=(z)
.
Notice that in this case,
v^{L}(z)\in V(H^{L})
holds. We construct a level-
L
scale-
i
covering chain
J
for
z
as follows. The chain consists of two quadruples. The first quadruple is
(L_{1},i,z,C)
, where
L_{1}=0
, and
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{0}_{i}(v^{0}(z))
. Recall that
z\in V^{F}(C)
must hold, and, since
v^{L}(z)\in V(H^{L})
, supernode
u^{L}(C)
is present in graph
H^{L}
. We let
\hat{z}
be the representative vertex of cluster
C
for level
L
, let
i^{\prime}=i+10L+10
, and let
\hat{C}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i^{\prime}}(v^{L}(\hat{z}))
. The second quadruple of the chain
J
is
(L,i^{\prime},\hat{z},\hat{C})
. Note that, since, as we established already,
D_{i}\cdot 2^{q+10L+10}\leq\hat{D}
,
i^{\prime}\leq\log\hat{D}
holds. Since we obtain a valid level-
L
scale-
i
chain
J
that vertex
z
owns, we conclude that
\hat{z}\in R^{L}_{i}(z)
. Since
z=z^{\prime}
, and
z\in V^{F}(C)
, edge
(v^{L}(z^{\prime}),u^{L}(C))
lies in graph
H^{L}
, as does edge
(v^{L}(\hat{z}),u^{L}(C))
. We denote
y^{\prime}_{0}=\hat{z}
, and we return path
Q_{0}
that is a concatenation of edges
(v^{L}(\hat{z}),u^{L}(C))
and
(u^{L}(C),v^{L}(z^{\prime}))
. Since the length of each of these edges is
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C)}=2^{i}
, the length of the resulting path is
2^{i+1}
. We assume from now on that
z\neq z^{\prime}
.

Let
L^{\prime}=\operatorname{Level}(P^{\prime})
, so
L^{\prime}<L
.
Whenever we refer to dynamic graphs, data structures that our algorithm maintains, and other dynamic objects, we refer to them at time
\tau
, unless stated otherwise.

From the induction hypothesis, there is a path
\tilde{Q}
in graph
H^{L^{\prime}}
, of length at most
2^{i+10L^{\prime}+4}
, connecting some vertices
v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}),v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}^{\prime})
, where
\tilde{z}\in R^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(z)
and
\tilde{z}^{\prime}\in R^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})
.

From the definition of set
R^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(z)
, there is a covering chain
J\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i}(z)
, whose last quadruple is
(L^{\prime},i^{\prime},C,\tilde{z})
, where
i^{\prime}={i+10L^{\prime}+10}
. From the definition of a covering quadruple,
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{L^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}
holds, and
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}_{i^{\prime}}^{L^{\prime}}(v^{L^{\prime}}(\tilde{z}))
. Note that
\operatorname{scale}(C)=i^{\prime}={i+10L^{\prime}+10}\leq i+10L
.
From
4.15
,
z\in V^{F}(C)
holds.
We use the following observation.

Observation 4.23
.

At time
\tau
,
z^{\prime}\in V^{F}(C)
holds.

The proof of the observation uses arguments that are identical to those used in the proof of
4.19
, and we do not repeat them here.
Notice that, from the definition of graph
H^{L}
, since a vertex
z^{\prime}\in S^{L}_{k}
lies in the flattened set
V^{F}(C)
of vertices, supernode
u^{L}(C)
is present in graph
H^{L}
, and it connects to vertex
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
with an edge, whose length is
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C)}\leq 2^{i+10L^{\prime}+10}\leq 2^{i+10L}
. Let
z^{\prime\prime}
be the vertex that serves as the representative of cluster
C
at level
L
.

Denote
i^{\prime\prime}=i+10L+10
.
Since we have established that
D_{i}\cdot 2^{10q+10}\leq\hat{D}
, we get that
i^{\prime\prime}\leq\log\hat{D}
holds.
Let
J^{\prime}
be the covering chain that is obtained from
J
, by appending the covering quadruple
(L,i^{\prime\prime},C^{*},z^{\prime\prime})
, where
C^{*}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i^{\prime\prime}}(v^{L}(z^{\prime\prime}))
. Then
J^{\prime}
is a valid level-
L
scale-
i
covering chain that belongs to vertex
z
. Since
z^{\prime\prime}
is the representative of cluster
C
at level
L
, edge
(v^{L}(z^{\prime\prime}),u^{L}(C))
is present in graph
H^{L}
, and it length is
2^{\operatorname{scale}(C)}\leq 2^{i+10L}
. If
z^{\prime\prime}=z^{\prime}
, then we let path
Q_{0}
consist of a single vertex
z^{\prime\prime}
. Otherwise, path
Q_{0}
is a concatenation of the edges
(v^{L}(z^{\prime\prime}),u^{L}(C))
and
(u^{L}(C),v^{L}(z^{\prime}))
. The length of path
Q_{0}
is then bounded by
2^{i+10L+1}
. Since
J^{\prime}
is a valid level-
L
scale-
i
covering chain owned by
z
, we get that
z^{\prime\prime}\in R^{L}_{i}(z)
holds as required.

Lastly, we need the following claim in order to deal with path
P_{r}
. Its proof is identical to the proof of
4.22
, with vertex
x_{r+1}
playing the role of vertex
y_{0}
and vertex
y_{r}
playing the role of
x_{1}
.

Claim 4.24
.

There is a path
Q_{r}
in graph
H^{L}
, connecting some vertex
v^{L}(x^{\prime}_{r+1})
with
x^{\prime}_{r+1}\in R^{L}_{i}(x_{r+1})
to vertex
v^{L}(y_{r})
, such that the length of the path is at most
2^{i+10L+1}
.

We are now ready to complete the proof of
4.17
.
Recall that we have denoted
E(P)\cap A^{L}_{k}=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{r}\right\}
,
where the edges are indexed in the order of their appearance on
P
. For
1\leq j\leq r
, we denoted
e_{j}=(x_{j},y_{j})
, where vertex
x_{j}
appears closer to
x
on path
P
than
y_{j}
. We also denoted
y_{0}=x
and
x_{r+1}=y
. For all
1\leq j<r
, we have now defined a path
Q_{j}
in graph
H^{L}
, that connects vertex
v^{L}(y_{j})
to vertex
v^{L}(x_{j+1})
, whose length is at most
2^{i_{j}+10L+1}
. From our definition of the paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r-1}
, for all
1\leq j\leq r
, the length of path
P_{j}
is
\ell_{j}\geq 2^{i_{j}-1}
. Lastly, we have defined a path
Q_{0}
in graph
H^{L}
, that connects some vertex
v^{L}(y^{\prime}_{0})
with
y^{\prime}_{0}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{L}_{i}(x)
to vertex
v^{L}(x_{1})
, and a path
Q_{r}
in graph
H^{L}
, that connects vertex
v^{L}(y_{r})
to some vertex
v^{L}(x^{\prime}_{r+1})
, with
x^{\prime}_{r+1}\in R^{L}_{i}(y)
. The lengths of both these paths are bounded by
2^{i+10L+1}
.

For all
1\leq j<r
, we also construct a path
Q^{\prime}_{j}
in graph
H^{L}
, that
connects vertices
v^{L}(x_{j})
and
v^{L}(y_{j})
: consider the following sequence of vertices in graph
H^{L}
:
(v^{L}(x_{j}),u^{L}(x_{j}),v^{L}(e_{j}),u^{L}(y_{j}),v^{L}(y_{j}))
. It is easy to verify from the definition of graph
H^{L}
that this sequence defines a path in graph
H^{L}
, whose length is
2+2\ell_{G}(e_{j})
.

By concatenating the paths
Q_{0},Q^{\prime}_{1},Q_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{r},Q_{r}
, we obtain the desired path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H^{L}
, that connects some vertex
v^{L}(x^{\prime})
with
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{L}_{i}(x)
to some vertex
v^{L}(y^{\prime})
, with
y^{\prime}\in R^{L}_{i}(y)
.

From the above discussion, the length of the path is bounded by:

2\cdot 2^{i+10L+1}+\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}2^{i_{j}+10L+1}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}(2+2\ell_{G}(e_{j}))\leq 2^{i+10L+2}+2^{10L+3}\cdot\ell_{G}(P)\leq 2^{i+10L+4}.

4.3.4
Algorithm for Responding to Short-Path Queries

We are now ready to describe our algorithm for supporting
short-path
queries. Suppose we are given a pair
x,y\in V(G)
of vertices at some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Recall that our goal is to either
to respond either “YES” or “NO”, in time
O\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\log\hat{m}\right)
, so that, if the algorithm responds “NO”, then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(x,y)>D^{*}
holds. If the algorithm responds “YES”, then it may be asked additionally to compute a path
P
in the current graph
G
, connecting
x
to
y
, of length at most
D^{*}\cdot\alpha^{O(1/\epsilon)}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Let
x
and
y
be a pair of input vertices, and let
i=\log D^{*}
.
We consider every level
0\leq L\leq q
one by one. When level
L
is considered, we use the algorithm from
4.14
, in order to compute the collections
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(y)
of covering chains. Recall that the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(2^{O(L)}\cdot L^{2})\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
. Recall also that
|{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)|,|{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(y)|\leq 2^{O(L)}\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
.
Using the collections
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(y)
of covering chains, we can now compute the sets
R^{L}_{i}(x),R^{L}_{i}(y)
of vertices of
G
, in time
2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
. Clearly,
|R^{L}_{i}(x)|,|R^{L}_{i}(y)|\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
. Next, we consider every pair
z\in R^{L}_{i}(x),z^{\prime}\in R^{L}_{i}(y)
of vertices. Let
i^{\prime}=i+10L+4
, and recall that
i^{\prime}\leq\log\hat{D}
.
Recall that, for each pair
z\in R^{L}_{i}(x),z^{\prime}\in R^{L}_{i}(y)
of vertices,
v^{L}(z),v^{L}(z^{\prime})\in V(H^{L})
must hold. We let
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i^{\prime}}(v^{L}(z))
, and we check, in time
O(\log\hat{m})
, whether
v^{L}(z^{\prime})\in V(C)
holds. If so, we respond “YES”. If the algorithm is then asked to compute a path connecting
x
to
y
in graph
G
, then we do so as follows.

First, we perform query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}^{L}_{i^{\prime}}(C,v^{L}(z),v^{L}(z^{\prime}))
in data stucture
\mathsf{DS}^{L}_{i^{\prime}}
. Recall that the data structure must return a path
Q
of length at most
2^{i^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha
, connecting
v^{L}(z)
to
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
in
H^{L}
, in time
O(|E(Q)|)
. We apply the algorithm from
Corollary

4.11
to path
Q
in graph
H^{L}
in order to compute a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
G
, connecting
z
to
z^{\prime}
, whose length is bounded by
\alpha^{O(L)}\cdot 2^{i^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha\leq\alpha^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot D^{*}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)
. Next, we compute a path
P_{0}
connecting
x
to
z
in
G
, as follows. Since
z\in R^{L}_{i}(x)
, there must be some covering chain
J\in{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x)
, such that, if we denote by
(L_{r},i_{r},x_{r},C_{r})
the last quadruple of the chain, then
z=x_{r}
,
L_{r}=L
, and
i_{r}=i+10L
holds. As observed already,
z\in V^{F}(C_{r})
must hold (see the discussion immediately following
Definition

4.13
). Moreover, from
4.15
,
x\in V^{F}(C_{r})
holds as well. We can now use the algorithm from
4.9
to compute a path
P_{0}
connecting
x
to
y
in graph
G
, whose length is at most
\alpha^{O(L)}\cdot 2^{i+10L}\leq\alpha^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot D^{*}
. The time required to compute path
P_{0}
is bounded by
O(|E(P_{0})|)
. We compute a path
P_{1}
connecting
z^{\prime}
to
y
in
G
, of length at most
\alpha^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot D^{*}
similarly. By concatenating paths
P_{0},P^{\prime}
and
P_{1}
, we obtain a path
P
in graph
G
connecting
x
to
y
, whose length is at most
\alpha^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot D^{*}
. The running time required to compute path
P
is bounded by
O(|E(P)|)
.

Once every level
0\leq L\leq q
is processed, if the algorithm never returned “YES”, then we return “NO”. We claim that graph
G
may not contain a path connecting
x
to
y
, whose length is at most
D^{*}
. Indeed, assume for contradiction that there is a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in
G
, and the length of
P
is
D^{*}
. Denote
\operatorname{Level}(P)=L
, and let
i^{\prime}=i+10L+4
. From
4.17
, there is a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H^{L}
, connecting a vertex
v^{L}(x^{\prime})
, with
x^{\prime}\in R_{i}^{L}(x)
to a vertex
v^{L}(y^{\prime})
, with
y^{\prime}\in R_{i}^{L}(x)
, whose length is at most
2^{10L+4}\cdot D_{i}
. Consider an iteration of our algorithm for processing level
L
when the pair
z=x^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime}=y^{\prime}
of vertices was considered. Since there is a path of length at most
2^{i+10L+4}=2^{i^{\prime}}
connecting
v^{L}(z)
to
v^{L}(z^{\prime})
, if we denote
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{L}_{i^{\prime}}(v^{L}(z))
, then
v^{L}(z^{\prime})\in V(C)
must hold, and so our algorithm must have returned “YES”, a contradiction.

Excluding the time that is required in order to compute a path
P
once the algorithm returns “YES”, for every level
0\leq L\leq q
, the algorithm spends time
O(2^{O(1/\epsilon)})
in order to compute the sets
{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(x),{\mathcal{K}}^{L}_{i}(y)
of covering chains. The time required to process every pair
z\in R^{L}_{i}(x),z^{\prime}\in R^{L}_{i}(y)
of vertices is
O(\log\hat{m})
, and the number of such vertex pairs is bounded by
2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
. Therefore, processing a single level requires time
O\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\log\hat{m}\right)
. Since the number of levels is
q+1\leq 2/\epsilon
, overall, the time required to process a query (excluding the time to compute a path
P
once the algorithm returns “YES”) is bounded by
O\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\log\hat{m}\right)
.

5
Algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
Problem - Proof of
Theorem

3.8

We start by providing a high-level overtview of our algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem. The discussion here is somewhat over-simplified and is only intended in order to provide intuition. As in
[
Chu21
]
, the structure of the proof is inductive. Assume that our goal is to solve the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on an instance
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, that contains
W
regular vertices. Assume that we are given a precision parameter
\epsilon
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer. We start by providing a rather straightforward algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, that, on instances of size at most
W^{3\epsilon}
, has total update time at most
W^{c\epsilon}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(D)
, for some fixed constant
c
, where
D
is the distance parameter in the initial instance of the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem. This algorithm serves as the basis of the induction. Next, for all
1\leq z\leq 1/\epsilon
, we show that, if there is an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, that, on instances of size at most
W^{z\epsilon}
has total update time at most
W^{c\epsilon+z\epsilon}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(D)
, then there is an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem that, on instances of size at most
W^{(z+1)\epsilon}
, has total update time at most
W^{c\epsilon+(z+1)\epsilon}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(D)
. By letting
z=\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, we can thus obtain the desired algorithm for
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
. The most challenging part of the proof is, naturally, the inductive step, in which we assume the existence of an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem of a certain size, and prove the existance of an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem of a larger size.
To this end, we will need to develop a number of subroutines that build on the fact that there exists an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem of some specific size. In order to simplify the notation, we state here a generic assumption that can be used in all these subroutines as needed, by substituting the correct parameters.

Assumption 5.1
.

For some parameters
W>0
and
c^{\prime}>0
, there is a deterministic algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, that, given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E},D\right)
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, such that the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm is
W^{\prime}\leq W
, achieves approximation factor
\alpha(W^{\prime})
, where
\alpha(\cdot)\geq 2
is a non-decreasing function, and has total update time at most
W^{\prime}\cdot W^{\delta}\cdot D^{c^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{c}
for some large enough constant
c\geq 22
that does not depend on
W
or
W^{\prime}
, and a parameter
0<\delta<1
that may depend on
W
. Moreover, the algorithm ensures that, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the total number of clusters in the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}
that the algorithm maintains, to which vertex
v
ever belongs over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
\Delta(W^{\prime})
, where
\Delta(\cdot)\geq 2
is a non-decreasing function.

Observe that, if the assumption holds for some
W>0
, then it holds for all parameters
0<\tilde{W}<W
.
We emphasize that we do not make the above assumption right now. Instead we will develop a number of algorithms, that work provided the above assumption is correct.

We now turn to formally prove
Theorem

3.8
.
Assume that we are given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E},D\right)
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations, with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, and parameters
0<\epsilon<1/400
, and let
N
be the number of regular vertices in
H
. In order to prove
Theorem

3.8
, we use induction. Let
q=\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
. We prove by induction that, for all
3\leq i\leq q
, there is a deterministic algorithm for solving the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on instances with at most
N^{i\epsilon}
regular vertices. The base of the induction is a straightforward algorithm that simply creates a separate cluster for every regular vertex of
H
. For the step of the induction, we use a subroutine, that, given two parameters
W_{1}>W_{2}
, assuming that there exists an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on graphs with
W_{2}
regular vertices, provides an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on graphs with
W_{1}
regular vertices. The subroutine is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.2
.

Assume that, for some parameter
W>0
and
c^{\prime}=3

5.1
holds.
Then there is a deterministic algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, that, on input
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, that undergoes a sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, a parameter
\hat{W}\geq N
, where
N
is the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, such that
W<\hat{W}\leq W^{1.5}
, and a precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer, achieves approximation factor

\alpha^{*}=\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log\log N,\frac{(\alpha(W))^{2}\cdot\log\log N}{\epsilon^{16}}\right\}.

The algorithm ensures that, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the total number of clusters to which
v
ever belongs over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
N^{4\epsilon}
.
The algorithm has total update time:

\begin{split}O\left(N\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon}\cdot(\Delta(W))^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\log D)^{c}\right)+O\left(N\cdot W^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot D^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta(W)\right),\end{split}

We also use a simpler analogue of
Theorem

5.2
, for the regime where the dynamic degree bound
\mu
is close to the number of regular vertices in the input graph
H
.

Theorem 5.3
.

There is a deterministic algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, that, on input
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, that undergoes a sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, such that
\mu\geq N^{1/10}
holds, where
N
is the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, and a precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log N)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer, achieves approximation factor

\alpha^{*}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log\log N
.
The algorithm ensures that, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the total number of clusters to which
v
ever belongs over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
N^{4\epsilon}
.
The algorithm has total update time:
O\left(N^{1+9\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D\right)
.

We provide the proofs of
Theorem

5.2
and
Theorem

5.3
below, after we complete the proof of
Theorem

3.8
using them. The proof easily follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
N>0
, and
1/(\log N)^{1/50}\leq\epsilon<1/400
be parameters, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer, and let
q=1/\epsilon
. For all
3\leq i\leq q
, there is a deterministic algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, that, given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, such that, if we denote by
N^{0}(H)
the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then
N^{0}(H)\leq N^{i\epsilon}
, achieves approximation factor
\alpha_{i}=(\log\log(N^{2}))^{\tilde{c}\cdot 2^{2i}/\epsilon^{24}}
, and has total update time:

\begin{split}N^{0}(H)\cdot N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+\tilde{c}i\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\mu^{\tilde{c}i}\cdot D^{3},\end{split}

where
\tilde{c}
is a fixed large enough constant.
The algorithm also ensures that, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, there are at most
\Delta_{i}
clusters that ever contain
v
over the course of the algorithm, where
\Delta_{3}=N^{3\epsilon}
, and, for all
i>3
,
\Delta_{i}=N^{4\epsilon^{4}}
.

The proof of
Lemma

5.4
is conceptually straightforward, but technically somewhat cumbersome. We prove it by induction on
i
. The base case, where
i=3
, is shown by a straightforward algorithm that creates a separater cluster
C_{v}
for every regular vertex
v
, and maintains an
ES-Tree
in
C_{v}
rooted at vertex
v
, with depth bound
D
. In order to perform the induction step, we use the induction hypothesis to establish that
5.1
holds for some setting of parameters, and then apply
Theorem

5.2
(or
Theorem

5.3
if the parameter
\mu
