is large). We defer the proof of the lemma to Section
D
of Appendix.

Theorem

3.8
immediately follows from

Lemma

5.4
: we let
\epsilon^{\prime}=1/\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, so that
1/\epsilon^{\prime}
is an integer, and
\epsilon/2\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon
holds. Since, from the statement of
Theorem

3.8
,
1/(\log\hat{W})^{1/100}\leq\epsilon<1/400
holds, it is easy to verify that
1/(\log\hat{W})^{1/50}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}<1/400
.
Theorem

3.8
follows from applying the algorithm from
Lemma

5.4
to the input instance, with parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}
and
i=\left\lceil 1/\epsilon^{\prime}\right\rceil
.

In the remainder of this subsection, we focus on the proofs of
Theorem

5.2
and
Theorem

5.3
.
In order to prove both theorems, we define two new problems. One problem, called
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
, was already defined in
[
Chu21
]
. The goal of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is to manage a single cluster that is added to the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}
. The algorithm must ensure that, at all times, the diameter of the cluster
C
is not too large, and, if the diameter of
C
becomes too large, it must provide a witness in the form of two regular vertices
x,y
of
C
, for which
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(x,y)
is large. The algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is also responsible for supporting
short-path-query
queries between pairs of regular vertices in cluster
C
. In the second problem, that we call
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
, the goal is to maintain the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}
. The algorithm is not required to support
short-path-query
queries in clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
. However, given any such cluster
C
and a pair
x,y
of its regular vertices, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(x,y)
is large, it needs to provide a sequence of valid updates to cluster
C
, at the end of which either
x
or
y
are deleted from
C
. Below, we define each of these two problems, and provide algorithms for them, assuming
5.1
. We then complete the proof of
Theorem

5.2
and
Theorem

5.3
, by carefully combining the two resulting algorithms.

5.1
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
Problem

In this subsection, we define a problem called
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
, and state our algorithm for solving this problem. The problem was initially defined in
[
Chu21
]
. Our definition is essentially identical, except that we use slightly different parameters.

Intuitively, the input to the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(C,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(C)},D\right)
, where
C
is a connected subgraph of the original graph
H
.
Graph
C
undergoes a sequence of valid update operations, with dynamic degree bound
\mu
. We assume that we are given as input a distance parameter
D^{*}\geq D
. The algorithm is required to support queries

\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,v,v^{\prime})
, in which, given a pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V(C)
of regular vertices of
C
, it needs to return a path of length at most
\alpha D^{*}
connecting them in
C
, where
\alpha
is the approximation factor of the algorithm. At any time, the algorithm is allowed to raise a flag
F_{C}
, and to supply a pair

x,y
of regular vertices of
C
, with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(x,y)>D^{*}
; we refer to
x,y
as a
witness pair
. The algorithm then receives, as part of its input update sequence
\Sigma
, a sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
of edge-deletions and isolated vertex-deletions, at the end of which either
x
or
y
are deleted from
C
. We call sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
a
flag lowering sequence
.
Once the flag lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
is processed, flag
F_{C}
is lowered. However, the algorithm may raise the flag
F_{C}
again immediately, as long as it provides a new pair
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
of vertices with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})>D^{*}
. We emphasize that we view the resulting flag lowering sequences
\Sigma^{\prime}
as part of the input sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations that cluster
C
undergoes.
Queries
short-path-query
may only be asked when flag
F_{C}
is down.
We also emphasize that the initial cluster
C
that serves as input to the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem may have an arbitrarily large diameter, and so the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem may repeatedly raise the flag
F_{C}
, until it is able to support
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,v,v^{\prime})
(that intuitively means that the diameter of
C
has fallen under
\alpha D^{*}
).
We now provide a formal definition of the problem, starting from the definition of a valid flag-lowering sequence.

Definition 5.5
(Valid flag-lowering sequence).

Given a valid input structure

{\mathcal{J}}=\left(C,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(C)},D\right)
and a pair
x,y
of regular vertices of
C
, a sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
of valid update operations for
C
is called a
valid flag-lowering sequence
, if:

•

sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
only contains edge-deletion and isolated vertex-deletion updates;

•

once the updates from
\Sigma^{\prime}
are applied to
C
, either
x
or
y
are deleted from
C
.

Definition 5.6
(
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem).

The input to the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(C,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(C)},D\right)
, where
C
is a connected graph, a distance parameter
D^{*}>D
that is an integral power of
2
, and the desired approximation factor
\alpha
.
Graph
C
undergoes an online sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations, and we are given its dynamic degree bound
\mu
. The algorithm must support queries
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,v,v^{\prime})
: given a pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V(C)
of regular vertices of
C
, return a path
P
of length at most
\alpha\cdot D^{*}
connecting them in
C
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. The algorithm may, at any time, raise a flag
F_{C}
, at which time it must supply a pair
\hat{v},\hat{v}^{\prime}
of regular vertices of
C
(called a
witness pair
), with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(\hat{v},\hat{v}^{\prime})>D^{*}
.
Once flag
F_{C}
is raised, the algorithm will obtain, as part of its input update sequence
\Sigma
, a valid flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Flag
F_{C}
is lowered after the updates from
\Sigma^{\prime}
are processed by the algorithm. Queries
short-path-query
may only be asked when flag
F_{C}
is down.

We note that each flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
is viewed as part of the sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations that cluster
C
undergoes.

We will use the following theorem, that provides a simple algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, in the regime where the dynamic degree bound
\mu
is very large compared to the initial number of regular vertices in
C
.

Theorem 5.7
.

There is a deterministic algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on input
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(C,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(C)},D\right)
, that undergoes a sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu\geq(N^{0}(C))^{1/10}
, where
N^{0}(C)
is the number of regular vertices in
C
at the beginning of the algorithm, and a distance bound
D^{*}
, achieves approximation factor
1
, and has total update time:
O(N^{0}(C)\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D^{*})
.

Proof.

For brevity, we denote
N=N^{0}(C)
, and
\hat{W}=N\cdot\mu
. Note that the total number of edges that are ever present in graph
C
is bounded by
\hat{W}
. Let
V^{\prime}
be the set of all regular vertices of
C
at the beginning of the algorithm. For every regular vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
, we use the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
in order to maintain a modified
ES-Tree

T_{v}
, rooted at vertex
v
, in graph
C
, with depth parameter
D^{*}
, as graph
C
undergoes valid update operations. We denote by
S^{*}(v)
the set
S^{*}=\left\{v^{\prime}\in V(C)\mid\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(v,v^{\prime})>D^{*}\right\}
of vertices that the algorithm maintains. Recall that the total update time of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
is bounded by
O(\hat{W}\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log\hat{W})
. The total update time that is required in order to maintain all trees
T_{v}
, for all
v\in V^{\prime}
, is bounded by:

O(\hat{W}\cdot N\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log\hat{W})\leq O(N^{3}\cdot\mu^{2}\cdot D^{*})\leq O(N\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D^{*}),

since
N\leq\mu^{10}
.
Whenever, for any vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
, a new regular vertex
v^{\prime}
is added to set
S^{*}(v)
, we raise the flag
F_{C}
, and supply
v,v^{\prime}
as a witness pair. If a regular vertex
v
is deleted from
C
, we delete the corresponding
ES-Tree
data structure
T_{v}
.

It remains to describe an algorithm to respond to queries
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,v,v^{\prime})
. Observe that the query may only be asked when flag
F_{C}
is down, so
v^{\prime}\not\in S^{*}(v)
currently holds. We execute query
SSSP-query
in data structure
T_{v}
, to compute a path
P
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
C
, whose length is at most
D^{*}
.

Our main result for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is summarized in the following theorem. The proof is deferred to
Section

7
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Assume that, for some parameter
W>0
and
c^{\prime}=3

5.1
holds.
Then there is a deterministic algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on input
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(C,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(C)},D\right)
, that undergoes a sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, a parameter
\hat{W}\geq N^{0}(C)
, where
N^{0}(C)
is the number of regular vertices in
C
at the beginning of the algorithm, such that
W<\hat{W}\leq W^{1.5}
, a distance parameter
D^{*}\geq D
, and a precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer, achieves approximation factor
\alpha^{\prime}=\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})},(8\alpha(W))^{2}\right\}
, and has total update time:

O\left(\hat{W}\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot(\Delta(W))^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right)+O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\Delta(W)\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\right).

5.2
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
Problem

Intuitively,
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem is vey similar to the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem: given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E},D\right)
that undergoes an online sequence of valid update operations, we need to maintain a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters of
H
, and, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, a cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}
that contains
B_{H}(v,D)
, such that the Consistent Covering Property holds. The clusers in
{\mathcal{C}}
may only undergo allowed changes as before. However, we no longer require that queries
short-path-query
are supported; intuitively, this will be ensured by applying the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem to every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. However, the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem may, from time to time, raise flag
F_{C}
for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and supply a pair
x,y
of regular vertices of
C
, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(x,y)
is sufficiently large. In such cases, the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem is responsible for producing a valid Flag Lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
for
C
. It must then apply the updates from the flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
to cluster
C
, deleting all corresponding edges and vertices from
C
. These updates may be interspersed with cluster-splitting updates applied to cluster
C
. We will partition the collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters that the algorithm maintains into two subsets: set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of
primary
clusters, and set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
of
secondary
clusters. Intuitively, every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
is significantly smaller than
H
, and we will eventually apply the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
probem to each such cluster recursively. Therefore, once a cluster
C
is added to the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
of secondary clusters, we no longer need to keep track of it, or to update it. The primary clusters will be managed via the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem. The main challenge is to ensure that every regular vertex of
H
only belongs to a sufficiently small number of primary and secondary clusters over the course of the entire algorithm.
We now define the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem formally.

Problem Definition.

The input to the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem is the same as the input to the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem: we are given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E},D\right)
, where graph
H
undergoes an online sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with some given dynamic degree bound
\mu
. Additionally, we are given three parameters:
W_{1}
,
W_{2}
, and
\gamma>0
, such that
W_{2}<W_{1}\leq W_{2}^{1.5}
, and, if
N^{0}(H)
denotes the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then
N^{0}(H)\leq W_{1}
holds. Additionally, we are given a precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log W_{2})^{1/4}}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1/400
.

As in the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, the goal is to maintain a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clustes of
H
. At the beginning of the algorithm,
{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{H\right\}
must hold, and, as the algorithm progresses, clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}
may only undergo allowed changes:
\operatorname{DeleteVertex},\operatorname{AddSuperNode}
, and
\operatorname{ClusterSplit}
, which are defined exactly like in the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem.

The notion of ancestor-clusters is also defined exactly like in the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem. For every regular vertex
v\in V
, the algorithm must maintain a cluster
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
, with
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(C)
, and, like in the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, we require that the Consistent Covering property holds: namely, if, at time
\tau
,
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
holds for a regular vertex
v
, then, for all
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C))
held.

The collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters that the algorithm maintains is partitioned into two subsets: set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of
primary clusters
, and set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
of
secondary clusters
. We require that the following properties hold:

R1.

at all times,
|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|\leq\frac{W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}
holds;

R2.

if we denote, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, the number of regular vertices that were in
C
when it was created by
N^{0}(C)
, then
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}N^{0}(C)\leq W_{1}^{1+2\epsilon^{\prime}}
holds at all times;

R3.

once a cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, no further updates may be applied to
C
, except those corresponding to the updates that graph
H
undergoes (e.g. if an edge or an isolated vertex are deleted from
H
, then the same edge or isolated vertex are deleted from
C
, and if a supernode
u\in U
that lies in
C
undergoes supernode-splitting, a similar supernode-splitting update is applied to
C
). In particular, cluster-splitting may only be applied to secondary clusters;

R4.

for every regular vertex
v\in V
, if
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
ever held, and if
\tau
is the first time when
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, then, at time
\tau
, cluster
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
just joined
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, and, from time
\tau
onwards,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C
always holds;

R5.

when a cluster
C
joins set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, it may contain at most
W_{2}
regular vertices; and

R6.

for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the total number of clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
to which
v
ever belongs is bounded by
W_{2}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}
.

Unlike the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, we no longer require that
short-path-query
queries are supported; this will eventually be ensured by applying the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem to each primary cluster, and by solving the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem recursively on each secondary clusters. However, we require that the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem supports
flag lowering
operations:

Flag-Lowering Operation.

At any time, the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem may receive a primary cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, and a pair
x,y
of regular vertices of
C
, with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(x,y)>\gamma\cdot D
. The algorithm is then required to compute a valid flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
(see
Definition

5.5
) for
(C,x,y)
. The algorithm then must apply the updates from the flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
to cluster
C
, though these updates can be intersperesed with cluster-splitting updates, in which new clusters
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
are added to set
{\mathcal{C}}
. Once all updates from
\Sigma^{\prime}
are processed, the Flag-Lowering operation terminates.

Our main result for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem is summarized in the following theorem. This algorithm is one of our main technical contributions.

Theorem 5.9
.

There is a deterministic algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem, that, given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E},D\right)
that undergoes an online sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, and parameters
W_{1},W_{2}
, and
\epsilon^{\prime}
as in the problem definition, together with a parameter
\gamma=\frac{2^{28}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}
, has total update time
O\left(\frac{W_{1}^{2+3\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{2}}{W_{2}}\right)
.

The proof of the theorem is provided in
Section

6
.

5.3
Completing the Proof of
Theorem

5.2

We assume that, for some parameter
W>0
and
c^{\prime}=3

5.1
holds. Assume that we are given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
,
that undergoes a sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
. Let
N
denote the number of regular vertices of
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, and let
\hat{W}\geq N
be the parameter that is given as part of input. Recall that
W<\hat{W}\leq W^{1.5}
. Additionally, we are given a precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer. We denote the parameters
\Delta(N)
and
\alpha(W)
from
5.1
by
\Delta
and
\alpha
, respectively. We will additionally use a parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{2}
. Clearly,
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/12}}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1/400
holds.
Let
\gamma=\frac{2^{30}\log\log N}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}
, and let
D^{*}
be the smallest itegral power of
2
with
D^{*}\geq\gamma\cdot D
, so
D^{*}\leq\frac{2^{31}\log\log N}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}\cdot D
.

Our algorithm maintains a collection
{\mathcal{R}}
of clusters of the input graph
H
, that we call
basic clusters
. This collection of clusters is different and separate from the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we set
{\mathcal{R}}=\left\{H\right\}
. As the algorithm progresses, clusters may be added to set
{\mathcal{R}}
, but they may never leave it. For every cluster
R
that ever belonged to
{\mathcal{R}}
, we denote by
N^{0}(R)
the number of regular vertices that
R
contained when it first joined
{\mathcal{R}}
. Notice that
N^{0}(R)\leq N
must hold. We say that cluster
R
is
large
, if
N^{0}(R)\geq N^{3\epsilon}
, and we say that it is
small
otherwise.
Once a cluster
R
joins the set
{\mathcal{R}}
, as graph
H
continues to undergo valid update operations, we update cluster
R
accordingly, as described in
Section

3.2.2
.
Whenever a new cluster
R
joins the collection
{\mathcal{R}}
of basic clusters, we will execute an algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
that will process this cluster. We now describe this algorithm.

Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
.

Let
R
be a cluster that was just added to the set
{\mathcal{R}}
of basic clusters. Assume first that cluster
R
is large. Denote
W_{1}(R)=N^{0}(R)
and
W_{2}(R)=W_{1}(R)/N^{\epsilon}
. Note that, since
R
is a large cluster,
W_{1}(R)\geq N^{3\epsilon}
, and so
W_{2}(R)<W_{1}(R)\leq(W_{2}(R))^{1.5}
holds. We initialize the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem from
Theorem

5.9
on cluster
R
, with parameters
W_{1}=W_{1}(R),W_{2}=W_{2}(R)
and
\epsilon^{\prime}
. Recall that cluster
R
undergoes a sequence of valid update operations, that correspond to the valid update operations performed in graph
H
. Therefore, we obtain a valid input to the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem. We denote the algorithm from
Theorem

5.9
applied to this input by
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
, and the data structure that it maintains by
\mathsf{DS}^{\prime}(R)
. Recall that the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left(\frac{(W_{1}(R))^{2+3\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{2}}{W_{2}(R)}\right)\leq O\left((N^{0}(R))^{1+3\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot N^{\epsilon}\right)\leq O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon}\right).

We denote the collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
maintains by
{\mathcal{W}}(R)
, and we denote the collections
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
of clusters by
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R)
, respectively.

Whenever a new cluster
C
is added to the set
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
of primary clusters, we initialize the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem
from
Theorem

5.8
on cluster
C
. Recall that cluster
C
undergoes a sequence of valid update operations that correspond to the valid update operations that cluster
R
undergoes. We use the distance parameter
D^{*}
that we defined above, and we let
\hat{W}(C)=N^{0}(C)
, where
N^{0}(C)\leq W_{1}(R)\leq N
is the number of regular vertices in cluster
C
when it joins the set
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
. The precision parameter
\epsilon
remain unchaged. Recall that we have assumed that
5.1
holds for a parameter
W
, and that
W<\hat{W}\leq W^{1.5}
holds.
If
\hat{W}(C)>W
, then we define
W^{\prime}=W
, and otherwise we let
W^{\prime}=\hat{W}(C)/2
. Clearly,
W^{\prime}<\hat{W}(C)\leq W^{1.5}
, and
\frac{\hat{W}(C)}{W^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{W}
hold. Since
5.1
holds for parameter
W
and for
c^{\prime}=3
, it must also hold for
W^{\prime}
and for
c^{\prime}=3
.
We denote the algorithm from
Theorem

5.8
for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, applied to the above input, by
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C)
. Recall that the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(\hat{W}(C)\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right)+O\left((\hat{W}(C))^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right)\\
&\quad\quad\leq O\left(N^{0}(C)\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+O\left(N^{0}(C)\cdot\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right).\end{split}

Whenever Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C)
raises flag
F_{C}
, and supplies a pair
x,y
of regular vertices of
C
with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(x,y)>D^{*}
, we supply this pair of vertices to Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
. Recal that
D^{*}\geq\frac{2^{30}\log\log N}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}\cdot D\geq\frac{2^{28}\log\log W_{1}(R)}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}\cdot D
holds, as required. Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
then provides a flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
, which is given to Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C)
for processing.

Lastly, whenever a new cluster
C
joins the set
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R)
of secondary clusters, we add
C
to the set
{\mathcal{R}}
of basic clusters. This completes the description of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
for the case where
R
is a large cluster. We now analyze its total update time. Recall that the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
is bounded by
O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon}\right)
.
The total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C)
for each primary cluster
C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(N^{0}(C)\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+O\left(N^{0}(C)\cdot\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right).\end{split}

Since, from Property
R2
,
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)}N^{0}(C)\leq(W_{1}(R))^{1+2\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon^{\prime}}
, we get that the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad+O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right).\end{split}

Next, we describe Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
for the case where the basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is small. Recall that, in this case,
N^{0}(R)\leq N^{3\epsilon}
holds. We construct a collection
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
of clusters that contains, for every regular vertex
v\in V(R)
, a cluster
C_{v}
, that is initially a copy of the cluster
R
. As cluster
R
undergoes valid update operations, the same valid update operations are applied to cluster
C_{v}
. If vertex
v
is deleted from
R
, then we delete all edges and vertices from cluster
C_{v}
. We initialize the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
for maintaining a modified
ES-Tree
in graph
C_{v}
, with source vertex
v
, distance bound
D^{*}
, as the graph undergoes valid update operations; since supernode-splitting is a special case of vertex-splitting, every update that graph
C_{v}
undergoes is either edge-deletion, or isolated vertex-deletion, or vertex-splitting.
Whenever some vertex
x\in V(C_{v})
is added to the set
S^{*}
of vertices (in which case
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C_{v}}(x,v)>D^{*}
must hold), we delete vertex
x
with its all incident edges from
C_{v}
. We denote the algorithm for maintaining
ES-Tree
in graph
C_{v}
by
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C_{v})
. Recall that the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(m^{*}\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log m^{*})
, where
m^{*}
is the total number of edges that ever belonged to graph
C_{v}
. Since
m^{*}\leq N^{0}(R)\cdot\mu\leq N^{3\epsilon}\cdot\mu
, we get that the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C_{v})
is bounded by
O(N^{4\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{2}\cdot D^{*})
, and the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
is bounded by
O(N^{7\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{2}\cdot D^{*})
.

For the remainder of the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, it will be convenient for us to define a partitioning tree
{\mathcal{T}}
associated with the collection
{\mathcal{R}}
of basic clusters. The set of vertices of tree
{\mathcal{T}}
contains, for every cluster
R
that ever belonged to
{\mathcal{R}}
, a corresponding vertex
v(R)
.
Let
C^{0}
be the cluster that was added to
{\mathcal{R}}
first (so at the time when
C^{0}
was added to
{\mathcal{R}}
,
C^{0}=H
held).
The root of the tree is vertex
v(C^{0})
. consider now any cluster
R\neq C^{0}
that ever belonged to
{\mathcal{R}}
. Then there must be another cluster
R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R}}
, with
R\in{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R^{\prime})
. We then make vertex
v(R)
the child-vertex of
v(R^{\prime})
in the tree
R
, and we say that cluster
R
is a
child-cluster
of cluster
R^{\prime}
.
Note that, from Property
R5
, and from our definition of parameters
W_{1}(R^{\prime})=N^{0}(R^{\prime})
and
W_{2}(R^{\prime})=W_{1}(R)^{\prime}/N^{\epsilon}
, if cluster
R
is a child-cluster of cluster
R^{\prime}
, then
N^{0}(R)\leq N^{0}(R^{\prime})/N^{\epsilon}
. From our algorithm, if vertex
v(R)
is a leaf in the tree
{\mathcal{T}}
, then
N^{0}(R)<N^{3\epsilon}
. Since
N^{0}(C^{0})\leq N
, the height of the tree
{\mathcal{T}}
is bounded by
1/\epsilon
. For all
0\leq i\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, we denote by
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
the collection of all basic clusters
R
, such that the distance from vertex
v(R)
to vertex
v(C_{0})
in the tree
{\mathcal{T}}
is exactly
i
, so
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=\left\{C^{0}\right\}
. For
0\leq i\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, we may sometimes refer to the clusters of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
as
level-
i
clusters
. Consider now any cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, such that
v(R)
is not a leaf vertex of the tree
{\mathcal{T}}
. From Property
R6
, for every regular vertex
x
of
R
, the total number of clusters in
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R)
to which
x
ever belongs is bounded by
N^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}
. Therefore:

\sum_{R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R)}N^{0}(R^{\prime})\leq N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}.

We then get that, for all
0\leq i<\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
:

\sum_{R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{i+1}}N^{0}(R^{\prime})\leq N^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}\cdot\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{L}}_{i}}N^{0}(R).

Altogether, we get that:

\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{R}}}N^{0}(R)\leq N^{\epsilon^{\prime}/(2\epsilon)}N^{0}(H)\leq N^{1+\epsilon/2},

(7)

sine
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{2}
.

We are now ready to complete the description of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem. At the beginning of the algorithm, we let
{\mathcal{R}}=\left\{H\right\}
, and we apply Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(H)
to cluster
H
. The collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters that the algorithm maintains is
{\mathcal{C}}=\bigcup_{R\in{\mathcal{R}}}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
. Notice that, from the definition of the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem, for every cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, the clusters in set
{\mathcal{W}}(R)
only undergo allowed updates. Therefore, in order to show that all updates to clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}
are allowed updates, it is enough to show that, whenever a new cluster
C
is added to
{\mathcal{C}}
, there is some cluster
C^{\prime}
that is currently in
{\mathcal{C}}
, with
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
. Assume that some new cluster
C
was added to set
{\mathcal{C}}
. Then there must be some basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{C}}
, with
C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
. If
C\neq R
, then cluster
C
was created by splitting-off from some other cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
, so
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
hold. Assume now that
C=R
. Then cluster
R
was just added to the set
{\mathcal{R}}
of basic clusters, so there is another cluster
R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R}}
, such that
R
was just added to the set
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R^{\prime})
of secondary clusters. But then, from the definition of the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem, there is some primary cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R^{\prime})
, such that cluster
R\subseteq C^{\prime}
; in other words, cluster
R
was created by splitting it off from
C^{\prime}
. But then
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
currently holds, and
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
, as required.

Next, we define variables
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
for regular vertices of
H
. In order to do so, for every basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, we define a collection
T(R)
of regular vertices of
R
, that cluster
R
is “responsible” for covering. We will ensure that, for every regular vertex
v\in T(R)
, either there is some primary cluster
C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
with
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C
; or there is some secondary cluster
R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R)
with
v\in T(R^{\prime})
.

Recall that, at the beginning of the algorithm, we set
{\mathcal{R}}=\left\{H\right\}
. We then let
T(H)
contain all regular vertices of
H
. For every regular vertex
v
of
H
, we also set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=H
at this time. Assume now that some cluster
R
was added to set
{\mathcal{R}}
. Then at this time, we set, for every regular vertex
v\in T(R)
,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R
. Assume first that
R
is a large basic cluster. Consider now any fixed regular vertex
v\in T(R)
. Recall that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
maintains a cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{W}}(R)
, that we denote, for convenience, by
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{R}(v)
. As long as
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{R}(v)\in{\mathcal{W}}^{1}(R)
, we set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{R}(v)
. Consider now the first time
\tau
when
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{R}(v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R)
, and denote
R^{\prime}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{R}(v)
. Then, from Property
R4
, cluster
R^{\prime}
just jointed set
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(R)
, and hence it was just added to the set
{\mathcal{R}}
of basic clusters. Recall that, at this time, we set
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)=\left\{R\right\}
, and we add cluster
R
to set
{\mathcal{C}}
. We then add vertex
v
to set
T(R^{\prime})
, and set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R^{\prime}
. Assume now that cluster
R
is a small cluster, and let
v
be a regular vertex that lies in
T(R)
. Recall that set
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
of clusters contains a cluster
C_{v}
associated with vertex
v
. We set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C_{v}
, and it remains unchanged for the remainder of the algorithm.
We now argue that our algorithm obeys the Consistent Covering property.

Claim 5.10
.

The algorithm obeys the Consistent Covering property.

Proof.

Let
v
be any regular vertex of
H
, and assume that, at some time
\tau
,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C
held. From the definition of the set
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters, there must be a basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, with
C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
.

We denote by
(v(H)=v(R_{0}),v(R_{1}),\ldots,v(R_{q})=v(R))
the path connecting vertex
v(H)
to vertex
v(R)
in the tree
{\mathcal{T}}
. For all
0\leq i\leq q
, we let
\tau_{i}
be the time when cluster
R_{i}
was added to set
{\mathcal{R}}
. Since
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
holds at time
\tau
, it must be the case that, at time
\tau_{q}
,
v\in T(R)
and
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R
held. Moreover, for all
0\leq i<q
, at time
\tau_{i}
,
v\in T(R_{i})
, and
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R_{i}
held. Observe that, for all
0\leq i\leq q
,
\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau_{i})}(C)=R_{i}
.

Consider now some time
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
, and let
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
. Assume first that
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau_{q}
, so
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
holds. If
R
is a large cluster, then, since Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
probem ensures the Consistent Covering property, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{R}(v,D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
held. Moreover, since, at time
\tau_{q}
,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R
held, we get that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(R)
holds. Therefore, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq B_{R}(v,D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
. If
R
is a small cluster, then
C^{\prime}=C_{v}=R=C
holds at time
\tau_{q}
. As before, since, at time
\tau_{q}
,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R
held, we get that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)=B_{R}(v,D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
.

Assume now that
\tau^{\prime}<\tau_{q}
. Then there is an integer
0\leq i<q
, such that
\tau_{i}\leq\tau^{\prime}<\tau_{i+1}
. Note that, at time
\tau_{i+1}
,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R_{i+1}
held. From our algorithm, it is easy to verify that
\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(R_{i+1})=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)=C^{\prime}
. Since Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R_{i})
for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
probem ensures the Consistent Covering property, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{R_{i}}(v,D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
held. Moreover, since, at time
\tau_{i}
,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=R_{i}
held, we get that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(R_{i})
holds. Therefore, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq B_{R_{i}}(v,D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
.

Bounding the Number of Clusters a Vertex May Belong to

Consider any regular vertex
v\in V(H)
. We start by bounding the number of basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, for which
v\in V(R)
ever held. Recall that
v\in V(H)
held at the beginning of the algorithm. Moreover, from Property
R6
of the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem, if
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is a large cluster for which
v\in V(R)
held when cluster
R
was added to
{\mathcal{R}}
, then the total number of child-clusters
R^{\prime}
of
R
for which
v\in V(R)
ever held is bounded by
N^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}=N^{\epsilon^{2}/4}
. Therefore, for all
0\leq i\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, the total number of clusters
R\in{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
for which
v\in V(R)
ever held is bounded by
N^{i\epsilon^{2}/4}
. Since the height of the tree
{\mathcal{T}}
is bounded by
1/\epsilon
, the total number of clusters
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, for which
v\in V(R)
ever held is bounded by
N^{\epsilon}
. Consider now some basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, for which
v\in V(R)
ever held. If
R
is a large cluster, then, from Property
R1
:

|{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)|\leq\frac{(W_{1}(R))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}(R)}\leq N^{2\epsilon},

since
W_{1}(R)\leq N
, and
\frac{W_{1}(R)}{W_{2}(R)}=N^{\epsilon}
. If
R
is a small cluster, then
|{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)|=N^{0}(R)\leq N^{3\epsilon}
. In any case, if
v\in V(R)
for a basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, then
v
may belong to at most
N^{3\epsilon}
clusters of
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
. Clearly, if
v\not\in V(R)
, then it may not belong to any cluster of
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
. Overall, we get that, for a regular vertex
v
of
H
, the total number of clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}
to which
v
may ever belong is bounded by

N^{\epsilon}\cdot N^{3\epsilon}\leq N^{4\epsilon}
.
Next, we bound the total update time of the algorithm.

Bounding the Total Update Time

Recall that, for a single cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/4}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right),\end{split}

if
R
is a large cluster, and by
O(N^{7\epsilon})\cdot\mu^{2}\cdot D^{*})
if
R
is a small cluster.

From Inequality
7
,

\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{R}}}N^{0}(R)\leq N^{1+\epsilon/2}
, so in particular the total number of small clusters is bounded by
N^{1+\epsilon/2}
. Therefore, the total update time of all algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
for small clusters
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is bounded by
O(N^{1+8\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{2}\cdot D^{*})
, and the total update time of all algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
for large clusters
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is bounded by:

\begin{split}O\left(N\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/2}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}+N\cdot W^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right),\end{split}

The total update time of algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
for all
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is then bounded by:

\begin{split}O\left(N\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\log D)^{c}+N\cdot W^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot D^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right),\end{split}

(we have used the fact that
D^{*}\leq O\left(\frac{\log\log N}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}\cdot D\right)\leq O\left(\frac{\log\log N}{\epsilon^{8}}\cdot D\right)\leq O(W^{\epsilon}\cdot D)
, since
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
).

It is easy to verify that the total time that is required in order to maintain variables
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
for regular vertices
v
of
H
is asymptotically bounded by the above time. The time that is required to maintain the lists
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}(x)
for vertices
x\in V(H)
and
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}(e)
for edges
e\in E(H)
is asymptotically bounded by the time required to maintain the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
. Overall, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}O\left(N\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\log D)^{c}+N\cdot W^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot D^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta\right),\end{split}

It now remains to provide an algorithm to respond to
short-path-query
queries.

Responding to Queries.

We now provide an algorithm for responding to query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,x,y)
, where
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
is a cluster, and
x,y
is a pair of regular vertices of
C
. Since
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, there is some basic cluster
R
with
C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
. If
R
is a small cluster, then
C=C_{z}
holds for some regular vertex
z
of
R
, and, from the definition of cluster
C_{z}
, both
x
and
y
currently lie in the
ES-Tree
that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C_{z})
maintains. The
ES-Tree
is rooted at vertex
z
, and has depth
D^{*}
. We perform
SSSP-query
query in this data structure, to obtain a path
P_{1}
connecting
x
to
z
in
C
, whose length is at most
D^{*}
, in time
O(|E(P_{1})|)
. Similarly, we obtain a path
P_{2}
connecting
y
to
z
in
C
, whose length is at most
D^{*}
, in time
O(|E(P_{1})|)
. By concatenating the two paths, we obtain a path
P
in cluster
C
, that connects
x
to
y
, and has length at most
2D^{*}\leq\frac{2^{32}\log\log N}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}\cdot D\leq\frac{2^{32}\log\log N}{\epsilon^{8}}\cdot D\leq\alpha^{*}\cdot D
.

Assume now that
R
is a large cluster, so
C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
holds. Recall that we used Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(C)
for solving the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem in graph
C
. We perform query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,x,y)
in the corresponding data structure, and obtain a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in graph
C
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
, such that the length of the path is bounded by:

\begin{split}\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})},(8\alpha(W))^{2}\right\}\cdot D^{*}&\leq\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})},(8\alpha(W))^{2}\right\}\cdot\frac{2^{31}\log\log N}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}\cdot D\\
&\leq\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})},(8\alpha(W))^{2}\right\}\cdot\frac{2^{31}\log\log N}{\epsilon^{8}}\cdot D\\
&\leq\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log\log N,\frac{(\alpha(W))^{2}\cdot\log\log N}{\epsilon^{16}}\right\}\cdot D\\
&\leq\alpha^{*}\cdot D.\end{split}

5.4
Completing the Proof of
Theorem

5.3

The proof of
Theorem

5.3
is essentially identical to that of
Theorem

5.2
, with one difference: for every large basic cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, for every primary cluster
C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)
, we apply the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem from
Theorem

5.7
to it, instead of the algorithm from
Theorem

5.8
. Since we have assumed that
\mu\geq N^{1/10}
, we get that
\mu\geq(N^{0}(C))^{1/10}
, and so the total update time of the algorithm from
Theorem

5.7
on cluster
C
is bounded by
O(N^{0}(C)\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D^{*})
.

As before, the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(R)
remains bounded by:

O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon}\right).

As before, from Property
R2
,
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)}N^{0}(C)\leq N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon^{\prime}}
. Altogether, the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
is bounded by:

\begin{split}O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon}\right)+O\left(\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(R)}N^{0}(C)\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D^{*}\right)\leq O\left(N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D^{*}\right).\end{split}

The total update time of all algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}(R)
for small clusters
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
remains bounded by
O(N^{1+8\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{2}\cdot D^{*})
. Since, from Inequality

7
,

\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{R}}}N^{0}(R)\leq N^{1+\epsilon/2}
, we get that the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O(N^{1+8\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{2}\cdot D^{*})+O\left(\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{R}}}N^{0}(R)\cdot N^{2\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D^{*}\right)\leq O\left(N^{1+8\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D^{*}\right)\leq O\left(N^{1+9\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{22}\cdot D\right),

since
D^{*}\leq O(\hat{W}^{\epsilon}D)
.

Since the approximation factor of the algorithm from
Theorem

5.7
is
1
, it is easy to see that our algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem achieves approximation factor at most
\frac{2D^{*}}{D}\leq\frac{2^{32}\log\log N}{\epsilon^{8}}\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\log\log N
.

6
Algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
Problem – Proof of
Theorem

5.9

In this section we prove
Theorem

5.9
by providing an algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem. Recall that we are given as input
a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E},D\right)
, where graph
H
undergoes an online sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations with some given dynamic degree bound
\mu
. Additionally, we are given parameters:
W_{1}
,
W_{2}
,
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<1
, and
\gamma=\frac{2^{28}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}
. We are guaranteed that
W_{2}\leq W_{1}\leq W_{2}^{1.5}
, and that, if
N^{0}(H)
denotes the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then
N^{0}(H)\leq W_{1}
holds.
We denote by
{\mathcal{T}}
the time horizon associated with
{\mathcal{J}}
and
\Sigma
, and we will use two additional distance parameters:
D^{\prime}=\frac{2^{14}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}\cdot D
and
D^{*}=\frac{2^{14}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}D^{\prime}=\frac{2^{28}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{4}}D=\gamma\cdot D
.

Our algorithm will maintain a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters, that is partitioned into two subsets: set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of primary clusters, and set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
of secondary clusters. Whenever a new cluster
C
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}
, it immediately joins either
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
or
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, and it remains in that set until the end of the algorithm.

The algorithm will maintain a collection
T
of regular vertices of
H
, that we refer to as
terminals
, and a vertex-induced subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
H
. Intuitively, a regular vertex
v
lies in
T
if
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we let
T
contain all regular vertices, and, as the algorithm progresses, vertices may leave the set
T
but they may never join it.
For every regular vertex
v
of
H
, we will maintain a cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}
, such that Consistent Covering property holds. We will ensure that, throughout the algorithm, properties
R1
–
R6
hold.
Additionally, we will ensure that the following invariants always hold.

I1.

for every regular vertex
v
, if
v\not\in T
, then
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
;

I2.

for every regular vertex
v\in T
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(H^{\prime})
;

I3.

for every regular vertex
v\in T
, there is some primary cluster
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, such that
B_{H^{\prime}}(v,D^{\prime})\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
.

For every terminal
v\in T
, our algorithm will maintain a cluster
\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, with
B_{H^{\prime}}(v,D^{\prime})\subseteq V(\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(v))
. It also ensures that
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(v)
for all
v\in T
, and that the following Extended Consistent Covering property holds:

I4.

for every time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, if
t\in T
and
C=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t)
at time
\tau
, and, for some
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
,
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
, then, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
C^{\prime}=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t)
held.

For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, we maintain a list
\lambda(C)\subseteq T
of terminals, that contains every terminal
t\in T
with
\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t)=C
.

Throughout, we denote by
V
the set of all regular vertices that lie in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, and we let
N=|V|
.
For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we denote by
N(C)
the number of regular vertices that currently lie in
C
, and by
N^{0}(C)
the number of regular vertices that lied in
C
when the cluster was first added to
{\mathcal{C}}
.

For every regular vertex
v\in V
, we maintain a counter
n_{v}
, whose value is the number of clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\cup\left\{H^{\prime}\right\}
, with
v\in V(C)
. These counters will be used in order to ensure that every regular vertex of
V
only lies in a small number of clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, using techniques that are almost identical to those employed in the proof of
Theorem

2.3
. The main difference is that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.3
is only applied once to a static graph
G
, while our algorithm will gradually cut clusters off from graph
H^{\prime}
and add them to
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, as graph
H
undergoes a sequence of valid update operations.

We use parameters
\hat{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{\prime}/32
and
r=\left\lceil 1/\hat{\epsilon}\right\rceil+1
.
As in the proof of
Theorem

2.3
, we maintain a partition of the set
V
of vertices into
r+1
classes
(S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{r})
, that are defined as follows. A regular vertex
v\in V
belongs to class
S_{0}
if
n_{v}\leq N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}
. For
1\leq j<r
, vertex
v
belongs to class
S_{j}
if
j\cdot N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}<n_{v}\leq(j+1)\cdot N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}
. If
n_{v}>r\cdot N^{2\hat{\epsilon}^{\prime}}
, then vertex
v
belongs to class
S_{r}
. As in the proof of
Theorem

2.3
, we will ensure that set
S_{r}
remains empty throughout the algorithm. This will allow us to ensure requirement
R6
.

In order to ensure that Requirements
R1
and
R2
are satisfied, we will employ
cluster budgets
, associated with clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. We will assign, to every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, a budget
\beta(C)
. When cluster
C
is first added to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, we set the budget
\beta(C)=(N^{0}(C))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. As the algorithm progresses, we may sometimes decrease the budget
\beta(C)
, but we will ensure that
\beta(C)\geq W_{2}
and
\beta(C)\geq N(C)
always holds. We then denote by
\beta=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}\beta(C)
. We will ensure that, at the beginning of the algorithm,
\beta\leq W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
holds, and that the total budget
\beta
does not increase over the course of the algorithm. This will ensure that, on the one hand,
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}N(C)\leq\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}\beta(C)\leq W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
always holds, while, on the other hand,
|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|\leq\frac{\beta}{W_{2}}\leq\frac{W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}
. The algorithm will ensure the following invariant:

I5.

For every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
,
\beta(C)\geq W_{2}
and
\beta(C)\geq(N(C))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
holds at all times. Throughout the algorithm, the total budget
\beta=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}\beta(C)
does not grow.

At the beginning of the algorithm, we set
H^{\prime}=H
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}=\left\{H\right\}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}=\emptyset
. We set the budget of cluster
H
to be
\beta(H)=(N^{0}(H))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. We also let
T
contain all regular vertices of
H
. For every regular vertex
v
, we let
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=H
, and
\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(v)=H
. We also initialize the list
\lambda(H)
to contain all vertices of
T
, and, for every regular vertex
v\in V
, we set
n_{v}=1
. It is easy to verify that all invariants hold at the beginning of the algorithm. We also set
S_{0}=V
and
S_{1}=S_{2}=\cdots=S_{r}=\emptyset
.

As the algorithm progresses, and graph
H
undergoes valid update operations, the clusters in set
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
are updated accordingly, as described in
Section

3.2.2
. The only additional changes to the clusters in set
{\mathcal{C}}
, and to variables in
\left\{\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)\right\}_{v\in V}
and
\left\{\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t)\right\}_{t\in T}
will be done during Flag Lowering operations. Budgets of clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
may also only be updated during Flag Lowering operations.
Therefore, if
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}
is any time interval during which no Flag Lowering operations are performed, and all invariants held at the beginning of
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
, then, from
3.7
, all invariants continue to hold at the end of
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
.
It now remains to describe an algorithm for a Flag Lowering Operation.

6.1
Flag-Lowering Operation

We assume that we are given a collection
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
of clusters, for which all invariants hold. We also assume that we are given a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, and a pair
v,v^{\prime}
of regular vertices that lie in
C
, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(v,v^{\prime})>D^{*}
. Our goal is to produce a valid flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
, which is then applied to cluster
C
, possibly interspersed with cluster-splitting updates, in which new clusters
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
are created. Our goal is to ensure that, at the end of the Flag-Lowering Operation, all invariants will continue to hold. The algorithm consists of two phases.
In Phase 1, we will either compute two terminals
t,t^{\prime}\in\lambda(C)
, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime})\geq D^{*}/2
, and each of
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime}),B_{C}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
contains at least
W_{2}
regular vertices (in which case we say that the phase is unsuccessful); or we will compute a flag lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
, that will be applied to cluster
C
, possibly interspersed with cluster-splitting updates (in which case we say that the phase is successful).
All clusters that are created during Phase 1 are added to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. If Phase 1 is successful, then we do not continue to Phase 2. Otherwise, during Phase 2, we will create a new cluster
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
, that is added to the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of primary clusters, and we will delete some edges and vertices from
C
, including at least one of the terminals
t,t^{\prime}
. We now describe each of the phases in turn.

6.1.1
Phase 1: Reaching Terminals

In Phase 1, we will either compute two terminals
t,t^{\prime}\in\lambda(C)
with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime})\geq D^{*}/2
, such that both
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})
and
B_{C}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
contain at least
W_{2}
regular vertices, or we will delete some vertices and edges from cluster
C
(the deletions may be interspersed with cluster-splitting updates), producing a valid flag-lowering update sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The main challenge is to execute this procedure efficiently, so that, if no pair of terminals with the above properties are found, then the running time of the algorithm can be charged to the edges that are deleted from
C
.

Assume first that
v
is an isolated vertex in
C
. If
v\not\in\lambda(C)
, then we simply delete
v
from
C
; it is easy to verify that all invariants continue to hold, since
C\neq\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(v)
. Assume now that
v\in\lambda(C)
, so
C=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(v)
. In this case,
B_{H^{\prime}}(v,D^{\prime})\subseteq V(C)
must hold, and, from Invariant
I2
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(H^{\prime})
. Since
H^{\prime}
is a vertex-induced subgraph of
H
, it then follows that
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(C)
. We create a new cluster
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
that only contains the vertex
v
, by spliting the cluster off from
C
. Cluster
C^{\prime}
is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, and vertex
v
is deleted from the set
T
of terminals. We set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C
. The flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
then only consists of a single operation: deletion of vertex
v
from
C
. Once vertex
v
is deleted from
C
, the Flag-Lowering Operation terminates. It is easy to verify that all invariants continue to hold. If vertex
v^{\prime}
is an isolated vertex in
C
, then we proceed in exactly the same way. Therefore, we assume from now on that vertices
v
and
v^{\prime}
are not isolated in
C
.

Our algorithm for Phase 1 will execute two procedures in parallel: procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
, that processes vertex
v
, and procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
, that processes vertex
v^{\prime}
. The two procedures are executed in parallel in order to ensure the efficiency of the algorithm: we will consider the procedure that terminates first, and we may choose to not complete the other procedure.
Each one of the two procedures may delete edges and vertices from
C
, and may create new clusters by splitting them off from
C
. However, we will ensure that all edges and vertices of
C
that procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
deletes lie in the subgraph of
C
induced by
B_{C}(v,D^{*}/3)
, and similarly, all edges and vertices of
C
that procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
deletes lie in the subgraph of
C
induced by
B_{C}(v^{\prime},D^{*}/3)
. Additionally, when procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
creates a new cluster
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
by splitting it off from
C
, then
V(C^{\prime})\subseteq B_{C}(v,D^{*}/3)
holds, and similarly, when procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
creates a new cluster
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
by splitting it off from
C
, then
V(C^{\prime})\subseteq B_{C}(v^{\prime},D^{*}/3)
holds. In fact Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
will perform a number of BFS searches from vertex
v
and its nearby vertices, and it will never explore any vertices that lie outside

B_{C}(v,D^{*}/3)
. Similarly, procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
will only explore vertices of
B_{C}(v^{\prime},D^{*}/3)
. Therefore, the two procedures can be executed in parallel and independently, since they do not interfere with each other in any way. We focus on describing procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
; the description of procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is identical, except that vertex
v
is replaced with
v^{\prime}
.

Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}

We start by applying the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
to graph
C
and vertex
v
, with distance parameter
D^{\prime}
, and parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}
remaining unchanged (we do not supply the sets
T_{1},\ldots T_{k}
of vertices, so
k=0
). Denote by
1<i\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
the integer that the algorithm returned, and denote
S_{v}=B_{C}(v,2(i-1)D^{\prime})
, and
S^{\prime}_{v}=B_{C}(v,2iD^{\prime})
. Recall that the running time of the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
is bounded by
O(|E_{C}(S_{v})|\cdot|E(C)|^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)\leq O(|E_{C}(S_{v})|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
. We then check whether
S^{\prime}_{v}\cap\lambda(C)=\emptyset
holds. If this is the case, then we say that Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
was successful. Note that, in this case, for every terminal
t\in\lambda(C)
,
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})\cap S_{v}=\emptyset
. Therefore, we can delete all edges incident to the vertices of
S_{v}
, and all vertices of
S_{v}
from cluster
C
, without violating any invariants.
Since
i\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, and
S^{\prime}_{v}=B_{C}(v,2iD^{\prime})
, while
D^{*}>\frac{64D^{\prime}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, all edges and vertices that we delete lie in
B_{C}(v,D^{*}/4)
. Moreover, the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
essentially performs a BFS from vertex
v
in cluster
C
up to depth
2iD^{\prime}
, so it does not explore any vertices of
C
that lie outside
S^{\prime}_{v}
. We then terminate Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
.

From now on we assume that
S^{\prime}_{v}\cap\lambda(C)\neq\emptyset
holds, and we denote
T_{1}=S^{\prime}_{v}\cap\lambda(C)
. We now perform iterations, as long as
T_{1}\neq\emptyset
.

Iteration Description.

We now describe a single iteration. We let
t\in T_{1}
be any terminal. We apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
to the current cluster
C
, vertex
t
, distance parameter
2D
, and precision parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}
remaining unchanged. We also set
k=r+1
, and we use sets
S_{0}\cap V(C),S_{1}\cap V(C),\ldots,S_{r}\cap V(C)
instead of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
.

Let
1<i_{t}\leq\frac{2r+4}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
be the integer that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
returned. We also let
B_{t}=B_{C}(t,4(i_{t}-1)D)
and
B^{\prime}_{t}=B_{C}(t,4i_{t}D)
. Since
i_{t}\leq\frac{2r+4}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
,
r=\left\lceil\frac{1}{\hat{\epsilon}}\right\rceil+1\leq\frac{4}{\hat{\epsilon}}
, and

\hat{\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{32}
, we get that
4i_{t}D\leq\frac{16r}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot D\leq\frac{2^{11}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}\cdot D
. Since
D^{\prime}=\frac{2^{14}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}\cdot D
and
D^{*}>4D^{\prime}
, we get that
B^{\prime}_{t}\subseteq B_{C}(t,D^{\prime}/2)\subseteq B_{C}(t,D^{*}/8)
. Note that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
performs a BFS from vertex
t
in cluster
C
up to distance
2i_{t}D
, so all vertices of
C
that it encounters are contained in
B_{C}(t,D^{*}/8)
, as required.
Recall that the algorithm guarantees that,
for all
0\leq j\leq r
,
|S_{j}\cap B^{\prime}_{t}|\leq|S_{j}\cap B_{t}|\cdot N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

We now consider two cases. The first case happens if the number of regular vertices in
B^{\prime}_{t}
is at least
W_{2}
. In this case, we say that Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
was
unsuccessful
, and we return terminal
t
. We also say that the current iteration was a
type-1
iteration. Notice that we are guaranteed that
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})
contains at least
W_{2}
regular vertices.

Assume now that the number of regular vertices in set
B^{\prime}_{t}
is less than
W_{2}
. We then say that the current iteration is a
type-2
iteration.
Since
t\in\lambda(C)
,
\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t)=C
must hold. Therefore, from Invariant
I3
,
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})\subseteq V(C)
. Since
C
is a vertex-induced subgraph of
H^{\prime}
, we get that
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})=B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})
. We use the following simple observation.

Observation 6.1
.

Let
t^{\prime}
be any terminal in
T
. If
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})\leq 4i_{t}D-2D
, then
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)\subseteq B^{\prime}_{t}
. Otherwise,
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)\cap B_{t}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
t^{\prime}\in T
be any terminal. Assume fist that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})\leq 4i_{t}D-2D
. From Invariant
I2
,
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)\subseteq H^{\prime}
holds, and, since
H^{\prime}
is a vertex-induced subgraph of
H
,
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)=B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},D)
. It is also easy to see that
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},D)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(t,4i_{t}D)=B_{C}(t,4i_{t}D)=B^{\prime}_{t}
.

Assume now that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})>4i_{t}D-2D
. Then for every vertex
y\in B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)=B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},D)
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}}(t,y)\geq 4i_{t}D-3D>4(i_{t}-1)D
holds. Therefore, if
y\in V(C)
, then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,y)>4(i_{t}-1)D
, and in any case
y\not\in B_{t}
. We conclude that
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)\cap B_{t}=\emptyset
.

Note that, for a terminal
t^{\prime}\in T
, if
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})\leq 4i_{t}D-2D
then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime})\leq 4i_{t}D-2D
must hold and vice versa.

Let
C^{\prime}
be the subgraph of
C
induced by the set
B^{\prime}_{t}
of vertices. We apply the cluster splitting operation to cluster
C
, creating a new cluster
C^{\prime}
, that is added to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. Recall that the number of regular vertices in
C^{\prime}
is at most
W_{2}
, as required.
Next, we consider every terminal
t^{\prime}\in T\cap B_{C}(t,4i_{t}D-2D)
. For each such terminal
t^{\prime}
, we set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t^{\prime})=C^{\prime}
, and we delete
t^{\prime}
from the set
T
of terminals, and from set
T_{1}
if
t^{\prime}\in T_{1}
holds. From
6.1
, for each such terminal
t^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
indeed holds. From this point onward,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t^{\prime})
will not change, and it will remain equal to
C^{\prime}
for the remainder of the algorithm. We claim that this the assignment of
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t^{\prime})
obeys the Consistent Covering property over the course of the entire algorithm. Indeed, let
\tau
be the time when cluster
C^{\prime}
is created. Until time
\tau
, Consistent Covering property for terminal
t^{\prime}
followed from Invariant
I4
, as
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t^{\prime})=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t^{\prime})
always held. Consider now time
\tau
, when
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(C)
was set to
C^{\prime}
. Note that, at time
\tau
,
C=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t)
and
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})<D^{\prime}/2
holds. Consider now any time
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
, and let
\hat{C}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
. From Invariant
I4
, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
\hat{C}=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t)
held, and so, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})\subseteq V(\hat{C})
held. Since, at time
\tau
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})\leq D^{\prime}/2
, and since distances in
H
may not shrink as the result of valid update operations, we get that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})\leq D^{\prime}/2
also held, and so, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)\subseteq B_{H}(t,D^{\prime})
. We conclude that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(t^{\prime},D)\subseteq V(\hat{C})
holds. Therefore, for every terminal
t^{\prime}
that we deleted from
T
during the current iteration, we are guaranteed that the Consistent Covering property holds for
t^{\prime}
over the course of the entire algorithm.

Lastly, we delete all edges incident to the vertices of
B_{t}
from cluster
C
, and then delete the vertices of
B_{t}
from
C
. These deletions become part of the flag lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
that we construct.
We also delete the vertices of
B_{t}
from graph
H^{\prime}
.

Notice that the regular vertices of
B_{t}
now belong to cluster
C^{\prime}
, and they were deleted from graph
H^{\prime}
. Therefore, for a regular vertex
x\in B_{t}
, the counter
n_{x}
does not need to be updated. However, if a regular vertex
x
lies in set
B^{\prime}_{t}\setminus B_{t}
, then
x
now lies in cluster
C^{\prime}
, and it continues to lie in graph
H^{\prime}
. We then say that a
new copy of vertex
x
was created – the copy that lies in cluster
C^{\prime}
. For each regular vertex
x\in B^{\prime}_{t}\setminus B_{t}
, we increase the value of the counter
n_{x}
, and, if needed, we update the set
S_{j}
, for
0\leq j\leq r
, to which the vertex belongs.

We now show that all invariants continue to hold at the end of the iteration. Invariants
I1
and
I2
continue to hold from
6.1
. It is immediate to verify that Invariant
I3
continues to hold as well, since the changes to graph
H^{\prime}
and cluster
C
over the course of the current iteration were identical. Invariant
I4
continues to hold, since we did not change clusters
\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t^{\prime\prime})
for any terminal that remains in
T
at the end of the iteration, and Invariant
I5
continues to hold, since we did not make any changes to cluster budgets.

This completes the description of the iteration.
If the current iteration was a type-1 iteration, then its running time can be bounded by
O(|E(C)|\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))\leq O(W_{1}\cdot\mu\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
. Otherwise, if we let
E^{\prime}=E_{C}(B_{t})
be the collection of edges that were deleted from cluster
C
over the course of the iteration, then, from
Lemma

2.1
, the running time of the iteration is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot|E(C)|^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))\leq O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
.

Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
terminates either after a type-1 iteration, when a terminal
t
with
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
containing at least
W_{2}
regular vertices is found (in which case we say that it is unsuccessful), or once
T_{1}=\emptyset
holds (in which case we say that it is successful). In the latter case, we delete all edges that are incident to the vertices of
S_{v}
from cluster
C
, and we delete all vertices of
S_{v}
from cluster
C
. These deletions become a part of the flag-lowering update sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
. We will charge the running time of the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
, when it was used by Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
for the first time, to the edges incident to vertices of
S_{v}
that we just deleted from
C
. Since all terminals from
T_{1}
have been deleted from the set
T
of terminals, for each remaining terminal
t^{\prime}\in\lambda(C)
, we are guaranteed that
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is disjoint from
S_{v}
, so we are guaranteed that
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})\subseteq V(C)
continues to hold. It is immediate to verify that all invariants continue to hold.

We now bound the running time of Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of all edges that were deleted over the course of the procedure from cluster
C
. If Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
was successful, then its total running time is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
. Otherwise, the running time of the first application of the algorithm from

Lemma

2.1
and of the last iteration can be bounded by
O(|E(C)|\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))\leq O(W_{1}\mu\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
, while the running time of the remainder of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
. Therefore, if Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
is unsuccessful, its running time is bounded by
O((W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
.

This concludes the description of Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is similar, except that we replace vertex
v
with vertex
v^{\prime}
.

Completing the Description of Phase 1

We execute Procedures
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
in parallel. Notice that the execution of each procedure can be partitioned into iterations. In every iteration, we first invoke the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
, that performs a weighted BFS from some given vertex. After that, we execute a clean-up step, during which some vertices and edges may be deleted from
C
or from
H^{\prime}
, and a new cluster may be created by performing cluster-splitting of cluster
C
. The running time of the lean-up step is asymptotically bounded by the running time of the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
. We assume w.l.o.g. that Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
terminates before Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
does, and we denote by
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})
the running time of Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. If, at the time when Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
terminates, procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
performs a clean-up step, then we allow it to complete the clean-up step, and then halt it (possibly temporarily). The running time of both procedures so far is bounded by
O({\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{A}}_{1}))
.

Assume first that Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
was successful. In this case, by the end of the procedure, vertex
v
is deleted from cluster
C
. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
denote the sequence of all edge-deletions and isolated vertex-deletions that procedures
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
applied to cluster
C
, and let
E^{\prime}
be the set of all edges that both procedures deleted so far from
C
. Then
\Sigma^{\prime}
is valid flag lowering sequence for
C
. We say Phase 1 was succecssful, and we terminate the Flag Lowering operation. Notice that the running time of Phase 1 is bounded by

O({\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{A}}_{1}))\leq O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
in this case.

Assume now that Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
was unsuccessful, and let
t
be the terminal that the procedure returned. Then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(v,t)\leq D^{*}/4
, and
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})
contains at least
W_{2}
regular vertices. In this case, we resume the execution of Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
, until it terminates. We denote by
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})
the running time of Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
.
We consider again two cases.

The first case happens if Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
was successful. In this case, by the end of the procedure, vertex
v^{\prime}
is deleted from cluster
C
. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
denote the sequence of all edge-deletions and isolated vertex-deletions that procedures
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
applied to cluster
C
, and let
E^{\prime}
be the set of all edges that both procedures deleted from
C
. Then
\Sigma^{\prime}
is valid flag lowering sequence for
C
. We say Phase 1 was successful, and we terminate the Flag Lowering operation. Notice that the running time of Phase 1 is bounded by

O({\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{A}}_{2}))\leq O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
in this case.

Lastly, we consider the second case, where Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
was unsuccessful. In this case, we say that Phase 1 was unsuccessful, and we let
t^{\prime}
be the terminal that Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
returned. We have now obtained two terminals
t,t^{\prime}
, such that both
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})
and
B_{C}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
contain at least
W_{2}
regular vertices each. Moreover, since, at the beginning of the algorithm,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(v,t)\leq D^{*}/4
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(v^{\prime},t^{\prime})\leq D^{*}/4
, and
B_{C}(v,v^{\prime})\geq D^{*}
held, we get that at the beginning of the algorithm,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime})\geq D^{*}/2
held. Since edge and vertex deletions from
C
may not decrease distances between vertices, we are guaranteed that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime})\geq D^{*}/2
currently holds. In this case, we will continue to Phase 2. Note that the running time of Phase 1 in this case is bounded by
O({\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{A}}_{2}))\leq O((W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
. We also let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the sequence of all edge-deletions and isolated vertex-deletions that Procedures
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
performed over the course of Phase 1. While
\Sigma^{\prime}
may not be a valid flag-lowering sequence (as it is possible that it did not delete either
v
or
v^{\prime}
from
C
), we will extend it in the remainder of the algorithm, to ensure that it becomes a valid flag-lowering sequence.

We note that budgets of clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
were not updated over the course of Phase 1, so Invariant
I5
continues to hold at the end of the phase. We have already established that Invariants
I1
–
I4
continue to hold over the course of the phase, and that, for every vertex
x
that is deleted from the set
T
of the terminals during the current phase, the Consistent Covering property holds over the course of the entire algorithm.
Let
E^{\prime}
denote the collection of edges that were deleted over the course of Phase 1 from cluster
C
. If Phase 1 is successful, then its running time is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
, and if the phase was unsuccessful, then its running time is bounded by
O((W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
.

6.1.2
Phase 2: Separating Terminals

The input to Phase 2 is a pair
t,t^{\prime}\in\lambda(C)
of terminals, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime})\geq D^{*}/2
, and each of
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})
and
B_{C}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
contains at least
W_{2}
regular vertices. Over the course of Phase 2, we will construct a new cluster
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
that will be added to the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of primary clusters and the set
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters that our algorithm maintains, by applying a cluster-splitting operation to cluster
C
. We will also delete some edges and vertices from cluster
C
, and we will update budget
\beta(C)
and define budget
\beta(C^{\prime})
, so that the total budget
\beta
does not grow, and Invariant
I5
is satisfied. Over the course of Phase 2, we will execute two procedures: procedure
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{1}
, which runs a BFS in cluster
C
sarting from vertex
t
, and procedure
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{2}
, that runs a BFS in
C
starting from
t^{\prime}
. We now describe procedure
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{1}
; procedure
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{2}
is identical, except that we replace terminal
t
with
t^{\prime}
.

Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{\prime}

Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{\prime}
will perform a weighted BFS from vertex
t
in graph
C
, up to a certain depth that will be determined during the procedure. We will again use the ball-growing technique, but in a manner that is slightly different from that in
Lemma

2.1
. As in the proof of
Lemma

2.1
, we define
layers
of the BFS, where for all
i>0
, layer
L_{i}
is defined as follows:

L_{i}=B_{C}(v,2iD^{\prime})\setminus B_{C}(v,2(i-1)D^{\prime}).

Let
N^{\prime}
denote the number of regular vertices currently in cluster
C
. For all
i>0
, let
N_{i}
be the number of regular vertices that lie in

L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{i}
, and let
\eta_{i}=\frac{N^{\prime}}{N_{i}}
. Clearly,
N_{1}\leq N_{2}\leq\cdots
, and
\eta_{1}\geq\eta_{2}\geq\cdots
.
We now define the notion of eligible layer, which is somewhat different from that in the proof of
Lemma

2.1
.

Definition 6.2
(Eligible Layer).

Let
i>1
be an integer. We say that layer
L_{i}
of the BFS is
eligible
, if one of the following condition holds:

•

either
N_{i}\leq\frac{N^{\prime}}{4}
and
N_{i}\leq N_{i-1}\cdot\eta_{i-1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}
; or

•

\frac{N^{\prime}}{4}\leq N_{i}\leq\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
, and
N_{i}\leq N_{i-1}\cdot\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)
.

We use the following simple observation, that follows from standard arguments.

Observation 6.3
.

If
B_{C}(t,D^{*}/4)
contains at most
\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
regular vertices, then there is an integer
1<i\leq\frac{2^{10}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}
, such that layer
L_{i}
is eligible.

Proof.

Denote
z=\frac{2^{10}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}
. Recall that
D^{*}=\frac{2^{14}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}D^{\prime}
. Since we have assumed that
B_{C}(t,D^{*}/4)
contains at most
\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
regular vertices, we get that
N_{z}\leq\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
.
Assume for contradiction that the claim is false, so none of the layers
L_{2},L_{3},\ldots,L_{z}
is eligible.

Let
i^{*}
be the smallest integer, for which
N_{i^{*}}\geq\frac{N^{\prime}}{4}
holds. Then for all
i^{*}<i\leq z
,
\frac{N^{\prime}}{4}\leq N_{i}\leq\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
holds, and
N_{i}>N_{i-1}\cdot\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)\geq N_{i-1}+\frac{N^{\prime}\cdot(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{64}
. We claim that
i^{*}\geq z-\frac{64}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}
must hold. Indeed, otherwise, if we let
i^{**}=i^{*}+\frac{64}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}
, then
i^{**}\leq z
and
N_{i^{**}}>N^{\prime}
must hold, a contradiction. We conclude that
i^{*}\geq z-\frac{64}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}\geq\frac{z}{2}
.

Next, we will designate some layers
L_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq i^{*}
as
special
layers. Instead of defining special layers explicitly, we describe an algorithm that computes special layers. The first special layer is layer
L_{1}
. Assume now that the largest index of a special layer that we have defined so far is
i
. We let
i^{\prime}
be the smallest integer, such that
i<i^{\prime}<i^{*}
, and
\eta_{i^{\prime}}\leq\sqrt{\eta_{i}}
. If no such integer exists, then we terminate the algorithm for computing special layers. Otherwise, we designate
L_{i^{\prime}}
as a special layer and continue the algorithm.

Let
1=i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{q}<i^{*}
denote the indices of special layers.
Recall that
B_{C}(t,D^{\prime})
contains at least
W_{2}
regular vertices, so
N_{1}\geq W_{2}
, and
\eta_{i_{1}}=\eta_{1}\leq\frac{N^{\prime}}{W_{2}}
. Since
i_{q}<i^{*}
, we get that
N_{i_{q}}<\frac{N^{\prime}}{4}
, and so
\eta_{i_{q}}>4
. From the definition of special layers, for all
1<j\leq q
,
\eta_{i_{j}+1}\leq\sqrt{\eta_{i_{j}}}
. It then follows that
q\leq\log\log(N^{\prime}/W_{2})\leq\log\log W_{1}
.

Next, we consider a consecutive pair of special layers, that we denote by
L_{i}
and
L_{i^{\prime}}
, with
i^{\prime}>i
. We claim that
i^{\prime}-i\leq 16/\epsilon^{\prime}
. Indeed, consider any index
i<i^{\prime\prime}<i^{\prime}
. Since we have assumed that layer
L_{i^{\prime\prime}}
is not eligible, we get that:

N_{i^{\prime\prime}}>N_{i^{\prime\prime}-1}\cdot\eta_{i^{\prime\prime}-1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}\geq N_{i}\cdot\eta_{i}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/8}.

Therefore,
N_{i^{\prime\prime}}>N_{i}\cdot\eta_{i}^{(i^{\prime\prime}-i)\epsilon^{\prime}/8}
. If
i^{\prime}>16/\epsilon^{\prime}
, then
N_{i^{\prime}}>N_{i}\cdot\eta_{i}=N^{\prime}
, which is impossible.
Using a similar reasoning,
i^{*}-i_{q}\leq 16/\epsilon^{\prime}
