must hold. Overall, we get that the number of special layers is
q\leq\log\log W_{1}
, and the number of layers lying between any pair of special layers is bounded by
16/\epsilon^{\prime}
. Similarly,
i^{*}\leq i_{q}+16/\epsilon^{\prime}
. We conclude that
i^{*}\leq\frac{32q}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\frac{32\log\log W_{1}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}<\frac{z}{2}
, a contradiction.

Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{1}
simply performs a weighted BFS in graph
C
starting from vertex
t
, until it encounters the first layer
L_{i}
, such that either
N_{i}>\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
, or layer
L_{i}
is eligible. Clearly, the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E(C)|\log(W_{1}\mu)\leq O(W_{1}\mu\log(W_{1}\mu))
. Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is identical to
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
, except that we run the BFS starting from vertex
t^{\prime}
instead of
t
.

Completing the Description of Phase 2

The algorithm for Phase 2 executes procedures
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
one after another. Recall that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime})\geq D^{*}/2
, and that
D^{*}=\frac{2^{14}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}D^{\prime}
. From
6.3
, we are then guaranteed that at least one of the two procedures must terminate with an integer
1<i\leq\frac{2^{10}\log\log W_{1}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}
, such that layer
L_{i}
is eligible for the corresponding BFS procedure. We assume w.l.o.g. that it is
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
, and we denote by
i
the index of the eligible layer that Procedure
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
computed. Let
C^{\prime}
be the subgraph of
C
induced by the vertices of
L_{1}\cup\cdots L_{i}
. Notice that the number of regular vertices in
C^{\prime}
is
N(C^{\prime})=N_{i}
. We add cluster
C^{\prime}
to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, and we set its budget
\beta(C^{\prime})
to be
N_{i}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. From our algorithm,
N_{i}\geq W_{2}
must hold. Next, we delete from cluster
C
all edges that are incident to vertices of
L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{i-1}
, and the vertices of
L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{i-1}
. These deletions are added to the flag lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
. For ease of discussion, we denote by
C^{\prime\prime}
cluster
C
that is obtained after these deletions, and, when we refer to cluster
C
itself in the remainder of the description of the phase, we refer to it at the beginning of the phase. We denote the number of regular vertices in
C^{\prime\prime}
by
N^{\prime\prime}
. Our algorithm guarantees that
N^{\prime\prime}\geq\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
, and also that
N^{\prime\prime}\geq W_{2}
. We set the new budget of cluster
C^{\prime\prime}
to be
\beta(C^{\prime\prime})=(N^{\prime\prime})^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. We will show below that these updates to budgets of clusters do not increase the total budget
\beta
.

Consider now some terminal
t^{\prime\prime}\in\lambda(C)
, and recall that
C=\mbox{\sf{SpecialCluster}}(t^{\prime\prime})
, and so
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime})\subseteq V(C)
. Notice that, if
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime\prime})>(2i-1)D^{\prime}
, then
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime})=B_{C}(t^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime})
is disjoint from vertices of
L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{i-1}
, and so
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime})\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
continues to hold. Otherwise, all vertices of
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime})=B_{C}(t^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime})
belong to
L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{i}
, so

B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime})\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
holds. We consider every terminal
t^{\prime\prime}\in\lambda(C)
with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C}(t,t^{\prime\prime})\leq(2i-1)D^{\prime}
. For each such terminal, we set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t^{\prime\prime})=C^{\prime}
, and we add all such terminals to list
\lambda(C^{\prime})
. From our discussion, after these updates, Invariants
I3
and
I4
continue to hold. It is also easy to verify that Invariants
I1
and
I2
continue to hold at the end of the phase.

Recall that, from our discussion, the number of regular vertices in both
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
is at least
W_{2}
, and, from the definition of cluster budgets, we ensure that
\beta(C^{\prime}),\beta(C^{\prime\prime})\geq W_{2}
, and
\beta(C^{\prime})\geq(N(C^{\prime}))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
, while
\beta(C^{\prime\prime})\geq(N(C^{\prime\prime}))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. In order to establish Invariant
I5
, it is enough to prove that the total budget
\beta
does not increase.

Recall that Invariant
I5
guaranteed that, at the beginning of the phase,
\beta(C)\geq(N^{\prime})^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
held. Recall also that we have set
\beta(C^{\prime})=N_{i}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
, and
\beta(C^{\prime\prime})=(N^{\prime\prime})^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. Therefore, it is enough to prove that:

\beta(C)\geq\beta(C^{\prime})+\beta(C^{\prime\prime}).

(8)

We consider two cases. The first case is when
N_{i}\leq\frac{N^{\prime}}{4}
, so
N_{i}\leq N_{i-1}\cdot\eta_{i-1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}
must hold. Notice that:

N^{\prime\prime}\leq N^{\prime}-N_{i-1}
, and so:

\beta(C^{\prime\prime})\leq(N^{\prime}-N_{i-1})^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq(N^{\prime}-N_{i-1})\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}.

At the same time,
N_{i}\leq N_{i-1}\cdot\eta_{i-1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}=N_{i-1}\cdot\frac{(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}}{N_{i-1}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}}
. Therefore:

\begin{split}\beta(C^{\prime})&=N_{i}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\\
&\leq N_{i-1}^{(1-\epsilon^{\prime}/4)\cdot(1+\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}/4}\\
&\leq N_{i-1}^{1+3\epsilon^{\prime}/4-(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}/4}\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4+(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}/4}\\
&\leq N_{i-1}\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}.\end{split}

Therefore, altogether:

\begin{split}\beta(C^{\prime})+\beta(C^{\prime\prime})&\leq(N^{\prime}-N_{i-1})\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}+N_{i-1}\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq(N^{\prime})^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta(C).\end{split}

It remains to consider the second case, where
\frac{N^{\prime}}{4}\leq N_{i}\leq\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}
, and
N_{i}\leq N_{i-1}\cdot\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)
.

In this case:

\beta(C^{\prime\prime})\leq(N^{\prime}-N_{i-1})^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq(N^{\prime}-N_{i-1})\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}},

as before. At the same time:

\begin{split}\beta(C^{\prime})&=N_{i}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\\
&\leq N_{i-1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\\
&\leq N_{i-1}\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{2}\right)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}.\end{split}

(we have used the fact that
N_{i-1}\leq N^{\prime}/2
).
Recall that for all
a\geq 2
,
(1+1/a)^{a}\leq e
. Therefore:

\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)^{(1+\epsilon^{\prime})/\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)^{2/\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)^{(16/(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2})\cdot(\epsilon^{\prime}/8)}\leq e^{\epsilon^{\prime}/8}\leq 2.

We conclude that

\left(1+\frac{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{2}}{16}\right)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq 2^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, and
\beta(C^{\prime})\leq N_{i-1}\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

Altogether, we get that:

\beta(C^{\prime})+\beta(C^{\prime\prime})\leq N_{i-1}\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}+(N^{\prime}-N_{i-1})\cdot(N^{\prime})^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq(N^{\prime})^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta(C).

We conclude that Invariant
I5
continues to hold at the end of the phase.

This finishes the description of the second phase of the algorithm. We refer to the algorithm for the Flag Lowering Operation that we have described so far as
basic algorithm
. Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of edges that were deleted from cluster
C
over the course of Phase 1. If Phase 1 was successful, then the running time of the basic algorithm is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
(and we did not execute Phase 2). If Phase 1 was unsuccessful, then the running time of the basic algorithm is bounded by
O((W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
, and we have added a new cluster to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. Recall that, if Phase 1 is successful, then the resulting sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
of edge- and vertex-deletions is a valid flag-lowering sequence for
C
, that is, either
v
or
v^{\prime}
are deleted from
C
at the end of the sequence. If Phase 1 is unsuccessful, then the sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
of edge- and vertex-deletions from cluster
C
that we obtain at the end of the basic algorithm may not be a valid flag-lowering sequence, if
v
and
v^{\prime}
both remain in cluster
C
once the updates from
\Sigma^{\prime}
are applied to
C
. In this case, we repeat the basic algorithm, with the same cluster
C
and pair
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices. We keep repeating the basic algorithm until either
v
or
v^{\prime}
are deleted from
C
. If the basic algorithm is repeated
k+1
times, and
E^{\prime}
is the set of edges that are eventually deleted from
C
via the resulting flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
, then the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by

O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)+k(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
. At the same time, at least
k
new clusters were added to
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
at the end of the Flag Lowering operation.

This completes the description of the Flag Lowering operation, and of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainNC}
problem.

6.1.3
Analysis

Note that our algorithm maintains a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters of
H
.
At the beginning of the algorithm,
{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{H\right\}
holds, and, as the algorithm progresses, clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}
only undergo allowed changes, as required. We argue that the algorithm has the Consistent Covering property. Indeed, if a regular vertex
v
is ever deleted from the set
T
of terminals, then we have shown already that the Consistent Covering property holds for that vertex throughout the entire algorithm. If vertex
v
remains in set
T
throughout the algorithm, then Consistent Covering property for that vertex follows from Invariants
I2
,
I3
and
I4
.

Next, we show that Properties
R1
–
R6
hold throughout the algorithm. Properties
R3
,
R4
and
R5
are immediate from the description of the algorithm.
We now establish Property
R6
.

Recall that our algorithm maintains a partition
(S_{0},\ldots,S_{r})
of the set
V
of regular vertices of
H
into classes. For all
0\leq j\leq r
, we denote by
S_{\geq j}=S_{j}\cup\cdots\cup S_{r}
.
We will use the following observation in order to establish Property
R6
. The proof is essentially identical to the arguments that were used in the proof of
Theorem

2.3
, and is delayed to Section
E
of Appendix.

Observation 6.4
.

For all
1\leq j\leq r
,
|S_{\geq j}|\leq N^{1-j\hat{\epsilon}}
holds throughout the algorithm.

Since
r=\left\lceil 1/\hat{\epsilon}\right\rceil+1
, we conclude that set
S_{r}
of vertices remains empty throughout the algorithm. Therefore, for every regular vertex
x\in V
, the number of clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
to which
x
ever belonged is bounded by:

r\cdot N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}\leq\frac{2N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}}{\hat{\epsilon}}\leq\frac{64N^{\epsilon^{\prime}/16}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq W_{2}^{\epsilon^{\prime}/4}.

(we have used the fact that
\hat{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{\prime}/32
,
N\leq W_{1}\leq W_{2}^{1.5}
, and
\frac{1}{(\log W_{2})^{1/4}}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1/400
). This establishes Property
R6
.

It remains to establish Properties
R1
and
R2
. Recall that, at the beginning of the algorithm,
\beta=\beta(H)=N^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
.
From Invariant
I5
, as the algorithm progresses, the total budget
\beta=\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}\beta(C)
does not grow, and, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
,
\beta(C)\geq W_{2}
holds. Therefore, at the end of the algorithm:

|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|\leq\frac{\beta}{W_{2}}\leq\frac{N^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}\leq\frac{W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}.

Since clusters may be added to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, but never deleted from it, this establishes Requirement
R1
.

It now remains to establish Requirement
R2
. Consider any cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, and recall that we have denoted by
N^{0}(C)
the number of regular vertices in
C
when
C
was added to
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. Recall that, when
C
is added to
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, we have set
\beta(C)=(N^{0}(C))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. Denote by
{\mathcal{W}}(C)=\left\{C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{q}\right\}
the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, that were created by our algorithm by applying the cluster-splitting operation to cluster
C
, and assume that they were added to
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
in the order of their indices. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, denote
M_{i}=N^{0}(C_{i})
. It is easy to verify from our algorithm that, if
M^{\prime}_{i}
denotes the number of regular vertices in cluster
C
just before cluster
C_{i}
was split from it, then
M_{i}\leq M^{\prime}_{i}/2\leq N^{0}(C)/2
holds. When cluster
C_{i}
was split from
C
, we set
\beta(C_{i})=M_{i}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. Since, after each such cluster
C_{i}
was split from
C
, the total budget
\beta
did not increase, it is easy to verify that
\sum_{i=1}^{q}M_{i}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq(N^{0}(C))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

We construct a partitioning tree
{\mathcal{T}}
, whose set of vertices is
\left\{v(C)\mid C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\right\}
(where we consider the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
of clusters at the end of the algorithm). Let
C^{0}
be the cluster of that was added to
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
first (that is, when
C^{0}
was added to
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
,
C^{0}=H
held, but subsequently vertices and edges may have been deleted from
C^{0}
). The root of the tree is vertex
v(C^{0})
, and, for every pair of clusters
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
with
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}(C)
, vertex
v(C^{\prime})
becomes the child vertex of
v(C)
in
{\mathcal{T}}
.
For every vertex
v(C)
, the weight of the vertex is defined to be
w(C)=(N^{0}(C))^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

From the above discussion, if
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}(C)
, then
N^{0}(C^{\prime})\leq N^{0}(C)/2
, so the depth of the tree
T
is bounded by
d=\left\lceil\log W_{1}\right\rceil
. For
0\leq i\leq d
, we let
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
denote the set of all clusters
C
, for which the distance from
v(C)
to
v(C^{0})
in the tree is exactly
i
. From our discussion so far, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
\sum_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}(C)}w(C^{\prime})\leq w(C)
, and
w(C^{0})\leq W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
. It follows that, for all
0\leq i\leq d
,
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{i}}w(C)\leq W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}
, and so
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}w(C)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{i}}w(C)\leq W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot 4\log W_{1}\leq W_{1}^{1+2\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

Note that, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
,
w(C)\geq N^{0}(C)
. Therefore, overall,
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}N^{0}(C)\leq W_{1}^{1+2\epsilon^{\prime}}
.
This establishes Requirement
R2
.

It now remains to bound the running time of the algorithm. Assume that the algorithm for the Flag-Lowering operation was executed
q
times. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, we denote by
C_{i}
the cluster that was processed during the
i
th execution of the Flag-Lower operation, by
m_{i}
the number of edges that were deleted from
C_{i}
during the operation, and by
k_{i}
the total number of clusters that were added to set
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
over the course of the operation. Then the running time of the
i
th Flag-Lowering operation is bounded by
O(|E_{i}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)+k_{i}(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu))
. The total running time of all Flag-Lowering operations is bounded by:

O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}|E_{i}|\cdot(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)\right)+O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}k_{i}(W_{1}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)\right)

Since the number of edges in every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
is bounded by
W_{1}\mu
, and, from Property
R1

|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|\leq\frac{W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}
, we get that the first summand is bounded by:
O\left(\frac{W_{1}^{2+2\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{1+2\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)\right)
. Since
\sum_{i=1}^{q}k_{i}\leq|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|\leq\frac{W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}
, the second summand is also bounded by
O\left(\frac{W_{1}^{2+2\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)\right)
. Therefore, the total time that all Flag Lowering operations take is bounded by
O\left(\frac{W_{1}^{2+2\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{1+2\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}\cdot\log(W_{1}\cdot\mu)\right)\leq O\left(\frac{W_{1}^{2+3\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{2}}{W_{2}}\right)
(we have used the fact that
\frac{1}{(\log W_{2})^{1/4}}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1/400
).

Note that the total number of edges that are ever present in graph
H
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
W_{1}\cdot\mu
. When an edge is deleted from
H
, or inserted into
H
(via supernode-splitting), we may need to perform the same update in a a number of clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. Since
|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|\leq\frac{W_{1}^{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}}{W_{2}}
, the total time that is required in order to maintain the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
(excluding the running time of the Flag Lowering operations) is bounded by
O\left(\frac{W_{1}^{2+\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu}{W_{2}}\right)
.

Overall, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\frac{W_{1}^{2+3\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\mu^{2}}{W_{2}}\right)
.

7
Algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
Problem - Proof of
Theorem

5.8

For convenience, we will denote the graph
C
that is given as input by
H
. Therefore,
throughout this section, we assume that we are given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, where
H
is a connected graph, that serves as input to the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem.
Graph
H
undergoes an online sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations, and we are given its dynamic degree bound
\mu
. Additionally, we are given a parameter
\hat{W}\geq N^{0}(H)
, where
N^{0}(H)
is the number of regular vertices of
H
at the beginning of the algorithm.
Lastly, we are given a precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
, where
1/\epsilon
is an integer, and a distance parameter
D^{*}\geq D
, that is an integral power of
2
.
Our goal is to design an algorithm that supports queries
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(H,v,v^{\prime})
: given a pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V(H)
of regular vertices of
H
, return a path
P
of length at most
\alpha^{\prime}\cdot D^{*}
connecting them in
H
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. The algorithm may, at any time, raise a flag
F_{H}
, at which time it must supply a pair
\hat{v},\hat{v}^{\prime}
of regular vertices of
H
(called a witness pair), with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(\hat{v},\hat{v}^{\prime})>D^{*}
.
Once flag
F_{H}
is raised, the algorithm will obtain, as part of its input update sequence
\Sigma
, a valid flag-lowering sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Flag
F_{H}
is lowered after the updates from
\Sigma^{\prime}
are processed by the algorithm. Queries
short-path-query
may only be asked when flag
F_{H}
is down.
We will assume in the remainder of the proof that
\mu<\hat{W}^{1/10}
holds, since otherwise we can simply use the algorithm from
Theorem

5.7
.

Throughout this subsection, we also assume that, for some parameter
W<\hat{W}
and
c^{\prime}=3
, Assumption
5.1
holds. We denote the approximation factor from
5.1
by
\alpha=\alpha(W)
. Recall that the desired approximation factor
\alpha^{\prime}
for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is
\alpha^{\prime}=\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})},(8\alpha)^{2}\right\}
.
Throughout the algorithm, we use two parameters:
\rho=\frac{4\hat{W}}{W}
and
\hat{D}=\frac{2^{14}\cdot D^{*}}{\epsilon^{2}}
. From the statement of
Theorem

5.8
,
\rho\leq 4\sqrt{W}
.

As in
[
Chu21
]
, the main tool that our algorithm uses is a
balanced pseudocut
. This notion was defined in
[
Chu21
]
; we use a very similar but slightly different definition. This modified definition allows us to somewhat simplify parts of the algorithm. Additionally, we will replace expanders, that were used in the construction of
[
Chu21
]
, with well-connected graphs. This allows us to improve the approximation factor that our algorithm achieves.

We start by defining balanced pseudocuts, and introducing some tools for them.

7.1
Balanced Pseudocut

The notion of a balanced pseudocut, that can be viewed as a generalization of the notion of a balanced cut, was first introduced in
[
Chu21
]
, and it is also a central notion that we use in our algorithm.
Intuitively, following a rather standard definition, for a given balance parameter
\rho
, a
balanced multicut
in a graph
G
can be defined as a subset
T\subseteq V(G)
of vertices of
G
, such that every connected component of
G\setminus T
contains at most
|V(G)|/\rho
vertices. We use instead a notion of
balanced pseudocuts
, that can be viewed as a relaxation of the notion of a balanced multicut. One advantage for using this notion is that, as was shown in
[
Chu21
]
, there is an efficient algorithm that, given a graph
G
, computes a balanced pseudocut
T
in
G
, and also embeds an expander
X
into
G
, where
V(X)\subseteq T
, and
V(X)
contains a large fraction of vertices of
T
. We slightly modify the definition of balanced pseudocut from
[
Chu21
]
. We will exploit the idea of
[
Chu21
]
of embedding an expander defined over a large subset of vertices of the pseudocut, but we will replace the expander with a well-connected graph.
We now formally define a pseudocut.

Definition 7.1
(Pseudocut).

Let
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
be a valid input structure, let
\hat{W}
be a parameter, such that the total number of regular vertices in
H
is at most
\hat{W}
, let
\hat{D}>D
be a distance parameter, and let
\rho>1
be a balance parameter. A subset
T\subseteq V
of regular vertices of
H
is a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-
pseudocut
, if, for every regular vertex
x\in V\setminus T
, the total number of regular vertices lying in
B_{H\setminus T}(x,\hat{D})
is at most
\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}
.

Note that, if
T
is a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for
H
, then every set
T^{\prime}
of regular vertices of
H
with
T\subseteq T^{\prime}
is also a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for
H
. Note also that, as graph
H
undergoes a sequence of valid update operations, set
T\cap V
remains a valid
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut. This is since valid update operations may not insert regular vertices into
H
, and since, from
3.3
, distances in graph
H
may only increase as it undergoes valid update operations.

As mentioned earlier, we use a subroutine that, given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, together with parameters
\rho>1
and
\hat{D}>D
, computes a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
in
H
, and embeds a well-connected graph
X
with
V(X)
containing a large fraction of vertices of
T
, into
H
. This subroutine is implemented as follows: we start with an arbitrary pseudocut
T
in
H
(for example, it may contain all regular vertices of
H
), and then iterate. In every iteration, we will either embed a well-connected graph
X
defined over a large enough subset of vertices of
T
into
H
as desired, or we will compute another
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T^{\prime}
in
H
, whose cardinality is significantly lower than the cardinality of
T
. We can think of
T^{\prime}
as an “improved” pseudocut. We then replace
T
with
T^{\prime}
and continue to the next iteration. In order to implement this idea, we will use the following observation that, under some conditions, allows us to improve a given pseudocut
T
.

Observation 7.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
be a valid input structure, let
\hat{W}
be a parameter, such that the total number of regular vertices in
H
is at most
\hat{W}
, and let
S
be a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for
H
. Suppose we are given a partition
(S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime})
of
S
into two subsets, such that, if we denote by
H^{\prime}=H\setminus S^{\prime\prime}
, then the number of regular vertices in
B_{H^{\prime}}(S^{\prime},2\hat{D})
is at most
\hat{W}/\rho
. Then
S^{\prime\prime}
is a valid
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for
H
.

Proof.

Let
H^{\prime\prime}=H\setminus S
. From the definition of a pseudocut, for every regular vertex
x\in V(H^{\prime\prime})
, the number of regular vertices in
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
is at most
\hat{W}/\rho
.

Assume now for contradiction that
S^{\prime\prime}
is not a valid
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for
H
. Then there is some regular vertex
x\in V(H^{\prime})
, such that
B_{H^{\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
contains more than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices.

We consider two cases. The first case happens if
B_{H^{\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
contains at least one vertex of
S^{\prime}
. If this is the case, then
B_{H^{\prime}}(x,\hat{D})\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(S^{\prime},2\hat{D})
, and so
B_{H^{\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
may contain at most
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, a contradiction.

Consider now the second case, where
B_{H^{\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
contains no vertices of
S^{\prime}
. Then deleting the vertices of
S^{\prime}
from
H^{\prime}
does not affect the subgraph of
H^{\prime}
induced by
B_{H^{\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
. In other words,
B_{H^{\prime}}(x,\hat{D})=B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
. But from the definition of a pseudocut,
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(x,\hat{D})
contains at most
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, a contradiction.

We now turn to the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, and the proof of
Theorem

5.8
. Recall that we denoted
\rho=\frac{4\hat{W}}{W}
.
Throughout the algorithm, we use a parameter
\hat{D}=\frac{2^{14}\cdot D^{*}}{\epsilon^{2}}
.
Our algorithm will maintain a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
for graph
H
, that may change overtime. The algorithm is partitioned into three stages. The first stage continues as long as
|T|>\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
holds, and it is partitioned into phases. At the beginning of every phase, we compute a pseudocut
T
, a well-connected graph
X
defined over a large subset of vertices
T
, and an embedding of
X
into
H
, so that the paths of the embedding are short, and the embedding causes a relatively low congestion. We then employ the algorithm for
APSP
on well-connected graphs from
Theorem

2.9
. Recall that the algorithm maintains a large decremental set
S^{\prime}\subseteq V(X)
of vertices, and supports
short-path
queries in graph
X
between pairs of vertices
x,y\in S^{\prime}
. We will also maintain an
ES-Tree
in graph
H
, that is rooted at the vertices of
S^{\prime}
, and has depth
\Theta(D^{*})
. These data structures are sufficient in order to support
short-path-query
queries between pairs of regular vertices in
H
. Additionally, whenever some vertex leaves the
ES-Tree
, we can obtain the desired pair
x,y
of vertices of
H
that are sufficiently far from each other. Intuitively, the number of edge deletions from
H
that the algorithm for
APSP
on well-connected graphs can accommodate is roughly comparable to
|T|^{1-O(\epsilon)}
. On the other hand, the algorithm that we employ at the beginning of the phase, in order to compute the pseudocut
T
, the well-connected graph
X
, and its embedding into
H
, has running time that is roughly bounded by
(\hat{W}\cdot\mu)^{1+O(\epsilon)}
. Therefore, as long as
|T|
is sufficiently large, we can maintain the data structures efficiently. Once
|T|\leq\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
holds, the first stage of the algorithm terminates. Over the course of the remainder of the algorithm, the pseudocut
T
remains unchanged (except when vertices of
T
are deleted from
H
– all such vertices are deleted from
T
as well).
The second stage of the algorithm is only executed if
64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}>\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
, and it only lasts as long as
T\cap V(H)>\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
. We partition the second stage into phases as well. At the beginning of every phase, we select an arbitrary vertex
t\in T\cap V(H)
, and initialize an
ES-Tree
data structure in graph
H
, with source vertex
t
, and depth parameter
\hat{D}
. This data structure will allow us to support
short-path
queries, and to identify regular vertices of
H
whose distance from
t
becomes too large. Once vertex
t
is deleted from
H
, the phase ends and a new phase begins. Note that the number of phases in the second stage is bounded by
64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}
, and every phase takes time
O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\log\hat{W})
. Once
|T|\leq\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
holds, the second stage terminates.
We employ a different strategy for stage
3
, which is similar to the one employed by
[
Chu21
]
. From the definition of a pseudocut, in graph
H\setminus T
, for every regular vertex
v
,
B_{H\setminus T}(v,\hat{D})
contains at most
W/4
regular vertices. Intuitively, we compute an initial neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}
in graph
H\setminus T
, with distance parameter
D^{*}
, and we ensure that the diameter of every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
is at most
\hat{D}
. This guarantees that every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
contains fewer than
W/4
regular vertices of
H
. We can now employ the algorithm from
5.1
in order to maintain, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
and distance scale
D_{i}\leq\hat{D}
, a neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{W}}_{i}^{C}
. We construct a contracted graph
\hat{H}
, with the set
T
of regular vertices, and supernodes corresponding to the clusters of
\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bigcup_{i}{\mathcal{W}}_{i}^{C}
. We show that distances between vertices of
T
in
\hat{H}
are approximately equal to those in
H
. We also design an algorithm for maintaining graph
\hat{H}
via a sequence valid update operations. Since
|T|
is sufficiently small, we can apply the algorithm from
5.1
to compute and maintain a neighborhood cover for the contracted graph
\hat{H}
. This, in turn, allows us to respond to
short-path
queries between pairs of vertices in
T
, and to correctly establish when a pair of such vertices become too far from each other in
H
. We also maintain an
ES-Tree
data structure in graph
H
, rooted at the set
T
of vertices, that allows us to support
short-path
queries between pairs of regular vertices of
H
, and to correctly establish when any regular vertex of
H
becomes too far from the vertices of
T
. Once all vertices of
T
are deleted from
H
, we will exploit the clusters in
\left\{{\mathcal{W}}_{i}^{C}\right\}
for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
i=\log D^{*}
, in order to continue to support
short-path
queries.
In the following three subsections, we provide our algorithm for each of the three stages.

7.2
Stage 1: Large Pseudocut Regime

In this subsection we provide the first part of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that deals with the large-pseudocut setting.

Recall that we are given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, where
H
is a connected graph, that serves as input to the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem.
Graph
H
undergoes an online sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations, and we are given its dynamic degree bound
\mu
. Additionally, we are given a parameter
\hat{W}\geq N^{0}(H)
, where
N^{0}(H)
is the number of regular vertices of
H
at the beginning of the algorithm. We also use the parameter
W
from
5.1
, and a parameter
\rho=\frac{4\hat{W}}{W}
. Recall that, from the statement of
Theorem

5.8
,
\rho\leq\sqrt{W}
.

Throughout the algorithm, we will maintain a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
in graph
H
, that may change overtime. At the beginning of the algorithm, we let
T
contain all regular vertices of
H
, so it is clearly a valid
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut. As the algorithm progresses, we will compute new pseudocuts, and we will ensure that the cardinality of the pseudocut that we maintain never increases. Once we obtain a pseudocut
T
with
|T|\leq\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, the first stage of the algorithm terminates. Following is a key theorem, that allows us to compute a pseudocut
T^{\prime}
in graph
H
, and, if
|T^{\prime}|>\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, it also allows us to compute a well-connected graph
X
defined over a large subset of the vertices of
T^{\prime}
, together with a Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, and an embedding of
X
into
H
via a set of short paths, that cause a relatively small congestion. We will use this algorithm repeatedly. This algorithm is our main technical contribution to the improved algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem.

Theorem 7.3
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, whose input consists of:

•

a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, such that the maximum vertex degree in
H
is at most
\mu
;

•

parameters
W<\hat{W}\leq W^{1.5}
, such that, if
N(H)
is the number of regular vertices in
H
, then
N(H)\leq\hat{W}
;

•

distance parameter
\hat{D}>D
, and precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer; and

•

a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
in
H
, with
|T|>\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, where
\rho=\frac{4\hat{W}}{W}
.

The output of the algorithm is a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T^{\prime}
in
H
, with
|T^{\prime}|\leq|T|
. Moreover, if
|T^{\prime}|>\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, then, additionally, the algorithm computes
a graph
X
with maximum vertex degree at most
\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}
and
V(X)\subseteq T
, such that
|V(X)|=N^{1/\epsilon}
for some parameter
N\geq\max\left\{\frac{W^{\epsilon}}{128\rho^{\epsilon}\mu^{2\epsilon}},\frac{W^{\epsilon^{2}}}{32}\right\}
. In the latter case, the algorithm also returns an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
via paths of length at most
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta=2^{20}\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\rho^{2}D^{\prime\prime}
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, such that
X
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(X)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{d}=2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{5}}
, with
\tilde{c}
being the constant used in the definition of the Hierarchical Support Structure.
The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
.

The proof of the theorem is somewhat technical, and we delay it to Section
7.2.2
, after we complete the description of Stage 1 of the algorithm using it.

Throughout the algorithm, we maintain a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
for graph
H
. Over the course of the algorithm, the cardinality of the pseudocut that we maintain may only decrease. Once we obtain a pseudocut
T
whose cardinality is at most
\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, we terminate Stage 1 of the algorithm.
At the beginning of the algorithm, we compute an initial
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
, that contains all regular vertices of the input graph
H
.
Let
\Lambda^{\prime}=\frac{W^{1-20\epsilon}}{\hat{D}\rho^{3}\mu^{2}}
.

The execution of the algorithm is partitioned into phases. At the beginning of the algorithm, the first phase starts.
Each phase continues as long as the number of edges that were deleted from graph
H
over the course of the phase is at most
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Once
\Lambda^{\prime}
edges are deleted from
H
since the start of the phase, a new phase commences. Since the total number of edges that are ever present in graph
H
is bounded by
N^{0}(H)\cdot\mu\leq\hat{W}\cdot\mu\leq\frac{W\rho\mu}{4}
, we get that the number of phases in the algorithm is bounded by:

\frac{W\rho\mu}{4\Lambda^{\prime}}\leq W^{20\epsilon}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu^{3}\cdot\hat{D}.

We now describe the execution of a single phase.

7.2.1
Execution of a Single Phase

At the beginning of a phase, we are given a set
T
of regular vertices, with
|T|>\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, that is a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for graph
H
; at the beginning of the first phase,
T
is the set of all regular vertices of
H
. At the beginning of a phase, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

7.3
to the current graph
H
and pseudocut
T
; parameters
\hat{D},W,\hat{W}
and
\epsilon
remain unchanged.
Recall that the running time of the algorithm is
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
.

If the algorithm returns a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T^{\prime}
with
|T^{\prime}|\leq\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, then we terminate Stage 1, and continue to Stage 2 of the algorithm.

Therefore, we assume from now on, that the algorithm returned a

(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T^{\prime}
in
H
, with
|T^{\prime}|>\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, together with a graph
X
with maximum vertex degree at most
\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, and
V(X)\subseteq T
, such that
|V(X)|=N^{1/\epsilon}
for some parameter
N\geq\max\left\{\frac{W^{\epsilon}}{128\rho^{\epsilon}\mu^{2\epsilon}},\frac{W^{\epsilon^{2}}}{32}\right\}

Notice that
|V(X)|=N^{1/\epsilon}\geq\frac{W}{2^{7/\epsilon}\cdot\rho\cdot\mu^{2}}
. The algorithm also returns an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
via paths of length at most
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta=2^{20}\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\rho^{2}D^{\prime\prime}
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, such that
X
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(X)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{d}=2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{5}}
, with
\tilde{c}
being the constant used in the definition of the Hierarchical Support Structure.
We now describe the data structures that the algorithm for a single phase maintains.

Data Structures

For every edge
e\in E(X)
, we denote by
P(e)\in{\mathcal{P}}
the path that serves as the embedding path of edge
e
. For each edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
, we initialize the set
S(e^{\prime})
of all edges
e\in E(X)
, such that
e^{\prime}\in P(e)
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most

\eta
, for each edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
,
|S(e^{\prime})|\leq\eta
holds. As updates from the input sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations are applied to the input graph
H
, whenever an edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
is deleted from
H
, we consider every edge
e\in S(e^{\prime})
. Each such edge
e
is deleted from graph
X
, and we also delete
e
from the lists
S(e^{\prime\prime})
of every edge
e^{\prime\prime}\in P(e)
. Since, for every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
, an edge
e\in E(X)
may only be added to set
S(e^{\prime})
at the beginning of the algorithm, and afterwards it may only be deleted from
S(e^{\prime})
once, the total time that is required in order to initialize and maintain the sets
\left\{S(e^{\prime})\mid e^{\prime}\in E(H)\right\}
of edges is asymptotically bounded by the time that was required in order to compute the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
, which, in turn, is bounded by the running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

7.3
.

Recall that, over the course of a single phase, graph
H
may undergo at most
\Lambda^{\prime}=\frac{W^{1-20\epsilon}}{\hat{D}\rho^{3}\mu^{2}}
edge deletions, and, for every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
:

|S(e^{\prime})|\leq\eta=2^{20}\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\rho^{2}D^{\prime\prime}\leq\frac{2^{29}}{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\rho^{2}\leq W^{2\epsilon}\hat{D}\rho^{2},

since
\hat{W}\leq W^{1.5}
,
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
, and
\hat{W}^{\epsilon}\geq W^{\epsilon}>2^{8/\epsilon}
from inequality
2
.

Therefore, the deletion of a single edge
from
H
may result in the deletion of at most
W^{2\epsilon}\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\rho^{2}
edges from
X
. Overall, the number of edges that may be deleted from
X
over the course of a single phase is bounded by:

\Lambda=\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot W^{2\epsilon}\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\rho^{2}\leq\frac{W^{1-20\epsilon}}{\hat{D}\rho^{3}\mu^{2}}\cdot W^{2\epsilon}\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\rho^{2}\leq\frac{W^{1-18\epsilon}}{\rho\mu^{2}}.

(since
\Lambda^{\prime}=\frac{W^{1-20\epsilon}}{\hat{D}\rho^{3}\mu^{2}}
.)

Recall that
|V(X)|\geq\frac{W}{2^{7/\epsilon}\cdot\rho\cdot\mu^{2}}\geq\frac{W^{1-\epsilon}}{\rho\cdot\mu^{2}}
. It is now easy to verify that
\Lambda\leq|V(X)|^{1-10\epsilon}
.

We apply the algorithm for
APSP
in well-connected graphs from
Theorem

2.9
to graph
X
, with parameters
N
and
\epsilon
remaining unchanged, and the Hierarchical Support Structure for graph
X
that we have computed. In order to be able to use the theorem, we need to verify that
\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{6}}
holds.

Recall that
N\geq\max\left\{\frac{W^{\epsilon}}{128\rho^{\epsilon}\mu^{2\epsilon}},\frac{W^{\epsilon^{2}}}{32}\right\}
. Also, since
N\leq|V(X)|^{\epsilon}\leq\hat{W}^{\epsilon}\leq W^{1.5\epsilon}
, we get that
\log N\leq 1.5\epsilon\cdot\log W\leq\log W
. Therefore:

\frac{N^{\epsilon^{4}}}{\log N}\geq\frac{W^{\epsilon^{6}}}{32\log W}\geq\frac{W^{\epsilon^{7}}}{\log W}\geq W^{\epsilon^{8}}\geq 2^{1/\epsilon^{10}}\geq 2^{128/\epsilon^{6}}

(we have used inequalities
3
and
2
).
We can now apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.9
to graph
X
, the Hierarchical Support Structure for graph
X
that we have computed, and parameters
N
and
\epsilon
that remain unchanged. As observed already, over the course of a single phase, graph
X
undergoes a sequence of
\Lambda\leq|V(X)|^{1-10\epsilon}
edge deletions. Recall that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.9
maintains, over the course of the phase, a non-empty set
S^{\prime}(X)
of vertices of
X
, that is decremental: that is, after the initialization, vertices may leave
S^{\prime}(X)
but they may not join it. The algorithm supports
short-path
queries between vertices of
S^{\prime}(X)
: given a pair
x,y\in S^{\prime}(X)
of such vertices, return a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in the current graph
X
, whose length is at most
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.
The total update time of the algorithm is
O(|E(X)|^{1+O(\epsilon)})
.
Since the maximum vertex degree in
X
is bounded by
\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, and
|V(X)|\leq\hat{W}
, we get that the total update time of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.9
is bounded by
O(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)})
.

The last data structure that we maintain is a modified
ES-Tree
in graph
H
, rooted at the set
S^{\prime}(X)
of vertices. More precisely, we construct a graph
H^{\prime}
, that is obtained from graph
H
by adding a source vertex
s
to
H
, and connecting it to every vertex of
S^{\prime}(X)
with an edge of length
1
. Once graph
H^{\prime}
is initialized, whenever graph
H
is updated with a valid update operation
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we apply the same update operation to graph
H^{\prime}
. Since a supernode-splitting operation is a special case of a vertex-splitting operation, graph
H^{\prime}
undergoes an online sequence of edge-deletion, isolated vertex-deletion, and vertex-splitting updates. We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
to maintain a modified
ES-Tree
in graph
H^{\prime}
, with the source vertex
s
, and a bound
2\hat{D}
on the tree depth. The running time of the algorithm is
O(m^{*}\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\log m^{*})
, where
m^{*}
is the total number of edges that ever belonged to graph
H^{\prime}
. It is easy to verify that
m^{*}\leq O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu)\leq O(\hat{W}^{1.1})
, and so the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\log W)
.
Recall that the algorithm also maintains a set
A
of vertices of
H
, such that, for all
v\in A
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}}(v,s)>2\hat{D}
. Whenever a new regular vertex
v
is added to set
A
, we let
x
be any vertex of
S^{\prime}(X)
. Note that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(x,v)>\hat{D}
must hold. We raise the flag
F_{H}
, and we provide the pair
x,v
of vertices as a witness.

We now bound the total running time of a single phase.
Recall that the running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

7.3
is
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
, the total update time of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.9
is bounded by
\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}
, and the running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
is bounded by
O(\hat{W}\mu\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\log W)\leq O(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\mu)
(since
\log W\leq W^{\epsilon^{12}}\leq\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)}
from Inequality
3
).
Therefore, the total running time of the algorithm for a single phase is at most
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
. Since the number of phases in Stage 1 is bounded by
W^{20\epsilon}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\hat{D}\cdot\mu^{3}
, we get that the total running time
of Stage 1 is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{3}\cdot\rho^{8}\cdot\mu^{4}\right)
.

Responding to Queries

We now provide an algorithm for supporting queries
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(H,v,v^{\prime})
over the course of Stage 1. Suppose we are given a query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(H,v,v^{\prime})
, where
v,v^{\prime}\in V(H)
are regular vertices of
H
. Recall that our goal is to return a path
P
of length at most
\alpha^{\prime}\cdot D^{*}
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
H
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Recall that a
short-path-query
cannot be asked while the flag
F_{H}
is up. Therefore, flag
F_{H}
is currently down, and the set
A
of vertices that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
maintains contains no regular vertices. Vertices
v,v^{\prime}
must then lie in the
ES-Tree
that the algorithm maintain. We perform query
\mbox{\sf{SSSP-query}}(v)
and
\mbox{\sf{SSSP-query}}(v^{\prime})
in the data structure maintained by the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
, that must return a path
P_{1}
connecting
v
to
s
in
H^{\prime}
, and a path
P_{2}
connecting
v^{\prime}
to
S
in
H^{\prime}
of length at most
2\hat{D}
each, in time
O(|E(P_{1})|+|E(P_{2})|)
. Let
s_{1}
be the penultimate vertex on path
P_{1}
, and let
s_{2}
be defined similarly for
P_{2}
. From the construction of graph
H^{\prime}
,
s_{1},s_{2}\in S^{\prime}(X)
must hold. If
s_{1}=s_{2}
, then we let
P
be the path obtained by concatenating paths
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, and we return path
P
as response to the query. The length of the path is at most
4\hat{D}\leq\alpha^{\prime}\cdot\hat{D}
, and the processing time of the query is
O(|E(P)|)
. Assume now that
s_{1}\neq s_{2}
.
We execute query
\mbox{\sf{short-path}}(s_{1},s_{2})
in the data structure maintained by the algorithm from
Theorem

2.9
, obtaining a path
Q
connecting
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
in
X
, whose length is at most
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}
, in time
O(|E(Q)|)
. Let
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{z})
denote the sequence of the edges on path
Q
. For all
1\leq i\leq z
, let
P(e_{i})\in{\mathcal{P}}
be the path that serves as the embedding of edge
e_{i}
; recall that the length of the path is bounded by
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
. Let
P^{\prime}
be the path in graph
H
, obtained by concatenating the paths
P(e_{1}),\ldots,P(e_{z})
. Then path
P^{\prime}
connects
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
in graph
H
, and its length is bounded by
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\hat{D}
. Let
P
be the path in graph
H
that is obtained by concatenating paths
P_{1},P^{\prime}
and
P_{2}
. Then path
P
connects
v
to
v^{\prime}
, and its length is bounded by
4\hat{D}+2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\hat{D}\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot\hat{D}\leq\frac{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot D^{*}}{\epsilon}\leq 2^{O(1/\epsilon^{6})}\cdot D^{*}\leq\alpha^{\prime}\cdot D^{*}
. We return path
P
as the response to the query. It is immediate to verify that the running time of the algorithm for processing the query is bounded by
O(|E(P)|)
.

In order to complete the algorithm for Stage 1, it remains to provide the proof of
Theorem

7.3
, which we do next.

7.2.2
Proof of
Theorem

7.3

The proof of the theorem easily follows from the following, slightly weaker, theorem.

Theorem 7.4
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, whose input consists of:

•

a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
, such that the maximum vertex degree in
H
is at most
\mu
;

•

parameters
W<\hat{W}\leq W^{1.5}
, such that, if
N(H)
is the number of regular vertices in
H
, then
N(H)\leq\hat{W}
;

•

distance parameter
\hat{D}>D
, and precision parameter
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq 1/400
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer; and

•

a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
in
H
, with
|T|>\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
, where
\rho=\frac{4\hat{W}}{W}
.

The algorithm returns either a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T^{\prime}
in
H
, with
|T^{\prime}|<|T|/2
; or a graph
X
with maximum vertex degree at most
\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}
and
V(X)\subseteq T
, such that
|V(X)|=N^{1/\epsilon}
for some parameter
N\geq\frac{|T|^{\epsilon}}{32\rho^{\epsilon}}
. In the latter case, the algorithm also returns an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
via paths of length at most
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta=2^{20}\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\rho^{2}D^{\prime\prime}
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, such that
X
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(X)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{d}=2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{5}}
, with
\tilde{c}
being the constant used in the definition of the Hierarchical Support Structure.
The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
.

We provide the proof of
Theorem

7.4
below, after we complete the proof of
Theorem

7.3
using it. Let
T
be the input
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for graph
H
. We perform iterations, as long as the current pseudocut
T
has cardinality greater than
\max\left\{\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}},64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}\right\}
. In every iteration, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

7.4
to the input structure
{\mathcal{J}}
, the current pseudocut
T
, and parameters
W,\hat{W},\hat{D}
and
\epsilon
that remain unchanged. If the algorithm returns a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T^{\prime}
in
H
, with
|T^{\prime}|<|T|/2
, then we replace
T^{\prime}
with
T
, and continue to the next iteration. Assume now that the algorithm from
Theorem

7.4
returned a graph
X
with maximum vertex degree at most
\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, with
V(X)\subseteq T
, such that
|V(X)|=N^{1/\epsilon}
for some parameter
N\geq\frac{|T|^{\epsilon}}{32\rho^{\epsilon}}
, together with an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
via paths of length at most
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta=2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}\cdot D^{\prime\prime}
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, such that
X
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(X)
of vertices defined by the support structure.
In this case, we terminate the algorithm, and return the graph
X
, its correponding Hierarchical Support Structure, and the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
.
Since
|T|\geq\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
, we are guaranteed that
N\geq\frac{|T|^{\epsilon}}{32\rho^{\epsilon}}\geq\frac{W^{\epsilon}}{128\rho^{\epsilon}\mu^{2\epsilon}}
, and, since
|T|\geq 64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}
, we also get that
N\geq\frac{W^{\epsilon^{2}}}{32}
. Therefore,
N\geq\max\left\{\frac{W^{\epsilon}}{128\rho^{\epsilon}\mu^{2\epsilon}},\frac{W^{\epsilon^{2}}}{32}\right\}
, as required.

Since, in every iteration,
|T|
decreases by at least factor
2
, the number of iterations is bounded by
O(\log\hat{W})\leq O(\log W)\leq W^{O(\epsilon)}
(the last inequality follows from Inequality
3
). The running time of a single iteration of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
, and so the running time of the whole algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
.

In the remainder of this subsection we focus on the proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

The proof of the theorem uses the following claim.

Claim 7.5
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, whose input is the same as that in
Theorem

7.4
. The algorithm returns one of the following:

•

either a collection
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{|T|}{16}
edges of
H
, such that, for every vertex
t\in T
,
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}\left(t,\frac{32\hat{D}}{\epsilon}\right)
contains at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
vertices of
T
; or

•

a graph
X
with maximum vertex degree at most
\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}
and
V(X)\subseteq T
, such that
|V(X)|=N^{1/\epsilon}
for some parameter
N\geq\frac{|T|^{\epsilon}}{32\rho^{\epsilon}}
, together with an an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
via paths of length at most
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta=2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}\cdot D^{\prime\prime}
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, such that
X
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(X)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{d}=2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{5}}
, with
\tilde{c}
being the constant used in the definition of the Hierarchical Support Structure.

The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
.

We provide the proof of
7.5
below, after we complete the proof of
Theorem

7.4
using it. Let
D^{\prime}=\frac{32\hat{D}}{\epsilon}
.
We apply the algorithm from
7.5
to the input graph
H
, with parameters
W,\hat{W},\mu
, and
\epsilon
remaining unchanged. If the algorithm from
7.5
returns a graph
X
, with its embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
into
H
, and a Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, then we return this graph, its embedding and the Hierarchical Support Structure as the outcome of the algorithm. Therefore, we assume from now on that the algorithm from
7.5
returned a collection
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{|T|}{16}
edges of
H
, such that, for every vertex
t\in T
,
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
contains at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
vertices of
T
.

Let
S^{\prime}
be the set of regular vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E^{\prime}
, so
|S^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|T|}{16}
.

Let
T^{*}=T\cup S^{\prime}
. Clearly,
T^{*}
remains a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut in
H
. Note that, if we identify a subset
S\subseteq T^{*}\setminus S^{\prime}
of vertices, with
B_{H\setminus(T^{*}\setminus S)}(S,2\hat{D})
containing fewer than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, then, from
7.2
,
T^{*}\setminus S
is a valid
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut in
H
. We can then delete
S
from
T^{*}
, and continue. Notice that
B_{H\setminus(T^{*}\setminus S)}(S,2\hat{D})\subseteq B_{H\setminus S^{\prime}}(S,2\hat{D})
, and so, in order to make progress, it is enough to compute a subset
S\subseteq T\setminus S^{\prime}
of terminals, such that
B_{H\setminus S^{\prime}}(S,2\hat{D})
contains fewer than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices. The main challenge is to execute such an algorithm efficiently. Let
H^{\prime}=H\setminus S^{\prime}
. Recall that we have defined a parameter
D^{\prime}=\frac{32\hat{D}}{\epsilon}
.

Our algorithm will consist of a number of iterations. In every iteration, we will select some terminal
t\in T
, and we will perform a weighted BFS in graph
H^{\prime}
from
t
, up to some depth (that is at least
2\hat{D}
, and at most
D^{\prime}/2
). If, over the course of this BFS procedure, we encounter fewer than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, then we can let
S\subseteq T\setminus S^{\prime}
be the set of all terminals that the BFS discovered, except for those that appear in the last few layers of the BFS. We are then guaranteed that
B_{H^{\prime}}(S,\hat{D})
contains fewer than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, so we can update the pseudocut
T^{*}
, by removing the vertices of
S
from it. We will also remove some edges from graph
H^{\prime}
, which no longer lie in sets
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,2\hat{D})
of the remaining terminals
t\in T^{*}
. Alternatively, if the BFS discovers more than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, then we are guaranteed, on the one hand, that
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
contains more than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, while on the other hand, the number of vertices of
T
lying in
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
is at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
, from
7.5
. We put the terminal
t
aside, and we will remove all terminals of
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
from the pool of terminals that need to be processed (but we do not update the pseudocut
T^{*}
). Intuitively, since
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
contains more than
\hat{W}/\rho
regular vertices, we will eventually put aside at most
\rho
terminals (more precisely, we will associate, with each terminal
t
that we put aside, a set
V(t)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
of regular vertices of cardinality at least
\hat{W}/\rho
, and we will ensure that all resulting sets
\left\{V(t)\mid t\in A\right\}
are mutually disjoint, where
A
is the set of terminals that we put aside). This ensures that the total number of terminals of
T
that are thus eliminated from the pool of terminals to be considered (while not being removed from the pseudocut
T^{*}
), is at most
|T|/4
. In turn, this ensures that the final pseudocut that we obtain has cardinality at most
|T|/4+|S^{\prime}|\leq|T|/2
, as required.

We now describe our algorithm in more detail. Denote
k=|T|
.
For convenience, we remove from
T
terminals that also lie in
S^{\prime}
. Note that this decreases the cardinality of
T
by at most
k/16
.
Throughout the algorithm, we maintain a set
T^{*}\subseteq T\cup S^{\prime}
of vertices, such that
T^{*}
is a valid
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut in
H
. We also maintain a partition of the set
T
of terminals into three subsets: set
T_{0}
containing the terminals that were removed from
T^{*}
(so
T^{*}\cap T_{0}=\emptyset
); set
T_{1}\subseteq T\cap T^{*}
containing the terminals that were removed from the pool of terminals to be processed; and set
T_{2}\subseteq T\cap T^{*}
containing all remaining terminals. We also maintain subgraph
H^{\prime\prime}\subseteq H^{\prime}
, a set
A\subseteq T_{1}
of
special terminals
, and for every special terminal
t\in A
, two subsets
V(t)
and
V^{\prime}(t)
of regular vertices of
H
, such that the following invariants hold.

I1.

for all
t\in A
,
V(t)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
,
V(t)\cap V(H^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
, and
|V(t)|\geq\hat{W}/\rho
;

I2.

for all
t\in A
,
V^{\prime}(t)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
;

I3.

for all
t,t^{\prime}\in A
, if
t\neq t^{\prime}
, then
V(t)\cap V(t^{\prime})=\emptyset
;

I4.

T_{1}\subseteq\bigcup_{t\in A}V^{\prime}(t)
; and

I5.

for every terminal
t\in T_{2}
, the subgraph of
H^{\prime}
induced by
B_{H^{\prime}}(t,2\hat{D})
is contained in
H^{\prime\prime}
.

At the beginning of the algorithm, we let
T^{*}=T\cup S^{\prime}
,
T_{0}=T_{1}=A=\emptyset
,
T_{2}=T
, and
H^{\prime\prime}=H^{\prime}
. It is easy to verify that all invariants hold. The algorithm then performs iterations, until
T_{2}=\emptyset
holds. We now describe a single iteration of the algorithm. We assume that all invariants hold at the beginning of the iteration.

Iteration description.

We let
t\in T_{2}
be any terminal. We apply the algorithm from

Lemma

2.1
to graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, terminal
t
, distance parameter
2\hat{D}
and precision parameter
\epsilon
remaining unchanged (so
k=0
and we do not supply subsets
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
of vertices). Let
1<i\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}

be the integer that the algorithm returns. Denote
\hat{B}(t)=B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,4(i-1)\hat{D})
, and
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)=B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,4i\hat{D})
. We now consider two cases.

The first case happens if the number of regular vertices in set
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)
is at most
\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}
. We then say that the current iteration is a type-1 iteration. Let
\hat{T}
be the collection of all terminals that lie in
T_{2}\cap B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,(4i-2)\hat{D})
. Then we are guaranteed that
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{T},2\hat{D})
contains at most
\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}
regular vertices. Notice also that, from Invariant
I5
,
for every terminal
t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
, the subgraph of
H^{\prime}
induced by
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},2\hat{D})
is contained in
H^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore,
B_{H^{\prime}}(\hat{T},2\hat{D})\subseteq B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{T},2\hat{D})
, and
B_{H^{\prime}}(\hat{T},2\hat{D})
contains at most
\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}
regular vertices. From

7.2
,
T^{*}\setminus\hat{T}
is a valid pseudocut. We update
T^{*}=T^{*}\setminus\hat{T}
, add the vertices of
\hat{T}
to
T_{0}
, and delete them from
T_{2}
. Notice that, for every vertex
t^{\prime}
that remains in
T_{2}
,
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},2\hat{D})
is disjoint from
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,4(i-1)\hat{D})
. Therefore, we can delete all edges of
E_{H^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{B}(t))
and all vertices of
\hat{B}(t)
from graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, without violating Invariant
I5
. If we denote by
E^{\prime}(t)=E_{H^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{B}(t))
, then the running time of the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}(t)|\cdot|E(H^{\prime\prime})|^{\epsilon}\cdot\log\hat{W})
. Since the edges of
E^{\prime}(t)
are deleted from graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, we can charge these edges for the running time of the current iteration. It is easy to verify that the remaining invariants continue to hold.

We now consider the second case, where the number of regular vertices in set
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)
is greater than
\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}
. We then say that the current iteration is a type-2 iteration.
In this case, we let
V(t)
be the set of all regular vertices lying in
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
, and we let
V^{\prime}(t)
be the set of all regular vertices lying in
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
. Since
D^{\prime}=\frac{32\hat{D}}{\epsilon}
, we are guaranteed that
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)\subseteq V(t)
, and so
|V(t)|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}
. Since graph
H^{\prime\prime}
is obtained from graph
H^{\prime}
by deleting edges and vertices from it, we are guaranteed that
V(t)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
and
V^{\prime}(t)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
. We add terminal
t
to set
A
, and we move all terminals in set
V^{\prime}(t)\cap T_{2}
from
T_{2}
to
T_{1}
. Lastly, we delete from
H^{\prime\prime}
all vertices of
V(t)
and edges that are incident to them. From our discussion, it is immediate to verify that Invariants
I1
,
I2
and
I4
continue to hold. Since, for every terminal
t^{\prime}
that lied in set
A
at the beginning of the iteration,
V(t^{\prime})\cap V(H^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
held from Invariant
I1
, it is immediate to verify that Invariant
I3
continues to hold. It now remains to verify that Invariant
I5
also continues to hold. Consider any terminal
t^{\prime}
that lies in set
T_{2}
at the end of the current itertation. From Invariant
I5
, the subgraph of
H^{\prime}
induced by
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},2\hat{D})
was contained in
H^{\prime\prime}
at the beginning of the iteration. Since terminal
t^{\prime}
remains in set
T_{2}
, at the beginning of the iteration,
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
did not contain
t^{\prime}
. Since we only deleted from
H^{\prime\prime}
vertices that lie in
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,D^{\prime}/2)
and their incident edges, and since
D^{\prime}>8\hat{D}
, we did not delete any vertices of
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},2\hat{D})=B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},2\hat{D})
, or edges connecting them from
H^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore, the subgraph of
H^{\prime}
induced by
B_{H^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},2\hat{D})
remains contained in
H^{\prime\prime}
. The running time of a type-2 iteration is bounded by
O(|E(H)|)
.

The algorithm terminates once
T_{2}=\emptyset
holds. Since, from Invariant
I3
, the sets
\left\{V(t)\mid t\in A\right\}
of vertices are all mutually disjoint, and since, from Invariant
I1
, each such set contains at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}
regular vertices, at the end of the algorithm
|A|\leq\rho
must hold. Moreover, since, from Invariant
I2
, for every terminal
t\in A
,
V^{\prime}(t)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
, and since, from
7.5
,

B_{H^{\prime}}\left(t,D^{\prime}\right)
contains at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
vertices of
T
, from Invariant
I4
, we conclude that
|T_{1}|\leq|A|\cdot\frac{k}{16\rho}\leq\frac{k}{16}
. Therefore, at the end of the algorithm,
|T_{0}|\geq|T|-\frac{k}{16}\geq\frac{14|T|}{16}
holds, and so:

|T^{*}|\leq|T|+|S^{\prime}|-|T_{0}|\leq\frac{|T|}{8}+\frac{|T|}{4}\leq\frac{|T|}{2}.

It now remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm. The running time of the algorithm from
7.5
is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
. Since, at the end of the algorithm
|A|\leq\rho
holds, the number of type-2 iterations is bounded by
\rho
, and each such iteration has running time
O(|E(H)|)
. If we consider a type-1 iteration in which a terminal
t
was processed, and we let
E_{t}
be the set of edges that were deleted from
H^{\prime\prime}
over the course of the iteration, then the running time of the iteration is
O(|E_{t}|\cdot|E(H)|^{\epsilon}\cdot\log\hat{W})
. Therefore, the total running time of all type-1 iterations is at most
O(|E(H)|^{1+\epsilon}\cdot\log\hat{W})
, and the total running time of all type-2 iterations is at most
O(|E(H)|\cdot\rho)
. Overall, the running time of the algorithm (excluding the running time of the algorithm from
7.5
) is bounded by
O(|E(H)|^{1+\epsilon}\cdot\rho\cdot\log\hat{W})\leq O(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\rho)
, and the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
.

In order to complete the proof of
Theorem

7.4
, it now remains to prove
7.5
.

Proof of
7.5

Denote
D^{\prime}=\frac{32\hat{D}}{\epsilon}
and
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{16D^{\prime}}{\epsilon}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon^{2}}
.
Throughout the proof, we refer to vertices of
T
as terminals.
The main idea of the algorithm is simple: we will maintain a set
E^{\prime}
of edges of
H
, starting from
E^{\prime}=\emptyset
. We will then iteratively identify terminals
t\in T
with
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime\prime})
containing more than
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
terminals, and apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
to the current graph
H\setminus E^{\prime}
and the set
T^{\prime}=T\cap B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime\prime})
of terminals, with parameter
d=D^{\prime\prime}
. If the algorithm computes a graph
X
, its embedding into
H
, and a Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, then we return this graph, its embedding, and the Hierarchical Support Structure as the outcome of the algorithm. In this case, we say that the application of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
was successful. Otherwise, the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
must return a relatively small subset
\tilde{E}
of edges, and two relatively large subsetes
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq T^{\prime}
of terminals, such that, if we add the edges of
\tilde{E}
to set
E^{\prime}
, then,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(T_{1},T_{2})>D^{\prime\prime}
holds. In this case, we say that the application of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
was unsuccessful. We prove that the number of unsuccessful applications of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
is relatively small; this is since, in each such application, there is a large number of terminals
t\in T
, for which
|T\cap B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime\prime})|
decreases significantly. This, in turn, ensures that, at the end of the algorithm,
|E^{\prime}|
remains sufficiently small.

The main challenge in implementing this idea is efficiency: if we implement this algorithm in a straightforward way, where in every iteration, we select some terminal
t\in T
, and check whether
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
contains many terminals, we may obtain an inefficient algorithm, since it is possible that for many such terminals, there are many edges connecting the vertices of
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
, but there are few terminals in
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
.

In order to overcome this difficulty, we will maintain a partition
(T^{A},T^{I})
of the set
T
of terminals, where the terminals in
T^{I}
are called
inactive
, and the terminals in
T^{A}
are called
active
. We also maintain a set
E^{\prime}
of edges, and a graph
H^{\prime\prime}\subseteq H\setminus E^{\prime}
. We ensure that the following invariants hold throughout the algorithm.

I’1.

for every inactive terminal
t\in T^{I}
,
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
contains at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
terminals of
T
;

I’2.

for every active terminal
t\in T^{A}
, if we denote by
S_{t}=B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
, then the subgraph of
H\setminus E^{\prime}
induced by
S_{t}
is contained in
H^{\prime\prime}
; and

I’3.

for all
i\geq 0
, after
i
applications of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
,
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{i\cdot|T|}{2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}}
holds.

Throughout, we denote
H^{\prime}=H\setminus E^{\prime}
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we let
E^{\prime}=\emptyset
,
H^{\prime\prime}=H
,
T^{A}=T
and
T^{I}=\emptyset
. It is easy to verify that all invariants hold. We then perform iterations, as long as
T^{A}\neq\emptyset
. We now describe the execution of a single iteration.

Execution of an iteration.

We select an arbitrary terminal
t\in T^{A}
. If
t
is an isolated vertex in
H^{\prime\prime}
, then we move
t
from
T^{A}
to
T^{I}
. It is immediate to verify that all invariants continue to hold. From now on, we assume that
t
is not an isolated vertex of
H^{\prime\prime}
.
We apply the algorithm from

Lemma

2.1
to graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, terminal
t
, distance parameter
2D^{\prime}
, and precision parameter
\epsilon
remaining unchanged (so
k=0
and we do not supply subsets
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
of vertices). Let
1<i\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}

be the integer that the algorithm returns. Denote
\hat{B}(t)=B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,4(i-1)D^{\prime})
, and
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)=B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,4iD^{\prime})
. We now consider two cases.

The first case happens if the number of terminals in set
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)
is at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
. We then say that the current iteration is a type-1 iteration. Let
\hat{T}
be the collection of all terminals that lie in
T^{A}\cap B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,(4i-2)D^{\prime})
. Then we are guaranteed that, for every terminal
t^{\prime}\in\hat{T}
,

B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
contains at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
terminals of
T
. Moreover, since, from
Invariant
I’2
, for each such terminal
t^{\prime}
,
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})=B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
, we get that
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, contains at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
terminals of
T
. We add the terminals of
\hat{T}
to set
T^{I}
of inactive terminals. Note that, for every terminal
t^{\prime}\in T^{A}
that remains active,
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is disjoint from
\hat{B}(t)
. Therefore, we can delete from
H^{\prime\prime}
all edges whose both endpoints lie in
\hat{B}(t)
, and all vertices that lie in
\hat{B}(t)
, without violating Invariant
I’2
. From our discussion, Invariant
I’1
continues to hold, and it is easy to verify that Invariant
I’3
does as well.
If we denote by
E^{\prime}(t)=E_{H^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{B}(t))
(where
H^{\prime\prime}
is the graph at the beginning of the iteration), then the running time of the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}(t)|\cdot|E(H^{\prime\prime})|^{\epsilon}\cdot\log\hat{W})
. Since the edges of
E^{\prime}(t)
are deleted from graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, we can charge these edges for the running time of the current iteration.

We now consider the second case, where the number of terminals in set
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)
is greater than
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
. We then say that the current iteration is a type-2 iteration. Let
\hat{T}
be the collection of all terminals of
T
that lie in
B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,D^{\prime\prime}/2)
. Since
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{16D^{\prime}}{\epsilon}
, while the integer
i
that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
returned is bounded by
2/\epsilon
, it is easy to verify that
\hat{B}^{\prime}(t)\subseteq B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t,D^{\prime\prime}/2)
, and so
|\hat{T}|\geq\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
. Notice that, for every terminal
t^{\prime}\in\hat{T}
,
|B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\cap T|\geq\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
.
We denote
k=|\hat{T}|
, so that
k\geq\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
.

Next, we will apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
to graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, set
\hat{T}
of termminals, distance paramter
d=D^{\prime\prime}
, and parameter
\epsilon
remaining unchanged. In order to be able to use the algorithm, we need to verify that

\frac{2}{(\log k)^{1/12}}<\epsilon<\frac{1}{400}
holds.

Recall that, from the statement of
Theorem

7.4
,
\frac{1}{(\log W)^{1/24}}\leq\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{400}
holds. Recall also that

|\hat{T}|=k\geq\frac{|T|}{16\rho}\geq W^{\epsilon}
(since
|T|\geq 64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}
).
Therefore,
\log k\geq\epsilon\log W\geq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{23}}
. We conclude that
(\log k)^{1/12}>\frac{2}{\epsilon}
, as required.

We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
to graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, the set
\hat{T}
of terminals, and parameter
\epsilon
remaining unchanged. We also use distance parameter
d=D^{\prime\prime}
, and congestion parameter
\eta^{*}=\frac{\eta}{\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}}
.
The running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}O\left(|T|^{1+O(\epsilon)}+|E(H)|\cdot|T|^{O(\epsilon^{3})}\cdot(\eta^{*}+D^{\prime\prime}\log\hat{W})\right)&\leq O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\mu\cdot\frac{\eta}{\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}}\cdot D^{\prime\prime}\cdot\log W\right)\\
&\leq O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\mu\cdot(D^{\prime\prime})^{2}\cdot\rho^{2}\cdot\log W\right)\\
&\leq O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\mu\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{2}\right).\end{split}

since
\eta=2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}\cdot D^{\prime\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}=\frac{512\hat{D}}{\epsilon}
. We have also used the fact that
512/\epsilon<\log W
, and that
W^{\epsilon}\geq\log^{2}W
from Inequality
3
.

We denote
N=\left\lfloor k^{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
, so
N\geq\left\lfloor\left(\frac{|T|}{16\rho}\right)^{\epsilon}\right\rfloor\geq\frac{|T|^{\epsilon}}{32\rho^{\epsilon}}
holds.
We now consider two cases. The first case happens if the algorithm from

Theorem

2.8
returned a graph
X
with
V(X)\subseteq\hat{T}
,
|V(X)|=N^{1/\epsilon}
, and maximum vertex degree at most
k^{32\epsilon^{3}}\leq\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}
, together with an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X
into
H
via paths of length at most
D^{\prime\prime}
, and cause congestion at most
\eta^{*}\cdot k^{32\epsilon^{3}}\leq\frac{\eta}{\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}}\cdot k^{32\epsilon^{3}}\leq\eta
, and a level-
(1/\epsilon)
Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
, such that
X
is
(\eta^{\prime},\tilde{d})
-well-connected with respect to the set
S(X)
of vertices defined by the support structure, where
\eta^{\prime}=N^{6+256\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{d}=2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{5}}
, with
\tilde{c}
being the constant used in the definition of the Hierarchical Support Structure.
In this case, we say that the current iteration is
successful
. We terminate the algorithm, and return the graph
X
, its embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
, and the Hierarchical Support Structure for
X
. As observed already,
N\geq\frac{|T|^{\epsilon}}{32\rho^{\epsilon}}
holds, so this is a valid output of the algorithm.

We assume from now on that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.8
returned a pair
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\hat{T}
of disjoint subsets of terminals, and a set
\tilde{E}
of edges of
H^{\prime\prime}
, such that
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
, and:

|T_{1}|\geq\frac{k^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{4}\geq\frac{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{64\rho}.

(since
k\geq\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
). We say that the current iteration is unsuccessful.
Recall that the algorithm also guarantees that:

\begin{split}|\tilde{E}|&\leq\frac{D^{\prime\prime}\cdot|T_{1}|}{\eta^{*}}\\
&\leq\frac{|T|\cdot D^{\prime\prime}\cdot\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}}{\eta}\\
&\leq\frac{|T|}{2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}},\end{split}

(since
\eta=2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{64\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}\cdot D^{\prime\prime})
.

Recall also that, for every pair
t\in T_{1},t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime\prime}\setminus\tilde{E}}(t,t^{\prime})>D^{\prime\prime}
holds.
We add the edges of
\tilde{E}
to set
E^{\prime}
, delete them from
H^{\prime\prime}
, and continue to the next iteration.
It is immediate to verify that Invariant
I’1
continues to hold. Since the changes to graphs
H\setminus E^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime\prime}
were identical, Invariant
I’2
also continues to hold. Lastly, since
|\tilde{E}|\leq\frac{|T|}{2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}}
, Invariant
I’3
continues to hold.

This completes the description of a single iteration, and of the algorithm. As mentioned already, the algorithm terminates once
|T^{A}|=\emptyset
holds. From Invariant
I’1
, at the end of the algorithm, for every terminal
t\in T
,
B_{H\setminus E^{\prime}}(t,D^{\prime})
contains at most
\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
terminals of
T
.

In order to analyze the algorithm, we need the following observation.

Observation 7.6
.

The number type-2 iterations of the algorithm is bounded by
2^{13}\rho^{2}\cdot|T|^{8\epsilon^{3}}
.

Proof.

Recall that at the beginning of each type-2 iteration
i
, we are given a set
\hat{T}\subseteq T
of terminals, such that
|\hat{T}|\geq\frac{|T|}{16\rho}
, and, for every terminal
t^{\prime}\in\hat{T}
,
\hat{T}\subseteq B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\cap T
.

At the end of iteration
i
, we obtain two subsets
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\hat{T}
of terminals, such that, once graph
H^{\prime\prime}
is updated (by deleting the edges of
\tilde{E}
form it),
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime\prime}}(T_{1},T_{2})>D^{\prime\prime}
holds. Recall also that

|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|\geq\frac{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{64\rho}
. Therefore, during iteration
i
, there are at least
\frac{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{64\rho}
terminals
t^{\prime}\in T_{1}
, such that, for each terminal
t^{\prime}\in T_{1}
,
|B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\cap T|
decreased by at least
\frac{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{64\rho}
.

Let
t\in T
be a terminal, and let
i
be any type-2 iteration. We say that terminal
t
is hit in iteration
i
, if, over the course of iteration
i
,
|B_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\cap T|
decreased by at least
\frac{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{64\rho}
. Clearly, for a fixed terminal
t
, the number of iterations in which
t
may be hit is bounded by
64\rho\cdot|T|^{4\epsilon^{3}}
. Let
\Pi
be the collection of pairs
(i,t)
, where
i
is a type-2 iteration, and terminal
t
was hit during iteration
i
. Then
|\Pi|\leq 64|T|^{1+4\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho
. From the above discussion, in each unsuccessful type-2 iteration, the number of terminals that are hit is at least
\frac{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}{64\rho}
. Therefore, the total number of unsuccessful type-2 iterations is bounded by:

\frac{|\Pi|}{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}/(64\rho)}\leq\frac{2^{12}\cdot|T|^{1+4\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}}{|T|^{1-4\epsilon^{3}}}\leq 2^{12}\rho^{2}\cdot|T|^{8\epsilon^{3}}.
