Since there is at most one successful type-2 iteration, we get that the total number of type-2 iterations is bounded by
2^{13}\rho^{2}\cdot|T|^{8\epsilon^{3}}
.

From Invariant
I’3
, at the end of the algorithm:

|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|T|}{2^{20}\cdot\hat{W}^{32\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\rho^{2}}\cdot 2^{13}\rho^{2}\cdot|T|^{8\epsilon^{3}}\leq\frac{|T|}{16},

as required.

It now remains to bound the running time of the algorithm. Recall that the running time of a single type-2 iteration is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{2}\cdot\mu\right)
.
Since the number of type-2 iterations is at most
O(\rho^{2}\cdot|T|^{8\epsilon^{3}})\leq O(\rho^{2}\cdot\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)})
, we get that the total time spent on type-2 iterations is at most

O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right)
.

Recall that in each type-1 iteration, we delete some subset
E^{\prime}(t)
of edges from graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, and the running time of the iteration is bounded by
O(|E^{\prime}(t)|\cdot|E(H^{\prime\prime})|^{\epsilon}\cdot\log\hat{W})\leq O(|E^{\prime}(t)|\cdot\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot\log\hat{W})
. Therefore, the total running time spent on type-1 iterations is bounded by
O\left(|E(H^{\prime\prime})|\cdot\hat{W}^{O(\epsilon)}\right)\leq O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\mu\right)
. We conclude that the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{2}\cdot\rho^{4}\cdot\mu\right).

7.3
Stage 2: Medium-Size Pseudocut

The second stage of the algorithm is only executed if
64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}>\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
, and it only lasts as long as
|T\cap V(H)|>\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
. Recall that, at the beginning of Stage 2,
|T|\leq 64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}
holds.
Throughout the algorithm, we denote by
T^{\prime}=T\cap V(H)
.
We partition the second stage into phases. At the beginning of every phase, we select an arbitrary vertex
t\in T\cap V(H)
, and initialize the modified
ES-Tree
data structure from
Theorem

2.6
in graph
H
, with source vertex
t
, and depth parameter
D^{*}
. Since supernode-splitting operations are a special case of vertex-splitting, we can think of graph
H
as undergoing an online sequence of edge-deletion, isolated vertex-deletion and vertex-splitting updates. Notice that vertex-splitting may not be applied to vertex
t
, since it is not a supernode. Recall that the total update time of the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
is

O(m^{*}\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log m^{*})
, where
m^{*}
is the total number of edges that ever belonged to graph
H
. Since
m^{*}\leq\hat{W}\cdot\mu
and
\mu\leq\hat{W}^{0.1}\leq W^{0.2}
, the total update time is bounded by
O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log W)
.

Recall that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
maintains a set
S^{*}
of vertices of
H
, such that, for every vertex
x\in S^{*}
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,x)>D^{*}
holds. Whenever a regular vertex
v
is added to set
S^{*}
, we raise the flag
F_{H}
, and provide
(t,v)
as a witness pair. Notice that it is possible that, during the flag-lowering update sequence, vertex
t
is deleted from
H
. In any case, the phase terminates once
t
is deleted from
H
. When
|T\cap V(H)|
falls below
\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
, the second stage terminates.

It is easy to verify that the number of phases in the second stage is bounded by
64W^{\epsilon}\rho\mu^{2}
, and, since the total update time of every phase is bounded by
O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log W)
, we get that the total update time of the algorithm for Stage 2 is bounded by
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot D^{*}\cdot\rho\cdot\mu^{3}\right)
.

Responding to Queries

We now provide an algorithm for supporting queries
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(H,v,v^{\prime})
over the course of Stage 2. Suppose we are given a query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(H,v,v^{\prime})
, where
v,v^{\prime}\in V(H)
are regular vertices of
H
. Recall that our goal is to return a path
P
of length at most
\alpha^{\prime}\cdot D^{*}
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
H
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Recall that a
short-path-query
cannot be asked while the flag
F_{H}
is up. Therefore, flag
F_{H}
is currently down, and the set
S^{*}
of vertices that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
maintains contains no regular vertices. Vertices
v,v^{\prime}
must then lie in the
ES-Tree
that the algorithm maintain. We perform query
\mbox{\sf{SSSP-query}}(v)
and
\mbox{\sf{SSSP-query}}(v^{\prime})
in the data structure maintained by the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
, that must return a path
P_{1}
connecting
v
to
t
in
H
, and a path
P_{2}
connecting
v^{\prime}
to
t
in
H
of length at most
D^{*}
each, in time
O(|E(P_{1})|+|E(P_{2})|)
. By concatenating the two paths, we obtain a path
P
in graph
H
, connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
, of length at most
2D^{*}\leq\alpha^{\prime}\cdot D^{*}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

7.4
Stage 3: Small Pseudocut Regime

Recall that, at the beginning of Stage 3, we are given a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut
T
, with
|T|\leq\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
. This pseudocut will remain fixed over the course of Stage 3, except that, whenever vertices of
T
are deleted from
H
, we delete them from
T
as well. Throughout the remainder of the algorithm, we refer to the vertices of
T
as terminals, and we denote
T^{\prime}=T\cap V(H)
. Clearly, set
T^{\prime}
remains a
(\hat{D},\rho)
-pseudocut for
H
in the remainder of the algorithm.

The algorithm for Stage 3 is almost identical to that from
[
Chu21
]
, with only slight technical differences, due to the slightly different definition of a pseudocut, and the specifics of the statement of
5.1
.
We start by providing a high-level overview of the algorithm.
We define a modified graph
\tilde{H}
, that will be more convenient for us to use. Intuitively, graph
\tilde{H}
is obtained from graph
H
by “splitting” every terminal
t\in T
into a number of “fake terminals”, that we refer to as
copies of
t
. We will ensure that, for every pair
t,t^{\prime}\in T
of terminals, if there is a short path
P
connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
in
H
, such that no inner vertices of
P
are terminals, then there is a path
P^{\prime}
connecting a copy of
t
to a copy of
t^{\prime}
in
\tilde{H}
, of a similar length. However, graph
\tilde{H}
will have one additional useful property: for every regular vertex
v
of
\tilde{H}
,
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,\hat{D})
only contains a relatively small number of regular vertices (from the definition of a pseudocut, this property holds for graph
H\setminus T
, but it does not necessarily hold for graph
H
). This property will allow us to construct a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of vertex-induced subgraphs of
\tilde{H}
, each of which contains at most
W
regular vertices, such that, for every fake terminal
\tilde{t}
, there is some graph
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, with
B_{\tilde{H}}(\tilde{t},\hat{D})
contained in
C
. While the graphs in
{\mathcal{C}}
are not necessarily disjoint, we will ensure that every regular vertex of
H
belongs to few such graphs.
For all
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, let
D_{i}=2^{i}
.
Since each graph
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
contains at most
W
regular vertices,
for all
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, we can use the algorithm from
5.1
in order to solve the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on graph
C
, with distance parameter
D_{i}
. We denote by
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}^{C}
the resulting collection of clusters that the algorithm maintains.

Next, we construct another dynamic graph
\hat{H}
, that we call a
contracted
graph. The regular vertices of
\hat{H}
correspond to the terminals of
T^{\prime}
, while supernodes correspond to the clusters of
\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\bigcup_{i\geq 0}{\mathcal{R}}_{i}^{C}
. We will show that distances between pairs of terminals in
\hat{H}
are approximately equal to the distances between the same terminals in graph
H
.
Once graph
\hat{H}
is initialized, as the clusters in the neighborhood covers
\left\{{\mathcal{R}}_{i}^{C}\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}},i\geq 0}
evolve, graph
\hat{H}
will undergo corresponding updates, that will be implemented via valid update operations.
Since
|T|<W
, we can again use the algorithm from
5.1
, in order to maintain a neighborhood cover
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
for the contracted graph
\hat{H}
, with distance parameter roughly comparable to
D^{*}
. This data structure allows us to correctly establish when the distance between a pair of terminals becomes sufficiently large, so we can raise flag
F_{H}
whenever this happens. The data structure also allows us to respond to
short-path-query
queries between pairs of terminals.

In addition to the above data structures, we will maintain an
ES-Tree
in graph
H
, that is rooted at the vertices of
T^{\prime}
. This data structure will allow us to correctly establish when a regular vertex is too far from the terminals, and to support
short-path-query
queries between pairs of regular vertices of
H
.

This completes the high-level description of the algorithm for Stage 3.
We now describe all data structures that we maintain. We start with the modified graph
\tilde{H}
, and then describe its decomposition into a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters. We then define the contracted graph
\hat{H}
and analyze its properties. Lastly, we provide all additional data structures that we maintain, analyze the total update time of the algorithm, and provide an algorithm for responding to
short-path-query
queries. We let
{\mathcal{T}}
denote the time horizon associated with Stage 3, and whenever we refer to “initial” graph
H
, we refer to graph
H
at the beginning of Stage 3.

7.4.1
Modified Graph
\tilde{H}
and Its Properties

At the beginnning of Stage 3, we construct a modified graph
\tilde{H}
from the initial graph
H
, as follows. We process every terminal
t\in T
one by one. When terminal
t
is processed, for ever edge
e\in\delta_{H}(t)
, we subdivide
e
with a new vertex
\tilde{t}_{e}
, that we call a
copy
of
t
; the lengths of the two resulting edges are both set to be
\ell_{H}(e)
. Once very edge incident to
t
in
H
is subdivided, we obtain a collection
F(t)=\left\{\tilde{t}_{e}\mid e\in\delta_{H}(t)\right\}
of copies of
t
; since the dynamic degree bound for
H
is
\mu
,
|F(t)|\leq\mu
must hold. We denote
F=\bigcup_{t\in T}F(t)
, and we refer to the vertices of
F
as
fake terminals
. We then delete all terminals of
T
from the resulting graph, obtaining the initial modified graph
\tilde{H}
.
The fake terminals and the regular vertices of
V(H)\setminus T
become regular vertices in graph
\tilde{H}
, while the supernodes of
H
remain supernode vertices for graph
\tilde{H}
. Clearly, every edge of
\tilde{H}
connects a regular vertex to a supernode.

As graph
H
undergoes valid update operations, we perform similar valid update operations in graph
\tilde{H}
, that we describe below. Throughout, we will ensure that, for every terminal
t\in T
, for every edge
e=(t,u)
that is incident to
t
in the current graph
H
, there is a corresponding edge
e^{\prime}
in the current graph
\tilde{H}
connecting a copy of
t
to
u
, whose length is
\ell_{H}(e)
. This correspondence is a bijection: every edge incident to a terminal in
H
corresponds to a unique edge incident to a copy of the same terminal in
F
and vice versa. We also maintain bi-directional pointers between such pairs of edges.
Additinally, if
e
is an edge of
H
whose endpoints are non-terminal vertices, then
e
must also be present in
\tilde{H}
. Similarly, every edge of
\tilde{H}
whose endpoints are not in
F
must be present in
H
.
At the beginning of the algorithm, if
e=(t,u)
is an edge of
H
that is incident to a terminal
t\in T
, then its corresponding edge in
\tilde{H}
is the edge
(\tilde{t}_{e},u)
, that was obtained by subdividing
e
. We now show an algorithm that, given a valid update operation
\sigma\in\Sigma
for graph
H
, performs a corresponding update in graph
\tilde{H}
.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma
be a valid update operation for graph
H
.
Assume first that
\sigma
is the deletion of an edge
e=(u,v)
, where
u
is a supernode. If
v\not\in T
, then we delete edge
e
from
\tilde{H}
as well. Otherwise, we locate the unique edge
e^{\prime}
that corresponds to
e
in
\tilde{H}
; this edge must connect a copy of
v
to
u
. We delete edge
e^{\prime}
from
\tilde{H}
.
Assume now that
\sigma
is the deletion of an isolated vertex
v
from
H
. If
v\not\in T
, then we delete
v
from
\tilde{H}
. Otherwise, we delete every copy of terminal
v
from
\tilde{H}
.

Lastly, assume that
\sigma
is a supernode-splitting operation, that is applied to supernode
u\in V(H)
, with a set
E^{\prime}\subseteq\delta_{H}(u)
of edges. We will apply the corresponding supernode-splitting operation to supernode
u
in
\tilde{H}
, but we may need to slightly modify the set
E^{\prime}
of edges. In order to do so, we construct a set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges in graph
\tilde{H}
, that correspond to the edges of
E^{\prime}
. We consider the edges
e\in E^{\prime}
one by one. Let
e=(u,v)
be any such edge, where
u
is a supernode. If
v\not\in T
, then we include edge
e
in
E^{\prime\prime}
. Otherwise, we include in
E^{\prime\prime}
the unique edge
\tilde{e}
of
\tilde{H}
corresponding to
e
, that must connect a copy of
v
to
u
. Once we process every edge in
E^{\prime}
, we obtain the final set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges, and we apply a supernode-splitting operation to vertex
u
in graph
\tilde{H}
, with the set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges. Recall that, as the result, a new supernode
u^{\prime}
is inserted into
\tilde{H}
.
Similarly, the supernode-splitting operation in graph
H
inserts the same supernode
u^{\prime}
into
H
. Consider now any edge
e=(u,v)\in E^{\prime}
.
If
v\not\in T
, then edge
(u^{\prime},v)
is inserted into both
H
and
\tilde{H}
. Assume now that
v\in T
. Recall that edge
\tilde{e}
of
\tilde{H}
, that corresponds to edge
e
, was added to set
E^{\prime\prime}
; this edge connects some copy
t^{\prime}
of terminal
v
to
u
. Edge
e^{\prime}=(v,u^{\prime})
was inserted into graph
H
, and edge
\tilde{e}^{\prime}=(t^{\prime},u^{\prime})
was inserted into graph
\tilde{H}
via the supenode-splitting operations. We make
\tilde{e}^{\prime}
be the edge of
\tilde{H}
corresponding to edge
e^{\prime}
of
H
, and we add pointers between the two edges in both directions.

This completes the definition of the dynamic graph
\tilde{H}
. Note that
\tilde{H}
is a bipartite graph, where one side of the bipartition contains all supernodes of
H
, and the other side contains regular vertices of
V(H)\setminus T
, and the fake terminals. In other words, we will view the fake terminals as regular vertices of
\tilde{H}
.
Therefore, we can view
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}=\left(\tilde{H},\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(\tilde{H})},D\right)
as a valid input structure, with graph
\tilde{H}
undergoing an online sequence
\tilde{\Sigma}
of valid update operations. It is easy to verify that the dynamic degree bound of
(\tilde{H},\tilde{\Sigma})
remains at most
\mu
. The number of regular vertices in
\tilde{H}
is bounded by the number of regular vertices in
H
plus
|F|\leq\mu\cdot|T|\leq\frac{W}{4\mu}
. Therefore, the total number of regular vertices in
\tilde{H}
is at most
N^{0}(H)+\frac{W}{4\mu}
.
Since
(H,\Sigma)
has dynamic degree bound
\mu
, the number of edges that are ever present in
H
is at most
N^{0}(H)\cdot\mu\leq\hat{W}\cdot\mu
, and so the number of edges that are ever present in
\tilde{H}
is also at most
\hat{W}\cdot\mu
.

From the discussion so far, there is a deterministic algorithm, that, given the initial graph
H
, initializes the initial graph
\tilde{H}
, in time
O(|E(H)|)
. Given the online sequence
\Sigma
of valid update operations for
H
, the algorithm also produces the corresponding online update sequence
\tilde{\Sigma}
for
\tilde{H}
, in time that is asymptotically bounded by the length of the description of
\Sigma
.

The following two observations easily follow the definition of graph
\tilde{H}
, and the bijection between the edges of
E(H)
and
E(\tilde{H})
that we defined.

Observation 7.7
.

Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
be any time during the time horizon, let
t,t^{\prime}\in T
be a pair of terminals, and let
P
be a path in graph
H^{(\tau)}
connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
, whose length is denoted by
D^{\prime}
, such that no inner vertex of
T
is a terminal. Then there is a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
\tilde{H}^{(\tau)}
connecting a copy of
t
to a copy of
t^{\prime}
, such that the length of
P^{\prime}
is at most
D^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Denote
P=(t,z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{a},t^{\prime})
. Observe that
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
must be supernodes, that we denote by
u
and
u^{\prime}
, respectively. For all
1\leq i<a
, we let
e_{i}=(z_{i},z_{i+1})
. From the definition of graph
\tilde{H}
, each such edge
e_{i}
is present in graph
\tilde{H}^{(\tau)}
. Moreover, there is an edge
e_{0}
in
\tilde{H}^{(\tau)}
, connecting some copy
\tilde{t}
of
t
to supernode
u=z_{1}
, and there is an edge
e_{r}
, connecting supernode
u^{\prime}=z_{a}
to some copy
\tilde{t}^{\prime}
of
t^{\prime}
. By concatenating edges
e_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
, we obtain a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
\tilde{H}
, that connects a copy
\tilde{t}
of terminal
t
to a copy
\tilde{t}^{\prime}
of terminal
t^{\prime}
. The length of the path is the same as the length of path
P
.

The next observation is immediate from our discussion.

Observation 7.8
.

Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
be any time during the time horizon, let
t,t^{\prime}\in T
be a pair of terminals, and let
P^{\prime}=(\tilde{t},z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{a},\tilde{t}^{\prime})
be a path in graph
\tilde{H}^{(\tau)}
connecting a copy
\tilde{t}
of terminal
t
, to a copy
\tilde{t}^{\prime}
of terminal
t^{\prime}
, whose length is denoted by
D^{\prime}
. Consider a sequence
S=(t,z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{a},t^{\prime})
of vertices of
H^{(\tau)}
, that is obtained from the sequence
\tilde{S}=(\tilde{t},z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{a},\tilde{t}^{\prime})
of vertices of
\tilde{H}^{(\tau)}
, by replacing every fake terminal with the corresponding original terminal from
T
. Then sequence
S
defines a path in graph
H^{(\tau)}
, that connects
t
to
t^{\prime}
, and has length
D^{\prime}
.

Lastly, the following observation bounds the number of regular vertices in balls of radius
\hat{D}/2
in graph
\tilde{H}
.

Observation 7.9
.

Let
v
be a regular vertex in
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
. If
v\not\in F
, then the number of regular vertices in
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,\hat{D})
is bounded by
W/2
, and if
v\in F
, then the number of regular vertices in
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,\hat{D}/2)
is bounded by
W/2
.

Proof.

Let
v
be a regular vertex of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
. Assume first that
v\not\in F
. Denote
S=B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,\hat{D})
, and let
G
be the subgraph of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
induced by
S
. Let
F^{\prime}=F\cap S
. Since
|T|\leq\frac{W}{4\mu^{2}}
holds, we get that
|F^{\prime}|\leq|F|\leq|T|\cdot\mu\leq\frac{W}{4\mu}
.

From the definition of graph
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
, every vertex of
F
has degree
1
in
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
.
Let
G^{\prime}=G\setminus F
. Then the distance from
v
to every vertex of
G^{\prime}
remains at most
\hat{D}
in
G^{\prime}
.

Let
H^{\prime}=H^{(0)}\setminus T
. From the definition of graph
\tilde{H}
,
G^{\prime}\subseteq H^{\prime}
, and so
V(G^{\prime})\subseteq B_{H^{\prime}}(v,\hat{D})
.

From the definition of a pseudocut,
B_{H^{\prime}}(v,\hat{D})
contains at most
\frac{\hat{W}}{\rho}\leq\frac{W}{4}
regular vertices. We conclude that the number of regular vertices in
S\setminus F^{\prime}=V(G^{\prime})
is bouded by
\frac{W}{4}
. Since
|F^{\prime}|\leq\frac{W}{4}
, we conclude that the number of regular vertices in
S
is bounded by
\frac{W}{2}
.

Assume now that
v\in F
, and let
u
be the unique neighbor of
v
in
\tilde{H}
. If all neighbors of
u
in
\tilde{H}
lie in
F
, then
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,\hat{D}/2)\subseteq F
(as all vertices of
F
have degree
1
in
\tilde{H}
), and so the number of regular vertices in
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,\hat{D}/2)
is bounded by
|F|\leq\frac{W}{4}
. Otherwise, at least one regular vertex
v^{\prime}\not\in F
is a neighbor of
u
. Since the length of every edge in
\tilde{H}
is bounded by
D\leq D^{*}<\hat{D}/8
, we get that
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,\hat{D}/2)\subseteq B_{\tilde{H}}(v^{\prime},\hat{D})
, and so the number of regular vertices in
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,\hat{D})
is bounded by
W/2
.

7.4.2
Constructing the Collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of Subgraphs of
\tilde{H}

We provide an algorithm that computes a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of vertex-induced subgraphs (that we call clusters) of the initial graph
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
, that have some useful properties. First, we ensure that the number of regular vertices in each cluster of
{\mathcal{C}}
is bounded by
W
. We also ensure that every regular vertex of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
lies in at most
W^{\epsilon/8}
such clusters. Lastly, we ensure that, for every regular vertex
v\in V(\tilde{H}^{(0)})
, some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
contains all vertices of
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,D^{*})
. The main result of this subsection is summarized in the following claim.

Claim 7.10
.

There is a deterministic algorithm that computes a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of vertex-induced subgraphs (clusters) of graph
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
with the following properties:

•

for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the number of regular vertices (including fake terminals) in
C
is at most
W
;

•

every regular vertex
v\in V(\tilde{H}^{(0)})
belongs to at most
W^{\epsilon/8}
graphs in
{\mathcal{C}}
; and

•

for every regular vertex
v
of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
, there is at least one cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, with
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(C)
.

Additionally, the algorithm computes, for every regular vertex
v
of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
a cluster
C(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}
with
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(C(v))
.
The running time of the algorithm is
O\left((\hat{W}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon}\right)
.

Proof.

We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.3
to graph
G=\tilde{H}^{(0)}
, where the set of terminals is the set of regular vertices of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
(which includes the set
F
of fake terminals). The precision parameter
\epsilon
remains unchanged, and the distance parameters are
D=D^{*}
and
D^{\prime}=\hat{D}/2
. Since
\hat{D}=\frac{2^{14}\cdot D^{*}}{\epsilon^{2}}
, we are guaranteed that
D^{\prime}\geq\frac{2^{13}\cdot D}{\epsilon^{2}}
. Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be the collection of clusters that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.3
computes.

The algorithm from
Theorem

2.3
ensures that, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, there is some regular vertex
v
of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
, with
V(C)\subseteq B_{H^{(0)}}(v,\hat{D}/2)
. From
7.9
, the number of regular vertices in each cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
is bounded by
W/2
. Recall that the number of regular vertices in graph
\tilde{H}
is bounded by
|F|+N^{0}(H)\leq\hat{W}\cdot\mu\leq W^{1.6}
. The algorithm from
Theorem

2.3
guarantees that every regular vertex of
\tilde{H}
belongs to at most
\frac{64(W^{1.6})^{\epsilon/16}}{\epsilon}\leq W^{\epsilon/8}
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
. It also computes, for every regular vertex
v
of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
, a cluster
C(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}
, with
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(C(v))
. Since the number of regular vertices in
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
is bounded by
\hat{W}\cdot\mu
, and
|E(\tilde{H}^{(0)})|\leq\hat{W}\cdot\mu
, the running time of the algorithm is bounded by

O\left((\hat{W}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon}\right)
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be the collection of clusters that the algorithm from
7.10
computed. As graph
\tilde{H}
undergoes the sequence
\tilde{\Sigma}
of valid update operations, we update each of the clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
accordingly, as described in Section
3.2.2
. Therefore, online sequence
\tilde{\Sigma}
of valid update operations to graph
\tilde{H}
naturally defines, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, an online sequence
\Sigma_{C}
of valid update operations to cluster
C
. Furthermore, since every regular vertex of
H
belongs to at most
W^{\epsilon/8}
graphs in
{\mathcal{C}}
, it is easy to see that there is a deterministic algorithm that, given an update
\sigma
ot graph
\tilde{H}
, produces the corresponding updates to graphs of
{\mathcal{C}}
, whose total running time is bounded by the length of the description of sequence
\tilde{\Sigma}
times
O(W^{\epsilon/8})
.

Consider now some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. We have now defined a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}_{C}=\left(C,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(C)},D\right)
associated with cluster
C
, and an online sequence
\Sigma_{C}
of valid update operations that cluster
C
undergoes. It is easy to verify that, since the dynamic degree bound of
(H,\Sigma)
is bounded by
\mu
, the dynamic degree bound of
(C,\Sigma_{C})
is bounded by
\mu
as well. Recall that the number of regular vertices in
C
is bounded by
W
. Notice that, from
3.7
,
for every regular vertex
v
of
\tilde{H}^{(0)}
, if
C(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}
is the cluster computed by
7.10
, then
at all times
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
,
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(C(v))
holds.

Consider again some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
For all
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, we can define a valid input structure

{\mathcal{J}}^{C}_{i}=\left(C_{i},\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(C_{i})},D_{i}\right)
associated with graph
C
and distance parameter
D_{i}=2^{i}
in a natural way: initially, graph
C_{i}
is obtained from
C
by deleting all edges whose length is greater than
D_{i}
. As graph
C
undergoes valid update operations, we perform similar update operations in graph
C_{i}
, but we ignore all edges whose length is greater than
D_{i}
; such edges do not need to be deleted from
C_{i}
, and we never insert such edges via supernode-splitting operations. We can now view
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}^{C}
, together with the online sequence of valid update operations that graph
C_{i}
undergoes, as an input to the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem. Since the number of regular vertices in
C_{i}
is bounded by
W
, we can apply the algorithm from
5.1
to this instance of
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
. We denote by
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}^{C}
the collection of clusters (the neighborhood cover of
C_{i}
with distance parameter
D_{i}
) that this data structure maintains, and we denote the data structure itself by
\mathsf{DS}_{i}^{C}
. For every regular vertex
v\in V(C)
, we denote by
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}_{i}^{C}(v)
the cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
that the data structure maintains. We also refer to queries
short-path-query
that the data structure supports as
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}_{i}^{C}
.
We denote all data structures
\left\{\mathsf{DS}_{i}^{C}\right\}_{0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}}
by
\mathsf{DS}^{C}
, and
{\mathcal{R}}^{C}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\log D^{*}}{\mathcal{R}}_{i}^{C}
.
Lastly, we denote by
\mathsf{DS}
all data structures in
\left\{DS^{C}\mid C\in{\mathcal{C}}\right\}
, and we denote by
{\mathcal{R}}=\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{R}}^{C}
.

Let
N^{0}(C)
denote the number of regular vertices in the initial graph
C
.
From
5.1
, for all
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
the total update time
of data structure
\mathsf{DS}_{i}^{C}
is bounded by
O\left(N^{0}(C)\cdot W^{\delta}\cdot D_{i}^{3}\cdot\mu^{c}\right)
. The total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{C}
is then bounded by
O\left(N^{0}(C)\cdot W^{\delta}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{c}\right)
. Since every regular vertex of
H
may lie in at most
W^{\epsilon/8}
graphs in
{\mathcal{C}}
, we get that
\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}N^{0}(C)\leq\hat{W}\cdot W^{\epsilon/8}
, and so the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}
is bounded by:

\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}O\left(N^{0}(C)\cdot W^{\delta}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{c}\right)\leq O\left(\hat{W}\cdot W^{\delta+\epsilon/8}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{c}\right).

For convenience, we denote
\Delta(W)
by
\Delta
.
Recall that the algorithm from
5.1
ensures that, for all
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, every regular vertex of
C
may lie in at most
\Delta
clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}^{C}_{i}
during the time horizon
{\mathcal{T}}
. Therefore, every regular vertex of
C
may belong to at most
2\Delta\log D^{*}
clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}^{C}
over the course of the time interval
{\mathcal{T}}
. Since every regular vertex of
\tilde{H}
lies in at most
W^{\epsilon/8}
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
, we conclude that, for every regular vertex
v\in V(\tilde{H})
, the number of clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}
to which it ever belongs during time horizon
{\mathcal{T}}
is bounded by
2W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\log D^{*}
.

7.4.3
Contracted graph
\hat{H}

In this subsection we define a dynamic graph
\hat{H}
, that we call a
contracted graph
, and analyze some of its properties. The definition of the graph and the analysis are very similar to those in
[
Chu21
]
, with some small technical differences.

Consider any time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
during the time horizon. Graph
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
is defined as follows. The set of vertices of
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
is the union of two subsets: set of regular vertices and set of supernodes. The set of regular vertices of
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
is the set
T^{\prime}=T\cap V(H^{(\tau)})
of terminals. The set of supernodes is
\left\{u(R)\mid R\in{\mathcal{R}}\emph{and}V(R)\cap F\neq\emptyset\right\}
.
For every cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, if
R\in{\mathcal{R}}^{C}_{i}
for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
an integer
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
, then we say that the
scale
of cluster
R
is
i
, and we denote
\operatorname{scale}(R)=i
.

We now define the edges of
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
. Consider some cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, and assume that
\operatorname{scale}(R)=i
. For every terminal
t\in T
, such that some copy of
t
lies in
R
, we add an edge
(t,u(R))
of length
2^{i}
to graph
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
.
For every edge
e=(t,u(R))
of graph
\hat{H}
, we maintain a list
L(e)
of all fake terminals
\tilde{t}\in V(R)
that are copies of terminal
t
, and a counter
n_{e}=|L(e)|
.

Maintaining graph
\hat{H}
.

At the begining of Stage 3, after we compute the modified graph
\tilde{H}
, the initial set
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters of
\tilde{H}
, and the initial collection
{\mathcal{R}}
of clusters, we initialize the graph
\hat{\mathcal{H}}^{(0)}
, the lists
L(e)
and the counters
n_{e}
for all edges
e
of
\hat{\mathcal{H}}^{(0)}
in a straightforward way. The time required to initialize the graph is asymptotically bounded by the time that is required to compute the initial collections
{\mathcal{C}}
,
{\mathcal{R}}
of clusters.

Next, we show that graph
\hat{H}
can be maintained correctly via valid update operations. Graph
\hat{H}
may only need to be updated in one of the following cases: (i) some terminal
t\in T
is deleted from
H
via isolated vertex deletion update; or (ii) some fake terminal
\tilde{t}
is deleted from some cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
; or (iii) a new cluster is inserted into set
{\mathcal{R}}
via a cluster-splitting update. We now consider each of these events in turn.

Assume first that some terminal
t\in T
is deleted from
H
via isolated vertex deletion update. Then terminal
t
is currently an isolated vertex in
H
, and so all fake terminals that are copies of
t
are also isolted vertices in
\tilde{H}
. All such fake terminals are deleted from graph
\tilde{H}
, and from clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
, which, in turn, leads to the deletion of all copies of
t
from the clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}
. We delete all edges incident to
t
from graph
\hat{H}
, and then delete terminal
t
via isolated vertex deletion update operation.

Assume now that some fake terminal
\tilde{t}
is deleted from some cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, and assume that
\tilde{t}
is the copy of some terminal
t\in T
. Notice that edge
e=(t,u(R))
currently lies in graph
\hat{H}
. If
n_{e}>1
, then another copy of
t
still lies in cluster
R
. In this case, no further updates to graph
\hat{H}
are needed, but we decrease the counter
n_{e}
by
1
, and we delete terminal
t
from list
L(e)
. Otherwise, we delete the edge
(t,u(R))
from graph
\hat{H}
. If vertex
u(R)
becomes isolated, then we delete this vertex from
\hat{H}
as well, via isolated vertex deletion update.

Lastly, assume that a new cluster
R^{\prime}
was inserted into
{\mathcal{R}}
. From the definition of the set
{\mathcal{R}}
of clusters and the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, this may only happen if, for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, distance scale
0\leq i\leq D^{*}
, a cluster-splitting update was applied to cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}^{C}_{i}
, so
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
must hold. If
R^{\prime}
contains no fake terminals, then no further changes to graph
\hat{H}
are required. Assume now that
R^{\prime}
contains at least one fake terminal, and let
F^{\prime}=F\cap V(R^{\prime})
. Then
F^{\prime}\subseteq R
must hold. Let
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
be the set of all terminals whose copies lie in
F^{\prime}
. Then for every terminal
t\in T^{\prime}
, edge
(t,u(R))
is currently present in graph
\hat{H}
. We let
E^{\prime}=\left\{(t,u(R))\mid t\in T^{\prime}\right\}
be a subset of edges incident to vertex
u(R)
. We then apply supernode-splitting update to supernode
u(R)
, with the set
E^{\prime}
of edges. As the result, we insert a new supernode
u(R^{\prime})
that corresponds to the new cluster
R^{\prime}
into
\hat{H}
, and we insert an edge connecting
u(R^{\prime})
to every terminal in
T^{\prime}
. We also initialize the lists
L(e)
and the counters
n_{e}
of all such newly inserted edges
e
.

It is easy to verify that the algorithm maintains the graph
\hat{H}
, the lists
L(e)
, and the counters
n_{e}
for edges
e\in E(\hat{H})
correctly. The running time of the algorithm is asymptotically bounded by the total update time of the data structure that maintains the collection
{\mathcal{R}}
of clusters.

We have now defined a valid input structure
\hat{\mathcal{J}}=(\hat{H},\left\{\ell(e)\right\})_{e\in E(\hat{H})},D^{*})
, that undergoes a sequence
\hat{\Sigma}
of valid update operations. We now bound the dynamic degree bound of
(\hat{\mathcal{J}},\hat{\Sigma})
. Recall that for every terminal
t\in T
, there are at most
\mu
fake terminals that are copies of
t
in
\tilde{H}
. As we have shown already, for every regular vertex
v\in V(\tilde{H})
, the number of clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}
to which
v
ever belongs during time horizon
{\mathcal{T}}
is bounded by
2W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log D^{*}
. Therefore, for every terminal
t\in T
, the total number of clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}
to which copies of
t
ever belong during the time horizon
{\mathcal{T}}
is bounded by
2W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\cdot\mu\cdot\log D^{*}
. If we denote by
\hat{\mu}
the dynamic degree bound of
(\hat{\mathcal{J}},\hat{\Sigma})
, then
\hat{\mu}\leq 2W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\cdot\mu\cdot\log D^{*}
. Since the number of regular vertices in
\hat{H}
is bounded by
W
, we can again apply the algorithm from
5.1
to instance
(\hat{\mathcal{J}},\hat{\Sigma})
of the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem. But before we do so, we discuss some useful properties of graph
\hat{H}
, namely that it approximately preserves distances between terminals.

Distance Preservation.

We start by showing that for every pair
t,t^{\prime}\in T
of terminals, if
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})\leq D^{*}
, then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(t,t^{\prime})\leq 4D^{*}
.

Claim 7.11
.

Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
be any time during the time horizon, and let
t,t^{\prime}\in T
be a pair of terminals with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{(\tau)}}(t,t^{\prime})=D^{\prime}
, where
D^{\prime}\leq D^{*}
. Then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}^{(\tau)}}(t,t^{\prime})\leq 4D^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We fix some time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Whenever we refer to dynamic graphs or data structures in this proof, we refer to the at time
\tau
, unless stated otherwise. Let
t,t^{\prime}\in T
be a pair of terminals with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{(\tau)}}(t,t^{\prime})=D^{\prime}
, for some
D^{\prime}\leq D^{*}
, and let
P
be a path of length
D^{\prime}
connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
in
H
. We can assume w.l.o.g. that path
P
contains no terminals as inner vertices, since otherwise we can partition path
P
into subpaths, such that the endpoints of each subpath are terminals, and no inner vertices of the subpath are terminals. By applying the claim to each subpath separately and concatenating the resulting paths, we obtain the desired path connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
in
\hat{H}
, of length at most
4D^{\prime}
. Therefore, we assume from now on that
P
contains no terminals as inner vertices.

Let
e=(t,u)
and
e^{\prime}=(u^{\prime},t^{\prime})
be the first and the last edges of
P
, respectively. From the construction of graph
\tilde{H}
, there is a copy
\tilde{t}
of
t
, and an edge
\tilde{e}=(\tilde{t},u)
in graph
\tilde{H}
, whose length is
\ell_{H}(e)
. Similarly, there is a copy
\tilde{t}^{\prime}
of
t^{\prime}
, and an edge
\tilde{e}^{\prime}=(\tilde{t}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
in
\tilde{H}
, whose length is
\ell_{H}(e^{\prime})
. All other edges of
P
are present in graph
\tilde{H}
. Therefore, there is a path
\tilde{P}
in graph
\tilde{H}
, whose length is
D^{\prime}
, that connects
\tilde{t}
to
\tilde{t}^{\prime}
. Since, from
3.3
, valid update operations cannot decrease distances between regular vertices,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(t,t^{\prime})\leq D^{\prime}\leq D^{*}
held.
From
7.10
, there is a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
with
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(\tilde{t},D^{*})\subseteq C
. From
3.7
, at time
\tau
,
B_{\tilde{H}^{(\tau)}}(\tilde{t},D^{*})\subseteq C^{(\tau)}
continues to hold. Therefore,
P^{\prime}\subseteq C
holds at time
\tau
, and
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C^{(\tau)}}(\tilde{t},\tilde{t}^{\prime})\leq D^{\prime}
.

Let
0\leq i\leq\log D^{*}
be the smallest integer for which
D_{i}\geq D^{\prime}
holds, so
D^{\prime}\leq D_{i}\leq 2D^{\prime}
. Since
D^{*}
is an integral power of
2
,
D_{i}\leq D^{*}
holds. Consider the cluster
R=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{C}_{i}(\tilde{t})
. This cluster must contain
B_{\tilde{H}}(\tilde{t},D_{i})
, and so it must contain both
\tilde{t}
and
\tilde{t}^{\prime}
. Therefore, graph
\hat{H}
contains edges
(t,u(R))
and
(u(R),t^{\prime})
of length
D_{i}
each. By concatenating the two edges, we obtain a path in graph
\hat{H}
, connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
, whose length is
2D_{i}\leq 4D^{\prime}
.

Notice that, from the above claim, whenever we identify a pair
t,t^{\prime}
of terminals with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(t,t^{\prime})>4D^{*}
, we can raise flag
F_{H}
with the pair
t,t^{\prime}
of witness vertices, since we are then guaranteed that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})>D^{*}
holds.

Next, we provide an algorithm that, given a path connecting a pair
t,t^{\prime}
of terminals in graph
\hat{H}
, returns a path of comparable length connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
in graph
H
. This algorithm will allow us to support
short-path-query
queries between pairs of vertices in
H
.
Recall that we denoted by
\alpha=\alpha(W)
the approximation factor achieved by the algorithm from
5.1
.

Claim 7.12
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given a pair
t,t^{\prime}\in T
of terminals, and a path
\hat{P}
connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
in the current graph
\hat{H}
of length
D^{\prime}
, returns a path
P
of length at most
\alpha\cdot D^{\prime}
, connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
in
H
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Proof.

We assume that we are given a pair
t,t^{\prime}\in T
of terminals, and a path
\hat{P}
connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
in the current graph
\hat{H}
of length
D^{\prime}
. We denote the sequence of vertices on path
\hat{P}
by
(t=t_{1},u(R_{1}),t_{2},u(R_{2}),\ldots,t_{q},u(R_{q}),t_{q+1}=t^{\prime})
.
For all
1\leq j\leq q
, we denote
i_{j}=\operatorname{scale}(R_{j})
. Then
D^{\prime}=2\sum_{j=1}^{q}2^{i_{j}}
. We now provide an algorithm that, for all
1\leq j\leq q
, computes a path
Q_{j}
in graph
H
, connecting
t_{j}
to
t_{j+1}
, such that the length of
Q_{j}
is at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i_{j}}
.

Consider an integer
1\leq j\leq q
. Since edges
e=(t_{j},u(R_{j})),e^{\prime}=(t_{j+1},u(R_{j}))
are present in graph
\hat{H}
, there must be a copy
\tilde{t}_{j}
of terminal
t_{j}
, and a copy
\tilde{t}_{j+1}
of terminal
t_{j+1}
, that lie in cluster
R_{j}
. We can find vertices
\tilde{t}_{j},\tilde{t}_{j+1}
in time
O(1)
using lists
L(e)
and
L(e^{\prime})
. We let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
be the cluster for which
R_{j}\in{\mathcal{R}}^{C}_{i_{j}}
. We execute query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}^{C}_{i_{j}}
in data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{C}_{i_{j}}
, to obtain a path
Q^{\prime}_{j}
in graph
C
, that connects
\tilde{t}_{j}
to
\tilde{t}_{j+1}
, and has length at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i_{j}}
. The time required for processing the query is
O(|E(Q^{\prime}_{j})|)
. Since
C\subseteq\tilde{H}
, path
Q^{\prime}_{j}
also lies in graph
\tilde{H}
. We let
\tilde{S}
be the sequence of vertices on path
Q^{\prime}_{j}
, and we let
S
be the sequence of vertices obtained from
\tilde{S}
by replacing every fake terminal with the corresponding original terminal from
T
. From
7.8
, sequence
S
of vertices defines a path in graph
H
that connects
t_{j}
to
t_{j+1}
, and has length at most
\alpha\cdot D_{i_{j}}
. We denote the resulting path by
Q_{j}
. Clearly, the time required to compute the path
Q_{j}
is bounded by
O(|E(Q_{j})|)
.

By concatenating the paths
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{r}
, we obtain a path
P
in graph
H
that connects
t
to
t^{\prime}
. The length of the path is at most
\sum_{j=1}^{q}\alpha\cdot D_{i_{j}}\leq\alpha\cdot D^{\prime}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(P)|)
.

7.4.4
Additional Data Structures

We now describe the data structures that the algorithm for Stage 3 maintains, in addition to the modified graph
\tilde{H}
, the collections
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}
of clusters, and the contracted graph
\hat{H}
.

Maintaining short distances between the terminals.

Recall that we have defined a valid input structure

\hat{\mathcal{J}}=(\hat{H},\left\{\ell(e)\right\})_{e\in E(\hat{H})},D^{*})
, that undergoes a sequence
\hat{\Sigma}
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\hat{\mu}\leq 2W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\cdot\mu\cdot\log D^{*}
, and that the number of regular vertices in the initial graph
\hat{H}
is at most
W/(4\mu^{2})
.
We view
(\hat{\mathcal{J}},\hat{\Sigma})
as an instance of the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, but we replace the distance parameter
D^{*}
with
4D^{*}
.

We apply the algorithm from
5.1
to this instance of
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
, and we denote by
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
the collection of clusters of graph
\hat{H}
that the algorithm maintain.
For every cluster
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, we also maintain a list
\lambda_{\hat{R}}
of all terminals
t\in T
, for which
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t)=\hat{R}
. We also maintain two counters: a counter
\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}=|\lambda_{\hat{R}}|
, and a counter
\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}=|T\cap V(\hat{R})|
. We initialize the lists and the counters at the beginning of the algorithm, when the initial collection
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
of clusters is computed. Whenever, for some terminal
t\in T
, the algorithm from
5.1
changes
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t)
from cluster
\hat{R}
to cluster
\hat{R}^{\prime}
, we delete
t
from
\lambda_{\hat{R}}
, and we insert it into
\lambda_{\hat{R}^{\prime}}
, and update the counters
\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}
and
\hat{n}_{\hat{R}^{\prime}}
accordingly. Whenever a terminal
t
is deleted from some cluster
\hat{R}
, we decrease the counter
\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}
. Whenever a new cluster
\hat{R}^{\prime\prime}
is inserted into
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, we set
\lambda_{\hat{R}^{\prime\prime}}=\emptyset
,
\hat{n}_{\hat{R}^{\prime\prime}}=0
, and we initialize the counter
\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}^{\prime\prime}}
to
|T\cap V(\hat{R}^{\prime\prime})|
. We denote by
\mathsf{DS}^{\prime}
the data structure that the algorithm from
5.1
maintains, augmented with the algorithm for maintaining the lists
\lambda_{\hat{R}}
and counters
\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}
,
\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}
for clusters
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}
. It is easy to see that the lists and the counters can be maintained without increasing the asymptotic total update time of the algorithm. Therefore, the total update time needed to maintain data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{\prime}
is bounded by:

O\left(W^{1+\delta}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\hat{\mu}^{c}\right)\leq O\left(W^{1+\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right),

since
\hat{\mu}=2W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\cdot\mu\cdot\log D^{*}
.

Recall that the algorithm from
5.1

ensures that every terminal
t\in T
may lie in at most
\Delta=\Delta(W)
clusters of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
over the course of Stage 3 of the algorithm. Our goal is to ensure that, as long as
T\cap V(H)\neq\emptyset
, there is some cluster
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, that contains all terminals of
T\cap V(H)
, and that such a cluster can be computed in time
O(1)
. We denote by
T^{\prime}=T\cap V(H)
the set of terminals that currently lie in graph
H
.
Consider a cluster
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}
. We say that cluster
\hat{R}
is
special
if
\frac{\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}}{\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}}\leq 2\Delta
. Note that, if
\hat{R}
is a special cluster, then
\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}\neq 0
and
\lambda_{\hat{R}}\neq\emptyset
must hold. We use the following observation.

Observation 7.13
.

For all
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, if
T^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
holds at time
\tau
, then at least one cluster
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}
is special.

Proof.

Consider any time
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
, such that
T^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
holds at time
\tau
, and assume for contradiction that no cluster of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
is special. Observe that
\sum_{\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}}\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}=|T^{\prime}|
, since for every terminal
t\in T^{\prime}
, there is a single cluster
\hat{R}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t)
. Since no cluster is special, we get that, for every cluster
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}
,
\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}>2\Delta\cdot\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}
, and so
\sum_{\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}}\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}>2\Delta\cdot\sum_{\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}}\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}\geq 2\Delta\cdot|T^{\prime}|
. However, every terminal of
T^{\prime}
may lie in at most
\Delta
sets of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, so
\sum_{\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}}\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}\leq\Delta\cdot|T^{\prime}|
must hold, a contradiction.

Notice that we can maintain, for every cluster
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, a bit
b_{\hat{R}}
that indicates whether cluster
\hat{R}
is currently special, and we can also maintain a list
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}\subseteq\hat{\mathcal{R}}
of special clusters, without increasing the asymptotic running time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{\prime}
. We will also maintain the cardinality of the set
T^{\prime}=T\cap V(H)
of terminals, that we denote by
\hat{N}
. From
7.13
, the list
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
of special clusters is always non-empty. Consider now a special cluster
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
. We say that cluster
\hat{R}
is
heavy
if
\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}}\geq\frac{|T^{\prime}|}{2}
, and we say that it is
light
otherwise. We need the following observation.

Observation 7.14
.

The total number of clusters
\hat{R}
that ever belonged to
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, such that
\hat{R}
was ever a heavy special cluster, is bounded by
4\Delta\log W
.

Proof.

We partition the second stage of the algorithm into phases. Each phase lasts until the number of terminals in
T^{\prime}
decreases by at least factor
2
since the beginning of the phase. Therefore, the number of phases is bounded by
\log|T|\leq\log W
.

Consider now some phase, and denote the number of terminals in
T^{\prime}
at the beginning of the phase by
N^{\prime}
.
Let
\Pi
be the collection of pairs
(\hat{R},t)
, where
t\in T
, and
\hat{R}
is a cluster that belonged to
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
at any time during the current phase, such that
t\in\hat{R}
held at any time during the current phase.
Since every terminal may belong to at most
\Delta
clusters of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
over the course of Stage 3, we get that
|\Pi|\leq\Delta\cdot N^{\prime}
. On the other hand, if any cluster
\hat{R}
was heavy at any time during the current stage, then it must have contained at least
N^{\prime}/4
terminals at that time. Therefore, if
z
is the number of clusters of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
that were ever heavy during the current phase, then
|\Pi|\geq z\cdot N^{\prime}/4
must hold. We conclude that
z\leq 4\Delta
, and so the total number of clusters

\hat{R}
that ever belonged to
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, such that
\hat{R}
was ever a heavy special cluster, is bounded by
4\Delta\log W
.

If, at any time
\tau
during Stage 3, a cluster
\hat{R}
becomes a heavy special cluster, then, from time
\tau
, we maintain a set
S(\hat{R})
that contains all terminals that do not lie in cluster
\hat{R}
. Maintaining set
S(\hat{R})
over the course of the remainder of Stage 3 takes time at most
O(|T|)
: after initializing set
S(\hat{R})
, we simply need to add to this set any terminal that is deleted from cluster
\hat{R}
, and delete from it any terminal that was deleted from graph
\hat{H}
. Since the total number clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}
that are heavy and special at any time during Stage 3 is bounded by
O(\Delta\log W)
, maintaining all such lists
S(\hat{R})
for all such clusters
\hat{R}
takes at most
O(W\Delta\log W)
time. For every heavy and special cluster
\hat{R}
, whenever the list
S(\hat{R})
is non-empty, we raise the flag
F_{H}
. As a witness pair, we supply a pair of terminals
(t,t^{\prime})
, where
t\in\lambda_{\hat{R}}
and
t^{\prime}\in S(\hat{R})
. Since
t\in\lambda_{\hat{R}}
,
\hat{R}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(t)
holds, and so cluster
\hat{R}
must contain
B_{\hat{H}}(t,4D^{*})
. Since
t^{\prime}\in S(\hat{R})
, it must be the case that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(t,t^{\prime})>4D^{*}
, and so from
7.11
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})>D^{*}
.

Whenever a new cluster
\hat{R}
is added to the list
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
of special clusters, and cluster
\hat{R}
is light, we construct two sets of terminals: set
T_{1}
containing all terminals in list
\lambda_{\hat{R}}
; and set
T_{2}
, containing
|T_{1}|
terminals that do not lie in
\hat{R}
. Both lists can be constructed in time
O(\hat{n}^{\prime}_{\hat{R}})\leq O(\Delta\cdot\hat{n}_{\hat{R}})
, by going over all terminals that lie in
\hat{R}
. Observe that, since cluster
\hat{R}
is not heavy, the number of terminals of
T^{\prime}
that do not lie in
\hat{R}
is at least
|T_{1}|
.
Moreover, since, for every terminal
t\in T_{1}
,
B_{\hat{H}}(t,4D^{*})\subseteq V(\hat{R})
, for every pair
t\in T_{1}
,
t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(t,t^{\prime})>4D^{*}
holds, and so
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(t,t^{\prime})>D^{*}
. As long as
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are non-empty, we raise the flag
F_{H}
, and provide a pair
(t,t^{\prime})
of terminals with
t\in T_{1}
and
t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
as a witness pair. Once either of the sets
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
become empty, we re-evaluate whether cluster
\hat{R}
remains special and light, and if so, we repeat the algorithm. Notice that we have spent time
O(\Delta\cdot\hat{n}_{\hat{R}})
in order to compute the sets
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
of the terminals, but then at least
\hat{n}_{\hat{R}}
terminals were subsequently deleted from graph
H
. Therefore, the total time that is required in order to process light special clusters is bounded by
O(\Delta\cdot|T|)\leq O(W\cdot\Delta)
. Overall, the time that we spent on processing special clusters is asymptotically bounded by the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{\prime}
. Our algorithm now guarantees that, at all times, at least one cluster lies in set
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
, and, if flag
F_{H}
is down, then every cluster in
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
contains all terminals that currently lie in graph
H
.

Maintaining short distances from regular vertices to the terminals.

As long as
T\cap V(H)\neq\emptyset
, we will maintain a modified
ES-Tree
in graph
H
, rooted at the vertices of
T
, with depth
D^{*}
, in order to ensure that the distances between regular vertices and terminals are small. Specifically, let
H^{\prime}
be the graph that is obtained from
H
by adding a source vertex
s
, and connecting it to every vertex in
T^{\prime}
with an edge of length
1
. Whenever graph
H
undergoes updates, we also update graph
H^{\prime}
accordingly. Whenever a terminal
t\in T
is deleted from graph
H
via an isolated vertex deletion operation, we delete the edge
(s,t)
and we delete termian
t
from
H^{\prime}
. It is easy to verify that graph
H^{\prime}
only undergoes edge-deletion, isolated vertex-deletion and supernode-splitting (which is a special case of vertex-splitting) updates. We use the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
to maintain a modified
ES-Tree
in graph
H^{\prime}
, with distance bound
D^{*}+1
. Recall that the total update time of the algorithm is
O(m^{*}\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log m^{*})
, where
m^{*}
is the total number of edges that ever belonged to graph
H^{\prime}
. Since
m^{*}\leq N^{0}(H)\cdot\mu+|T|\leq 2\hat{W}
, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log(\hat{W}\mu))\leq O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log W)
. Recall that the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
maintains a collection
S
of vertices of
H^{\prime}
, such that, for every vertex
v\in S
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}}(v,s)>D^{*}+1
holds. Whenever a regular vertex
v
of
H
joins the set
S
, we raise a flag
F_{H}
, and provide a witness pair
(v,t)
, where
t
is any terminal vertex. We are then guaranteed that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,t)>D^{*}
holds.

The
ES-Tree
data structure is maintained as long as not all vertices of
T
are deleted from
H
. Let
\tau^{*}
be the first time when all vertices of
T
are deleted from
H
. From time
\tau^{*}
onwards, we will maintain another data structure, that will allow us to support
short-path-query
queries, which we describe next.

Maintaining short distances between regular vertices.

Once all terminals are deleted from
H
, we will employ additional data structures, in order to ensure that, at all times
\tau\geq\tau^{*}
, there is a cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, that contains all regular vertices. Starting from time
\tau^{*}
onwards, we discard all clusters from
{\mathcal{R}}
, except for those whose scale is
\log D^{*}
. In other words, we now set
{\mathcal{R}}=\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{R}}^{C}_{\log D^{*}}
. Since the algorithm from
7.10
guarantees that every regular vertex
v\in V(\tilde{H})
belongs to at most
W^{\epsilon/8}
clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}
, in the remainder of the algorithm, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the total number of clusters in
{\mathcal{R}}
that
v
can ever belong to is bounded by
\Delta^{\prime}=W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta
.

We initialize a counter
N
, that counts the number of regular vertices currently in graph
H
.
For every regular vertex
v\in V(H)
, we maintain a cluster
R^{*}(v)\in{\mathcal{R}}
, that is defined as follows. Recall that the algorithm from
7.10
computes a cluster
C(v)\in{\mathcal{C}}
with
B_{\tilde{H}^{(0)}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(C(v))
, and from
3.7
,
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(C(v))
continues to hold throughout Stage 3. We let
R^{*}(v)=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{C(v)}_{\log D^{*}}(v)
. Note that
B_{\tilde{H}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(R^{*}(v))
must hold at all times, though cluster
R^{*}(v)
may change over the course of the algorithm.
Moreover, once all terminals are deleted from
H
, starting from time
\tau^{*}
onwards,
\tilde{H}=H
holds, and so
B_{H}(v,D^{*})=B_{\tilde{H}}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(R^{*}(v))
must hold. Therefore, for all times
\tau\geq\tau^{*}
, if
R=R^{*}(v)
holds for a regular vertex
v
, then
B_{H}(v,D^{*})\subseteq R
holds.

For every cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, we will maintain a list
\lambda(R)
of all regular vertices
v\in V(H)
with
R^{*}(v)=R
, together with two counters: counter
n_{R}=|\lambda_{R}|
, and a counter
n^{\prime}_{R}
, that is equal to the number of regular vertices in cluster
R
. We initialize all the lists and the counters at time
\tau^{*}
. Notice that we can keep track of the clusters
R^{*}(v)
for regular vertices
v\in V(H)
using the data structure
\mathsf{DS}
, without increasing its asymptotic running time. Whenever, for any regular vertex
v\in V(H)
, cluster
R^{*}(v)
changes from
R
to
R^{\prime}
, we delete
t
from
\lambda_{R}
, insert it into
\lambda_{R^{\prime}}
, and update the counters
n_{R}
and
n_{R^{\prime}}
accordingly. Whenever a regular vertex
v
is deleted from some cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, we decrease the counter
n^{\prime}_{R}
. Whenever a new cluster
R^{\prime\prime}
is inserted into
{\mathcal{R}}
, we set
\lambda_{R^{\prime\prime}}=\emptyset
,
n_{R}=0
, and we initialize the counter
n^{\prime}_{R}
to the number of regular vertices that lie in
R^{\prime\prime}
. It is easy to verify that these additional modifications of data structure
\mathsf{DS}
do not increase its asymptotic total update time, and that the time required to initialize the lists
\lambda_{R}
and conters
n_{R},n^{\prime}_{R}
for all clusters
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is also asymptitcally bounded by the total update time of data structrure
\mathsf{DS}
.

Consider a cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
. We say that cluster
R
is special if
\frac{n^{\prime}_{R}}{n_{R}}\leq 2\Delta^{\prime}
. Since, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the total number of clusters in
{\mathcal{R}}
that
v
can ever belong to is bounded by
\Delta^{\prime}
, we obtain the following observation, whose proof is essentially identical to the proof of
7.13
and is omitted here.

Observation 7.15
.

For all
\tau\geq\tau^{*}
, at time
\tau
, at least one cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
is special.

As before, we can maintain, for every cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}
, a bit
b_{R}
that indicates whether cluster
R
is currently special, and we can also maintain a list
{\mathcal{R}}^{S}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}
of special clusters, without increasing the asymptotic running time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}
. Recall that we denoted by
N
the total number of regular vertices currently in
H
. From
7.15
, the list
{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
of special clusters is always non-empty. Consider now a special cluster
R\in{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
. We say that cluster
R
is heavy if
n^{\prime}_{R}\geq\frac{N}{2}
, and we say that it is light otherwise.
Using the same arguments as in the proof of
7.14
, we conclude that, the total number of clusters
R
that ever belonged in
{\mathcal{R}}
, such that
R
was ever a heavy special cluster is bounded by
4\Delta^{\prime}\log W
.

If, at any time
\tau>\tau^{*}
, a cluster
R
becomes a heavy special cluster, then, from time
\tau
, we maintain a set
S(R)
that contains all regular vertices that do not lie in cluster
R
. Maintaining set
S(R)
over the course of the remainder of Stage 3 takes time at most
O(\hat{W})
: after initializing set
S(R)
, we simply need to add to this set any regular vertex that is deleted from cluster
R
, and delete from it any regular vertex that was deleted from graph
H
. Since the total number clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}
that are heavy and special at any time during Stage 3 is bounded by
O(\Delta^{\prime}\log W)\leq O\left(W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\right)
, maintaining all such lists
S(R)
for all such clusters
R
takes at most
O(W^{1+O(\epsilon)}\Delta)
time. For every heavy and special cluster
R
, whenever the list
S(R)
is non-empty, we raise the flag
F_{H}
. As a witness pair, we supply a pair of regular vertices
(v,v^{\prime})
, where
v\in\lambda_{R}
and
v^{\prime}\in S(R)
. Recall that, if
v\in\lambda_{R}
, then
B_{H}(v,D^{*})\subseteq R
must hold. Since
v^{\prime}\not\in R
, we conclude that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})>D^{*}
currently holds, so
(v,v^{\prime})
is a valid witness pair.

Whenever a new cluster
R
is added to the list
{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
of special clusters, and cluster
R
is light, we construct two sets of regular vertices: set
V_{1}
containing all regular vertices in list
\lambda_{R}
; and set
V_{2}
, containing
|V_{1}|
regular vertices that do not lie in
R
. Both lists can be constructed in time
O(n^{\prime}_{R})\leq O(\Delta^{\prime}\cdot n_{R})\leq O(W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\cdot n_{R})
, by going over all regular vertices that lie in
R
. Observe that, since cluster
R
is not heavy, the number of regular vertices of
H
that do not lie in
R
is at least
|V_{1}|
.
Moreover, since, for every vertex
v\in V_{1}
,
B_{H}(v,D^{*})\subseteq V(R)
, for every pair
v\in V_{1}
,
v^{\prime}\in V_{2}
of regular vertices,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})>D^{*}
holds. As long as
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are non-empty, we raise the flag
F_{H}
, and provide a pair
(v,v^{\prime})
of regular vertices with
v\in V_{1}
and
v^{\prime}\in V_{2}
as a witness pair. Once either of
V_{1}
or
V_{2}
become empty, we re-evaluate whether cluster
R
remains special and light, and if so, we repeat the algorithm. Notice that we have spent time
O(W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta\cdot n_{R})
in order to construct the sets
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
of the regular vertices, but then at least
n_{R}
regular vertices were subsequently deleted from graph
H
via flag-lowering sequences. Therefore, the total time that is required in order to process light special clusters is bounded by
O(N\cdot W^{\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta)\leq O(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta)
. Overall, the time that we spent on processing special clusters is asymptotically bounded by the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}
plus
O(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta)
. Our algorithm now guarantees that, at all times
\tau\geq\tau^{*}
, at least one cluster lies in set
{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
, and, if flag
F_{H}
is down, then every cluster in
{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
contains all regular vertices that currently lie in graph
H
.

This completes the description of the data structures that the algorithm for Stage 3 maintains. We now bound the total update time of the algorithm.

Recall that the running time of the algorithm from
7.10
is bounded by:

O\left((\hat{W}\cdot\mu)^{1+\epsilon}\right);

total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}
is bounded by:

O\left(\hat{W}\cdot W^{\delta+\epsilon/8}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{c}\right).

The total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{\prime}
is bounded by:

O\left(W^{1+\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right),

and the total update time of the data structure from
Theorem

2.6
that maintains the modified
ES-Tree
is bounded by:

O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log(\hat{W}\mu))\leq O(\hat{W}\cdot\mu\cdot D^{*}\cdot\log W).

Additionally, maintaining and processing the special clusters of
{\mathcal{R}}
takes
O(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta)
time, in addition to the total update time of data structure
\mathsf{DS}
.

The remaining time, that is needed in order to maintain the modified graph
\tilde{H}
, the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
, and the contracted graph
\hat{H}
are asymptotically bounded by the above.

Overall, the total update time of the algorithm from Stage 3 is bounded by:

O\left(\hat{W}\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}+(\hat{W}\mu)^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta\right),

Since the total update time of the algorithm from Stage 1 is bounded by:

O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{3}\cdot\rho^{8}\mu^{4}\right),

the total update time of the algorithm from Stage 2 is bounded by:

O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot D^{*}\cdot\rho\cdot\mu^{2}\right),

and since
\hat{D}=O(D^{*}/\epsilon^{2})
and
\rho=\frac{4\hat{W}}{W}
, we get that the total running time of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is bounded by:

O\left(\hat{W}\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon/8}\cdot\Delta^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot(\log D^{*})^{c}\right)+O\left(\hat{W}^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot(D^{*})^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\Delta\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\right).

7.4.5
Responding to Queries

We now provide an algorithm for supporting queries
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(H,v,v^{\prime})
over the course of Stage 3. Suppose we are given a query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(H,v,v^{\prime})
, where
v,v^{\prime}\in V(H)
are regular vertices of
H
. Recall that our goal is to return a path
P
of length at most
\alpha^{\prime}\cdot D^{*}
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
H
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

We employ different algorithms, depending whether
T\cap V(H)=\emptyset
holds. Assume first that at least one terminal
t\in T
lies in graph
H
. In this case, we query the
ES-Tree
data structure to obtain a path
P_{1}
connecting
v
to
s
in graph
H^{\prime}
, whose lenghth is at most
D^{*}+1
, in time
O(|E(P_{1})|)
. By deleting the last vertex from path
P_{1}
, we obtain a path in graph
H
, connecting
v
to some terminal
t\in T
, such that the length of the path is at most
D^{*}
. Similarly, we compute a path
P_{2}
, connecting
v
to some terminal
t^{\prime}\in T
, of length at most
D^{*}
, in time
O(|E(P_{2})|)
. If
t=t^{\prime}
, then we obtain a path
P
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
H
, by concatenating
P_{1}
to
P_{2}
. The length of the path is at most
2D^{*}\leq\alpha^{\prime}\cdot D^{*}
, and the running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(P)|)
.

Assume now that
t\neq t^{\prime}
. Recall that we maintain a list
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
of special clusters of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
, that is always non-empty. Let
\hat{R}\in\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
be any such cluster. Since the queries cannot be asked when flag
F_{H}
is up,
T^{\prime}\subseteq V(\hat{R})
must hold. We run query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(\hat{R},t,t^{\prime})
in data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{\prime}
, to obtain a path
Q
in graph
\hat{H}
that connects
t
to
t^{\prime}
, and has length at most
4\alpha\cdot D^{*}
, in time
O(|E(Q)|)
. We then apply the algorithm from
7.12
to path
Q
, in order to obtain a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H
, connecting
t
to
t^{\prime}
, whose length is at most
4\alpha^{2}\cdot D^{*}
, in time
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)
. By concatenating paths
P_{1},P^{\prime}
and
P_{2}
, we obtain a path
P
in graph
H
, connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
. The length of the path is bounded by
2D^{*}+4\alpha^{2}\cdot D^{*}\leq 8\alpha^{2}D^{*}\leq\alpha^{\prime}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(P)|)
.

Assume now that all terminals of
T
have already been deleted from
H
. Recall that we maintain a set
{\mathcal{R}}^{S}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}
of special clusters, which is non-empty. We let
R\in{\mathcal{R}}^{S}
be any such cluster. Since flag
F_{H}
is currently down, every regular vertex of
H
lies in cluster
R
. Recall also that
\operatorname{scale}(R)=\log D^{*}
. Assume that
R\in{\mathcal{R}}^{C}_{\log D^{*}}
for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. We apply query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(R,v,v^{\prime})
to data structure
\mathsf{DS}^{C}_{\log D^{*}}
, to obtain a path
P
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
in graph
H
, of length at most
\alpha\cdot D^{*}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. We return this path as the outcome of the query.

Appendix A
Proof of
Corollary

1.5

We assume that we are given an instance
G
of fully dynamic
APSP
problem, with lengths
\ell(e)>0
on edges
e\in E(G)
and
|V(G)|=n
. We assume without loss of generality that the shortest edge length is
1
and longest edge length is
\Lambda
. At the cost of losing factor
2
in the approximation ratio, we can assume that all edge lengths are integral. For all
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil
, we denote
D_{i}=2^{i}
.

Notice that
Theorem

1.4
implies that
1.1
holds, for
\alpha(\hat{W})=(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}
.

For all
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil
, we use
Theorem

1.3
to obtain Algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{i}
for the
D_{i}
-restricted
APSP
problem on graph
G
, with parameters
m
and
\epsilon
remaining unchanged. We denote by
\mathsf{DS}_{i}
the data structure that algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{i}
maintains. Recall that the amortized update time of Algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{i}
is bounded by
n^{O(\epsilon)}
per operation. Therefore, the amortized update time of all algorithms
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil
is at most

O\left(n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot\log\Lambda\right)
per operation.

Recall that each such algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{i}
achieves approximation factor
(\alpha(n^{3}))^{O(1/\epsilon)}\leq(\log\log n)^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}=\alpha^{\prime}
.

Next, we provide an algorithm for responding to query
dist-query
between a pair
x,y
of vertices of
G
. We perform a binary search on integers
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil
, to find an integer
i^{\prime}
, such that the response of data structure
\mathsf{DS}_{i^{\prime}}
to query
\mbox{\sf{short-path}}(x,y)
is “YES”, and the response of data structure
\mathsf{DS}_{i^{\prime}-1}
to the same query is “NO”. Since query time for a single such query is
O\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\log n\right)
, the time required to compute the index
i^{\prime}
is bounded by
O\left(2^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\log n\log\log\Lambda\right)
. We then return
D_{i^{\prime}}=2^{i^{\prime}}
as the estimate on
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(x,y)
. Since the response of data structure
\mathsf{DS}_{i^{\prime}-1}
to the query is “NO”, we are guaranteed that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(x,y)\geq D_{i^{\prime}}/2
. On the other hand, since data structure
\mathsf{DS}_{i^{\prime}}
responded “YES”, we are guaranteed that there exists a path in graph
G
, connecting
x
to
y
, of length at most
D_{i^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}
.

Finally, we provide an algorithm for responding to query
shortest-path-query
between a pair
x,y
of vertices of
G
. We start by executing the algorithm for responding to
dist-query
between
x
and
y
, and obtain an integer
i^{\prime}
as described above, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(x,y)\geq D_{i^{\prime}}/2
, and algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{i^{\prime}}
, in response to query
\mbox{\sf{short-path}}(x,y)
responds “YES”. We then ask the same algorithm to compute a path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in the current graph
G
, of length at most
D_{i^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
, and return the resulting path
P
. Clearly, the running time of the algorithm is
O\left(|E(P)|+2^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\log n\cdot\log\log\Lambda\right)
.

Appendix B
Proofs Omitted from Section
2

B.1
Proof of
Lemma

2.1

For all
i\geq 1
, we denote by
L_{i}
the set of all vertices of
G
that lie at distance
2(i-1)D+1
to
2iD
from
v
in
G
. In other words:

L_{i}=B_{G}(v,2iD)\setminus B_{G}(v,2(i-1)D).

We refer to the vertices of
L_{i}
as
layer
i
of the BFS
. We denote by
m_{i}
the total number of edges
e\in E(G)
, such that both endpoints of
e
lie in
L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{i}
. For all
1\leq j\leq k
, we also denote by
N^{j}_{i}
the total number of vertices of
T_{j}
that lie in
L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{i}
. The following definition is central to our algorithm.

Definition B.1
(Eligible Layer).

Let
i>1
be an integer. We say that layer
L_{i}
of the BFS is
eligible
, if the following conditions hold:

C1.

m_{i}\leq m_{i-1}\cdot|E(G)|^{\epsilon}
;

C2.

for all
1\leq j\leq k
,
N^{j}_{i}\leq N^{j}_{i-1}\cdot|T_{j}|^{\epsilon}
.

We use the following simple observation, that follows from standard arguments.

Observation B.2
.

There is an integer
1<i\leq\frac{2k+2}{\epsilon}
, such that layer
L_{i}
is eligible.

Proof.

Let
z=\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
. We start by showing that there are at most
z
layers
L_{i}
with
i>1
, for which condition
C1
is violated. Indeed, assume otherwise. Let
1<i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{z}
be indices of layers for which condition
C1
is violated. Since we have assumed that vertex
v
is not isolated in
G
,
m_{1}\geq 1
must hold. From Condition
C1
, for all
1<a\leq z
,
m_{i_{a}}>m_{i_{a}-1}\cdot|E(G)|^{\epsilon}\geq m_{i_{a-1}}\cdot|E(G)|^{\epsilon}
. Therefore,
m_{z}>m_{1}\cdot|E(G)|^{z\epsilon}\geq|E(G)|
, which is impossible

Next, we show that, for all
1\leq j\leq k
, the number of layers
L_{i}
with
i>1
, for which the condition
N^{j}_{i}\leq N^{j}_{i-1}\cdot|T_{j}|^{\epsilon}
is violated, is bounded by
z+1
. Indeed, assume otherwise, and let
1<i^{\prime}_{1}<i^{\prime}_{2}<\cdots<i^{\prime}_{z+1}
be indices of layers for which the condition is violated.
Then
N^{j}_{i^{\prime}_{1}}\geq 1
must hold, and, for all
1<a\leq z+1
,
N^{j}_{i_{a}}>N^{j}_{i_{a}-1}\cdot|T_{j}|^{\epsilon}\geq N^{j}_{i_{a-1}}\cdot|T_{j}|^{\epsilon}
. Therefore,
N_{z+1}>|T_{j}|^{z\epsilon}\geq|T_{j}|
, which is impossible.

We conclude that the number of layers
L_{i}
with
i>1
that are not eligible is bounded by
z+k\cdot(z+1)\leq\frac{2k+1}{\epsilon}
, since
z=\left\lceil\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rceil
, and
\epsilon<1/4
.

We run the weighted BFS algorithm from vertex
v
in graph
G
, until we reach the first layer
1<i\leq\frac{2k+2}{\epsilon}
that is an eligible layer. We then return integer
i
. if we denote by
S=B_{G}(v,2(i-1)D)
, and
S^{\prime}=B_{G}(v,2iD)
, then the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E_{G}(S^{\prime})|\cdot\log n)\leq O(|E_{G}(S)|\cdot|E(G)|^{\epsilon}\cdot\log n)
, as required. From the definition of an eligible layer, it is easy to verify that the output
i
has all required properties.

B.2
Modified Even-Shiloach Trees – Proof of
Theorem

2.6

The proof uses standard techniques, and is essentially identical to the proof of Theorem 3.2 in
[
Chu21
]
. The main difference is that we allow vertex-splitting operations to be applied to any vertex of
G
except for the source
s
, while in
[
Chu21
]
, the input graph
G
is bipartite, and vertex-splitting could only be applied to vertices on one side of the bipartition.

Data Structures.

We maintain the graph
G
as an adjacency list: for every vertex
v
, we maintain a linked list of its neighbors.
Throughout the algorithm, we will maintain the following data structures:

•

A shortest-path tree
T
rooted at vertex
s
, that contains all vertices
x\in V(G)
with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(s,x)\leq D
. For every such vertex
x
, its correct distance
\lambda(x)=\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(s,x)
from
s
is stored together with
x
.

•

A set
S^{*}
containing all vertices of
G
that do not lie in
T
.

•

For every vertex
x\in V(T)
, let
\mathsf{pred}(x)
be the set of all vertices
y\in V(T)
with
(x,y)\in E(G)
. We maintain a heap
\mathsf{Heap}(x)
in which all elements of
\mathsf{pred}(x)
are stored, where the key associated with each element
y\in\mathsf{pred}(x)
is
\lambda(y)+\ell(y,x)
.

•

For every edge
e=(y,x)
with
y,x\in V(T)
, we store, together with vertex
y
, a pointer to its corresponding element in the heap
\mathsf{Heap}(x)
and vice versa.

Throughout, we denote by
m^{*}
the total number of edges that are ever present in
G
.

Initialization.

We run Dijkstra’s algorithm on graph
G
time
O(|E(G)|\log m^{*})\leq O(m^{*}\cdot\log m^{*})
, up to distance threshold
D
, to construct the initial tree
T
. For every vertex
x\in V(T)
, we initialize
\lambda(x)=\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(s,x)
, and for all
x\not\in V(T)
, we set
\lambda(x)=\infty
. We then process all vertices of
V(T)
in the order of their distance from
s
, and insert each such vertex
x
into all heaps
\mathsf{Heap}(y)
of all vertices
y\in V(G)
that are neighbors of
x
in
G
. Lastly, we initialize the set
S^{*}
of vertices to contain every vertex
x\in V(G)\setminus V(T)
. Clearly, initialization takes time at most
O(m^{*}\cdot\log m^{*})
.

We now assume that we are given a valid data structure, and describe an algorithm for handling updates.

Edge Deletion.

The procedure for processing edge deletions is completely standard. The description provided here is due to Chechik
[
Che18
]
. It is somewhat different but equivalent to the standard description.
Suppose an edge
e=(x,y)
is deleted from the graph
G
. We start by updating the heaps
\mathsf{Heap}(x)
,
\mathsf{Heap}(y)
with the deletion of this edge. If
e\not\in E(T)
, then no further updates are necessary.

We assume from now on that
e=(x,y)
is an edge of the tree
T
, and we assume w.l.o.g. that
y
is the parent of
x
in
T
. Let
S
be the set of all vertices of
T
that lie in the subtree
T_{x}
of
T
that is rooted at
x
. We delete the edge
(x,y)
from
T
, thereby disconnecting all vertices of
S
from
T
. The remainder of the algorithm consists of two phases. In the first phase, we identify the set
R\subseteq S
of all vertices, whose distance from
s
has increased, and connect all remaining vertices of
S
to
T
. In the second phase, we attempt to reconnect vertices of
R
to
T
.

In order to implement the first phase, we maintain a heap
Q
of all vertices that need to be examined, where the key associated with each vertex
a
in
Q
is
\lambda(a)
.
We initialize
Q
to maintain a single vertex – the vertex
x
, and we also initialize
R=\emptyset
. Heap
Q
will have the property that, if some vertex
a
belongs to
Q
, and vertex
a^{\prime}
was the parent of
a
in
T
, then
a^{\prime}
was added to
R
(or, if
a=x
, then
a^{\prime}\in T
). Moreover, if
a
is a vertex of
Q
with smallest key
\lambda(a)
, and
a^{\prime}
is the element lying at the top of
\mathsf{Heap}(a)
, then either
\lambda(a^{\prime})\geq\lambda(a)
, or
a^{\prime}\in T
must hold. Both these invariants hold at the beginning.

The algorithm iterates, as long as
Q\neq\emptyset
. Let
a
be a vertex of
Q
with smallest key
\lambda(a)
.
Let
b
be the element lying at the top of
\mathsf{Heap}(a)
.
If
b\in V(T)
, then
we check whether connecting vertex
a
to the tree
T
via vertex
b
will allow us to keep
\lambda(a)
unchanged, or, equivalently, whether
\lambda(a)=\lambda(b)+\ell(a,b)
. If this is the case, then we connect
a
to the tree
T
via
b
, delete
a
from
Q
, and proceed to the next iteration. Otherwise, we are guaranteed that
\lambda(a)
must increase. We then add
a
to
R
, add all children of
a
in the original tree
T
to the heap
Q
, and delete
a
from
\mathsf{Heap}(b^{\prime})
for all neighbors
b^{\prime}
of
a
.
Alternatively, if
b\not\in V(T)
, then, from our invariant
\lambda(b)\geq\lambda(a)
currently holds, which again means that
\lambda(a)
must increase. In this case, we also add
a
to
R
, add all
children of
a
in the original tree
T
to the heap
Q
, and delete
a
from
\mathsf{Heap}(b^{\prime})
for all neighbors
b^{\prime}
of
a
.

Note that the algorithm examines vertices of
S
in the non-decreasing order of their label
\lambda(a)
(except that, when some vertex
a
is reconnected to the tree
T
, then its descendants will never be examined). Once a vertex
a
is examined, it is either connected to the tree, or it is added to
R
. In the latter case, all children of
a
are added to
Q
. This ensures that, throughout the algorithm, if
a
is a vertex of
Q
with smallest key
\lambda(a)
, and
a^{\prime}
is the element lying at the top of
\mathsf{Heap}(a)
, then either
\lambda(a^{\prime})\geq\lambda(a)
, or
a^{\prime}\in T
must hold. Indeed, if
\lambda(a^{\prime})<\lambda(a)
but
a^{\prime}\not\in T
, then we should have examined
a^{\prime}
before, and, if it was added to
R
, then it would have been deleted from
\mathsf{Heap}(a)
.

The first phase terminates once
Q=\emptyset
. It is not hard to see, using standard analysis, that its running time is bounded by
O(1+\sum_{v\in R}\deg_{G}(v)\cdot\log m^{*})
.

In the second phase, we run Dijkstra’s algorithm on the vertices of
R
, up to distance
D
, trying to reconnect them to the tree
T
. We also update the heaps of all vertices of
R
, and of their neighbors, accordingly. This step can also be implemented in time
O(\sum_{v\in R}\deg_{G}(v)\log m^{*})
. Every vertex of
R
that was not reconnected to tree
T
is added to set
S^{*}
.

To summarize, in processing edge-deletion operations, whenever, for any vertex
v
, its distance from
s
increases, we may need to pay
O(\deg_{G}(v)\log m^{*})
in running time. It is then easy to see that the total update time due to processing edge deletions is bounded by
O(m^{*}\cdot D\cdot\log m^{*})
.

Deletion of Isolated Vertices.

Deletion of isolated vertices is straightforward, and takes time
O(1)
per vertex. Note that an isolated vertex may not belong to
T
(unless that vertex is
s
and
T
only contains the vertex
s
), so apart from deleting the vertex from
G
and from
S^{*}
(if it belongs to
S^{*}
), no further updates are necessary.

Vertex Splitting.

Recall that in a vertex-splitting update, we are given a vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus\left\{s\right\}
, and a non-empty set
E^{\prime}\subseteq\delta_{G}(e)
of edges. We need to add a new vertex
v^{\prime}
to the graph, and, for every edge
e=(v,u)\in E^{\prime}
, insert an edge
e^{\prime}=(v^{\prime},u)
of length
\ell(e)
into
G
.

If
v\not\in V(T)
, then we simply set
\lambda(v^{\prime})=\lambda(v)=\infty
, add
v^{\prime}
to set
S^{*}
