, and terminate the update algorithm.

Assume now that
v\in V(T)
. Let
\hat{v}
be the parent of the vertex
v
in the tree
T
. We now proceed as follows:

1.

Add a new vertex
v^{\prime}
as the child of
\hat{v}
to the tree
T
(it is convenient to think of it as a copy of
v
); set
\ell(v^{\prime},\hat{v})=\ell(v,\hat{v})
,
\lambda(v^{\prime})=\lambda(v)
, add
\hat{v}
to
\mathsf{Heap}(v^{\prime})
, and add
v^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{Heap}(\hat{v})
.

2.

For every edge
e=(v,u)\in E^{\prime}
with
u\neq\hat{v}
, add an edge
e^{\prime}=(v^{\prime},u)
of length
\ell(e)
to the graph
G
, add
u
to
\mathsf{Heap}(v^{\prime})
, and add
v^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{Heap}(u)
. Notice that the insertion of these edges does not decrease the distance of any vertex from
s
, since
\lambda(v)=\lambda(v^{\prime})
, and every vertex that serves as an endpoint of a newly inserted edge is also a neighbor of
v
.

3.

If edge
(v,\hat{v})\not\in E^{\prime}
, delete the edge
(v^{\prime},\hat{v})
from the graph
G
, and update the
ES-Tree
data structure accordingly.

The processing time of this update procedure, excluding the calls to the edge-deletion updates in the
ES-Tree
data structure, is
O(|E^{\prime}|\cdot\log m^{*})
.
The insertion of edge
(v^{\prime},\hat{v})
into
T
, if this edge does not lie in
E^{\prime}
, increases the total number of edges inserted into
G
. However, since
|E^{\prime}|\geq 1
must hold, this new inserted edge can be charged to some edge of
E^{\prime}
, increasing the total number of edges that are ever present in
G
by at most factor
2
.
From the above discussion, if we denote the length of the input update sequence
\Sigma
by
k
, the total update time of the
ES-Tree
data structure is bounded by
O((m^{*}\cdot D+k)\cdot\log m^{*})
.

Lastly, observe that each update operation in
\Sigma
either inserts at least one edge into
H
, or deletes at least one vertex or an edge from
H
. Since the initial graph
G
is connected,
k\leq O(m^{*})
holds, and the total running time of the algorithm is at most
O(m^{*}\cdot D\cdot\log m^{*})
.

Responding to Queries
SSSP-query
.

Recall that in
SSSP-query
, we are given a vertex
x\in V(G)
, and our goal is to either correctly establish, in time
O(1)
, that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(s,x)>D
, or to return a shortest
s
-
x
path
P
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
. Given a query vertex
x
, we check whether
\lambda(x)=\infty
. If so, we report that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(s,x)>D
. Otherwise, we retrace the unique path
P
connecting
x
to
s
in the tree
T
, and return it, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Appendix C
Completing the Proof of
Theorem

1.4

In this subsection we prove
Theorem

1.4
using the algorithm from
Theorem

3.8
. The proof is practically identical to a proof of a similar statement that appeared in
[
Chu21
]
.
We can assume that graph
H
has no isolated vertices, as all such vertices can be ignored (e.g. each such vertex can be placed in a separate cluster). We will also assume w.l.o.g. that
\mu\geq 3
holds, since otherwise we can simply increase
\mu
to
\mu=3
; this does not affect the asymptotic bounds in the theorem statement. Using the arguments from Section
3.2.1
, at the cost of losing a factor
2
in the approximation ratio, we can assume that
D\leq 3\hat{W}/\mu\leq\hat{W}
.
As in
[
Chu21
]
, the main idea of the proof is to apply the algorithm from
Theorem

3.8
recursively, for smaller and smaller distance bounds. Specifically, we prove the following lemma by induction.

Lemma C.1
.

There is a universal constant
\hat{c}
, and a deterministic algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, that, given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H=(V,U,E),\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, and parameters
\hat{W}
,
1/(\log\hat{W})^{100}\leq\epsilon<1/400
, and
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
such that, if we denote by
N^{0}(H)
the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then
N^{0}(H)\cdot\mu\leq\hat{W}
and
D\leq 6\hat{W}^{\epsilon i}
, achieves approximation factor
\alpha_{i}=(\log\log\hat{W})^{\hat{c}i\cdot 2^{\hat{c}/\epsilon^{2}}}
, and has total update time at most:
\left(\hat{c}^{i}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
.
Moreover, the algorithm ensures that for every regular vertex
v\in V
, the total number of clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, to which vertex
v
ever belongs over the course of the algorithm, is bounded by
\hat{W}^{4i\epsilon^{4}}
.

Theorem

1.4
follows immediately from
Lemma

C.1
, by using
i=\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
.
In the remainder of this subsection, we focus on proving
Lemma

C.1
.

The proof is by induction on
i
. The base case is when
i=1
, so
D\leq 6\hat{W}^{\epsilon}
.
We use the algorithm from
Theorem

3.8
, whose approximation factor is
(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}\leq(\log\log\hat{W})^{\hat{c}\cdot 2^{\hat{c}/\epsilon^{2}}}=\alpha_{1}
(if constant
\hat{c}
is large enough), and total update time is bounded by:

O\left((N^{0}(H))^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot\mu^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot D^{3}\right)\leq\left(\hat{c}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right),

as required.

For the induction step, we consider some integer
1<i\leq\left\lceil 1/\epsilon\right\rceil
, and assume that the lemma holds for all integers below
i
.
Consider the input graph
H
and the distance threshold
D\leq 6\hat{W}^{\epsilon i}
.
We use a parameter
D^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\hat{W}^{\epsilon(i-1)}/4\right\rfloor
.
Clearly,
D^{\prime}\geq\hat{W}^{\epsilon(i-1)}/8
.
We say that an edge
e
of
H
is
long
if
\ell(e)>D^{\prime}
, and we say that it is
short
otherwise.

Let
H^{\prime}
be a dynamic graph that is obtained from
H
by deleting all long edges from it. We can generate a valid update sequence for graph
H^{\prime}
from the valid update input sequence
\Sigma
for graph
H
in a natural way, by ignoring all updates concerning long edges.
Recall that the input to the supernode-splitting update is a supernode
u\in U
and a nonempty set of edges
E^{\prime}\subseteq\delta_{H}(u)
. By deleting from
E^{\prime}
all long edges, we get a valid input for supernode-splitting operation in
H^{\prime}
(if
E^{\prime}
only contains long edges, then we ignore this update).
We can now define a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}=\left(H^{\prime},\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H^{\prime})},4D^{\prime}\right)
, that undergoes a sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
. If
N^{0}(H^{\prime})
denotes the number of regular vertices in the initial graph
H^{\prime}
, then
N^{0}(H^{\prime})=N^{0}(H)
, and
N^{0}(H^{\prime})\cdot\mu\leq\hat{W}
holds. Since
4D^{\prime}\leq\hat{W}^{\epsilon(i-1)}
, we can apply the algorithm from the induction hypothesis to input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}
, and sequence
\Sigma^{\prime}
of valid update operations, distance bound
4D^{\prime}
. Recall that the approximation factor of the algorithm is
\alpha_{i-1}
, and its total update time is bounded by

\left(\hat{c}^{i-1}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
.
Additionally, the algorithm ensures that for every regular vertex
v\in V
, the total number of clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, to which vertex
v
ever belongs over the course of the algorithm, is bounded by
\Delta=\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime})
the corresponding data structure, and by

{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
the collection of clusters that the algorithm maintains.
We denote the algorithm that we have described so far, that maintains the data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime})
, by
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{1}
.

We use the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
in order to define another dynamic graph
\hat{H}
, as follows.
We start with letting
\hat{H}=H
, and then round all edge lengths up to the nearest integral multiple of
D^{\prime}
, denoting the resulting new length of each edge
e
by
\hat{\ell}(e)
.
Notice that, if
e
is a short edge, then
\ell(e)\leq\hat{\ell}(e)=D^{\prime}
, and if
e
is a long edge, then
\ell(e)\leq\hat{\ell}(e)\leq 2\ell(e)
. Additionally, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, we add a supernode
u(C)
to graph
\hat{H}
, and connect
u(C)
with an edge to every
regular
vertex
v\in V(H^{\prime})
that lies in
C
; the length of this edge is
4D^{\prime}
. This ensures that the length of every edge in
\hat{H}
is an integral multiple of
D^{\prime}
. For each time point
\tau
, we denote by
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
the graph
\hat{H}
obtained immediately after the
\tau
th update in sequence
\Sigma
to graph
H
is processed.

We now proceed as follows. First, we show an algorithm to construct an initial graph
\hat{H}^{(0)}
, and an algorithm to produce an online sequence of valid update operations
\hat{\Sigma}
for this graph, so that, at every time
\tau
, the resulting graph that we obtain is precisely
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
. We will also show that, for every pair of regular vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in V(H)
, the distance between them in
\hat{H}
is close to that in
H
. We will use the algorithm from
Theorem

3.8
on graph
\hat{H}
, to maintain a neighborhood cover
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
of regular vertices in graph
\hat{H}
. Lastly, we show that we can use the resulting dynamic neighborhood cover
\hat{C}
for graph
\hat{H}
in order to maintain the desired neighborhood cover for graph
H
.

Maintaining Graph
\hat{H}
.

Recall that, from the definition of the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem, initially, the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
of clusters consists of a single cluster
H^{\prime}
. As the time progresses, the clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
may only be updated via allowed change operations:
\operatorname{DeleteVertex}
,
\operatorname{AddSuperNode}
, and
\operatorname{ClusterSplit}
.
We define the initial graph
\hat{H}^{(0)}
as follows. We set
\hat{H}^{(0)}=H
, except that we set the edge lengths
\left\{\hat{\ell}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(\hat{H}^{(0)})}
as described above. Additionally, we add a single supernode
u(H^{\prime})
, that connects to every regular vertex of
H^{\prime}
with an edge of length
4D^{\prime}
. We use the following claim in order to produce input update sequence
\hat{\Sigma}
for
\hat{H}
.

Claim C.2
.

There is a deterministic algorithm that, given, at each time
\tau\geq 1
, the
\tau
-th update
\sigma_{\tau}
in the input update sequence
\Sigma
for
{\mathcal{J}}
, produces a sequence
\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
of valid update operations for graph
\hat{H}
, such that, for all
\tau\geq 0
, the graph obtained from
\hat{H}^{(0)}
by applying the update sequence
(\hat{\Sigma}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau})
to it is precisely
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
.
The dynamic degree bound for the resulting dynamic graph
\hat{H}
is at most
\mu+\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
.
The total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(\hat{c}^{i-1}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
.

Proof.

We run Algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{1}
, that solves the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on graph input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}=\left(H^{\prime},\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H^{\prime})},4D^{\prime}\right)
, that undergoes a sequence of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
. Recall that the algorithm
maintains the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, achieves approximation factor
\alpha_{i-1}
, and has total update time at most
\left(\hat{c}^{i-1}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
.
We are also guaranteed that for every regular vertex
v
of
H^{\prime}
, the total number of clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
that ever contain
v
is bounded by
\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
.

Recall that initially,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}=\left\{H^{\prime}\right\}
. Consider now some time
\tau>0
, when an update
\sigma_{\tau}\in\Sigma
for input structure
{\mathcal{J}}
arrives. We initialize
\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau}=\emptyset
, and we start by updating the data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime})
with the update operation
\sigma_{\tau}
, which may result in some changes to the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
. We consider the resulting changes to the clusters one by one.
If the change is
\operatorname{AddSuperNode}
, in which a new supernode is added to some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, then we ignore this update.
If the change is
\operatorname{DeleteVertex}
, where, for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, and a vertex
x\in V(C^{\prime})
, vertex
x
is deleted from
C
, and
x
is a regular vertex, then we add to the sequence
\hat{\Sigma}_{t}
an edge-deletion operation for the edge
(x,u(C))
; if
x
is a supernode then we ignore this change.
If the change is
\operatorname{ClusterSplit}
, where, for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, we create a new cluster
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
, then we add a supernode-splitting operation to
\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
, defined as follows. The supernode-splitting is applied to supernode
u(C)
, and the corresponding set
E^{\prime}
of edges contains all edges
(u(C),v)
, where
v
is a regular vertex of
H
lying in
C^{\prime}
. The new supernode that is added to graph
\hat{H}
is
u(C^{\prime})
, where
C^{\prime}
is the new cluster. Note that the time that is needed in order to compute the set
E^{\prime}
of edges is
O(|V(C^{\prime})|)
; since the algorithm for the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}
needs to explicitly store the new cluster
C^{\prime}
, this time is subsumed by the time that Algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{1}
takes in order to execute the cluster-splitting update.

Lastly, we consider the update operation
\sigma_{\tau}
itself, and add additional updates to
\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
as follows. If
\sigma_{\tau}
is the deletion of an isolated vertex
x
from graph
H
, then
x
must also currently be an isolated vertex of
\hat{H}
(as it was just deleted from all clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
containing it). We then add isolated vertex deletion operation for vertex
x
to
\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
. If
\sigma_{\tau}
is the deletion of an edge
e
, then add to
\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
the deletion of
e
.
Lastly, if
\sigma_{\tau}
is a supernode-splitting operation, for a supernode
u
of graph
H
, whose corresponding set of edges is
E^{\prime}\subseteq\delta_{H}(u)
, then we perform the same supernode-splitting operation in graph
\hat{H}
, with edge set
E^{\prime}
.
This completes the description of the algorithm for producing the sequence
\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
.

It is immediate to verify that for all
\tau>0
, the graph obtained by applying update sequence
(\hat{\Sigma}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{\Sigma}_{\tau})
to graph
\hat{H}^{(0)}
is precisely
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
.
Next, we bound the dynamic vertex degree for the resulting dynamic graph
\hat{H}
. Recall that, from the statement of
Lemma

C.1
, for every regular vertex
v
of graph
H^{\prime}
, the total number of clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
that ever contain
v
is bounded by
\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
. Therefore, for every regular vertex
v
of
\hat{H}
, there may be at most
\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, such that vertex
v
is connected with an edge to supernode
u(C)
. Since the dynamic degree bound of every regular vertex in
H
is at most
\mu
, we get that the dynamic degree bound for
\hat{H}
is
\mu+\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
.

The total update time of this algorithm is subsumed by the total update time of Algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{1}
, and is bounded by
\left(\hat{c}^{i-1}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
.

Distance Preservation.

The following two lemmas were proved in
[
Chu21
]
. Since our construction of graph
\hat{H}^{(\tau)}
from graph
H^{(\tau)}
for all
\tau\geq 0
is identical to that of
[
Chu21
]
, the proofs of both lemmas are identical to those in
[
Chu21
]
, and are omitted here. The only difference is that the value of the parameter
\alpha_{i-1}
that we use is different from that in
[
Chu21
]
, but it plays no role in the proofs.

Lemma C.3
(Lemma C.2 in
[
Chu21
]
).

Throughout the algorithm, for every pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V(H)
of regular vertices, if
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})\leq D
, then
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(v,v^{\prime})\leq 20\cdot\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})+8D^{\prime}
.

Lemma C.4
(Claim C.3 in
[
Chu21
]
).

There is a deterministic algorithm, that we call

\operatorname{AlgTransformPath}
, that, given a path
P
in graph
\hat{H}
, connecting a pair
v,v^{\prime}
of regular vertices, computes a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H
, connecting the same pair of vertices, such that
\ell_{H}(P^{\prime})\leq\alpha_{i-1}\cdot\hat{\ell}_{\hat{H}}(v,v^{\prime})
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)
.

Remainder of the Algorithm.

Consider now the dynamic graph
\hat{H}
.
Recall that the length of every edge in graph
\hat{H}
is an integral multiple of
D^{\prime}
.

Let
\hat{H}^{\prime}
be a graph that is identical to
\hat{H}
, except that for every edge
e\in E(\hat{H})
, we set its new length
\hat{\ell}^{\prime}(e)=\hat{\ell}(e)/D^{\prime}
(recall that
\hat{\ell}(e)
is the length of
e
in graph
\hat{H}
).

We set
\hat{D}=50D/D^{\prime}
. Since
D\leq 6\hat{W}^{\epsilon i}
, while
D^{\prime}=\left\lfloor\hat{W}^{\epsilon(i-1)}/4\right\rfloor
, we get that
\hat{D}\leq\Theta(\hat{W}^{\epsilon})
.
Notice that, for every edge
e
of
\hat{H}^{\prime}
,
\hat{\ell}^{\prime}(e)\leq\hat{D}
. Therefore, we have now defined a valid input structure
\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}=\left(\hat{H}^{\prime},\left\{\hat{\ell}^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(\hat{H}^{\prime})},\hat{D}\right)
.
Using the algorithm from
C.2
, we obtain an online sequence
\hat{\Sigma}=(\hat{\Sigma}_{1}\circ\hat{\Sigma}_{2}\circ\cdots)
of valid update operations for graph
\hat{H}^{\prime}
, with dynamic degree bound
\mu^{\prime}\leq\mu+\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
where
\mu
is the dynamic degree bound of graph
H
.
Let
N^{0}(\hat{H}^{\prime})=N^{0}(H)
be the number of regular vertices that belonged to graph
\hat{H}^{\prime}
initially. We set
\hat{W}^{\prime}=\hat{W}^{1+4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}
.
Since
N^{0}(H)\cdot\mu\leq\hat{W}
, we get that
N^{0}(\hat{H}^{\prime})\cdot\mu^{\prime}\leq\hat{W}^{\prime}
. Clearly
\epsilon\geq 1/(\log\hat{W}^{\prime})^{100}
continue to hold.

We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

3.8
in order to maintain a solution to the
\mathsf{RecDynNC}
problem on graph
\hat{H}^{\prime}
, with distance bound
\hat{D}
, and parameter
\hat{W}^{\prime}
replacing
\hat{W}
, with parameter
\epsilon
remaining unchanged. Recall that the approximation factor that the algorithm achieves is:

\hat{\alpha}=(\log\log\hat{W}^{\prime})^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}\leq(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}.

The total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left((N^{0}(H))^{1+O(\epsilon)}\cdot(\mu^{\prime})^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\hat{D}^{3}\right)\leq\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon},

since
\hat{D}\leq\Theta(\hat{W}^{\epsilon})
, and
\mu^{\prime}\leq\mu\cdot\hat{W}^{4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}\leq\mu\cdot\hat{W}^{4\epsilon^{3}}
.

Moreover, the algorithm ensures that for every regular vertex
v\in V
, the total number of clusters in the weak neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}
that the algorithm maintains, to which vertex
v
ever belongs over the course of the algorithm, is bounded by:

(\hat{W}^{\prime})^{4\epsilon^{4}}\leq\left(\hat{W}^{1+4(i-1)\epsilon^{4}}\right)^{4\epsilon^{4}}\leq\hat{W}^{4i\epsilon^{4}}.

We denote the corresponding data structure by
{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{H}^{\prime})
. The corresponding neighborhood cover is denoted by
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, and the algorithm that we have just described, for maintaining the data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{H}^{\prime})
, by
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{2}
. For every regular vertex
v
of
\hat{H}^{\prime}
, we denote the cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(x)
that the algorithm maintains by
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{\prime}(x)
.

The neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{C}}
that we maintain for graph
H
is defined as follows.
For every cluster
\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, we define the corresponding cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Cluster
C
is a subgraph of
H
induced by the set
S(\hat{C})=V(\hat{C})\cap V(H)
of vertices.
Once set
{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters is initialized from set
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
at the beginning of the algorithm, all updates to the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
can be implemented via allowed update operations. Indeed, if
\hat{C}
is a cluster in
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, and
C
is the corresponding cluster in
{\mathcal{C}}
, then cluster
C
may only need to be updated if cluster
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
undergoes
\operatorname{DeleteVertex}
or
\operatorname{AddSuperNode}
update, and the affected vertex lies in graph
H
. In such a case, we perform an identical update to cluster
C
. if cluser
\hat{C}
undergoes a cluster-splitting operation, which results in the addition of a new cluster
\hat{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\hat{C}
to set
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, then we let
C^{\prime}
be the subgraph of
H
induced by the set
V(\hat{C}^{\prime})\cap V(H)
of vertices; since
\hat{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\hat{C}
, it is easy to verify that
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
, so we can create a new cluster
C^{\prime}
via the cluster-splitting update applied to cluster
C
, and add it to
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Since every regular vertex
v
of
\hat{H}^{\prime}
may ever belong to at most
\hat{W}^{4i\epsilon^{4}}
clusters of
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
over the course of the entire algorithm, it is immediate to verify that every regular vertex
v
of
H
may ever belong to at most
\hat{W}^{4i\epsilon^{4}}
clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
over the course of the algorithm.

For every regular vertex
v
, and for all time points
\tau
, we set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
as follows. Let
\hat{C}
be the cluster
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{\prime}(v)
that data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{H}^{\prime})
maintains at time
\tau
, and let
C
be the cluster of
{\mathcal{C}}
corresponding to
\hat{C}
. We then set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C
.
We will use the following observation to show that, if
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
, then
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(C)
.

Observation C.5
.

Let
v
be a regular vertex of
H
,
\hat{C}
a cluster of
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, and
C
the cluster of
{\mathcal{C}}
corresponding to
\hat{C}
. Assume that, at some time
\tau
,
B_{\hat{H}^{\prime}}(v,\hat{D})\subseteq V(\hat{C})
. Then, at time
\tau
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(C)
.

Proof.

Consider any regular vertex
v\in V(H)
, a cluster
\hat{C}
of
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
, and the corresponding cluser
C
of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
We fix some time
\tau
, and whenever we refer to graphs, data structures, or other dynamic objects in this proof, we refer to them at time
\tau
.

Denote
X=B_{H}(v,D)
. Recall that, from
Lemma

C.3
, for every regular vertex
v^{\prime}\in X
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(v,v^{\prime})\leq 20\cdot\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})+8D^{\prime}
, and so
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}^{\prime}}(v,v^{\prime})\leq\frac{20\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})+8D^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}}\leq\hat{D}
.
Similarly, if
u\in X
is a supernode, then there is a regular vertex
v^{\prime}\in X
, that is a neighbor of
u
, with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})\leq\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,u)-\ell_{H}(v^{\prime},u)
.
We then get that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(v,v^{\prime})\leq 20\cdot\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})+8D^{\prime}
, and
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}}(v,u)\leq 20\cdot\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(v,v^{\prime})+9D^{\prime}+\ell_{H}(v^{\prime},u)\leq 20D+9D^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{\hat{H}^{\prime}}(v,u)\leq(20D+9D^{\prime})/D^{\prime}\leq\hat{D}
.
We conclude that

X=B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq B_{\hat{H}^{\prime}}(v,\hat{D})
.

Since
B_{\hat{H}^{\prime}}(v,\hat{D})\subseteq V(\hat{C})
, we get that
X\subseteq V(\hat{C})
also holds. Since
V(C)=V(\hat{C})\cap V(H)
, and
X\subseteq V(H)
, we get that
X\subseteq V(C)
, as required.

Consider now some regular vertex
v\in V(H)
, and assume that, at time
\tau
,
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C
held. Let
\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}
be the corresponding cluster of
{\mathcal{C}}
, so
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{\prime}(v)=\hat{C}
. Then
B_{\hat{H}^{\prime}}(v,\hat{D})\subseteq V(\hat{C})
must hold, and, from
C.5
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(C)
holds.

It is also easy to see that our assignment of covering clusters obeys the Consistent Covering property. Indeed, consider any regular vertex
v
of
H
, and two time points
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
. Let
C=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)
at time
\tau
, and let
C^{\prime}=\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(C)
. It is enough to show that, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
held.

Let
\hat{C},\hat{C}^{\prime}
be the clusters of
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
corresponding to
C
and
C^{\prime}
, respectively. Then, at time
\tau
,
\hat{C}=\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}^{\prime}(v)
, and
\operatorname{Ancestor}^{(\tau^{\prime})}(\hat{C})=\hat{C}^{\prime}
. From the Consistent Covering property of data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{H}^{\prime})
, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{\hat{H}^{\prime}}(v,\hat{D})\subseteq V(\hat{C}^{\prime})
held. From
C.5
, at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
B_{H}(v,D)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})
held. This establishes the Consistent Covering property of our algorithm.

Responding to queries

We now show an algorithm for responding to queries
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C,v,v^{\prime})
, where
C
is a cluster in
{\mathcal{C}}
, and
v,v^{\prime}\in V(C)

are regular vertices lying in
C
. Denote
S^{\prime}=V(C)
, and let
\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}
be the corresponding cluster (with
S^{\prime}\subseteq V(\hat{C})
). We run query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(\hat{C},v,v^{\prime})
in data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{H}^{\prime})
, obtaining a path
P
in graph
\hat{H}^{\prime}
, connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
, of length at most
\hat{D}\cdot\hat{\alpha}\leq\frac{50D}{D^{\prime}}\cdot(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}
(we have used the fact that
\hat{D}=50D/D^{\prime}
and
\hat{\alpha}\leq(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}
). Note that the length of path
P
in graph
\hat{H}
is at most
50D\cdot(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}}\leq D\cdot(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{2}}}
. The time required to process query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(\hat{C},v,v^{\prime})
in data structure
{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{H}^{\prime})
is
O(|E(P)|)
. Lasly, we apply Algorithm
\operatorname{AlgTransformPath}
from
Lemma

C.4
to path
P
in graph
\hat{H}
, to obtain a path
P^{\prime}
in graph
H
, connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
, whose length is bounded by:

\begin{split}\alpha_{i-1}\cdot\hat{\ell}_{\hat{H}}(v,v^{\prime})&\leq\alpha_{i-1}\cdot D\cdot(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{2}}}\\
&\leq D\cdot(\log\log\hat{W})^{\hat{c}(i-1)\cdot 2^{\hat{c}/\epsilon^{2}}}\cdot(\log\log\hat{W})^{2^{\tilde{c}/\epsilon^{2}}}\\
&\leq D\cdot(\log\log\hat{W})^{\hat{c}i\cdot 2^{\hat{c}/\epsilon^{2}}}\\
&=D\cdot\alpha_{i}.\end{split}

The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)
.

From the above discussion, and the fact that our algorithm supports
short-path-query
queries, it is immediate to verify that, throughout the algorithm,
{\mathcal{C}}
is a weak
(D,\alpha_{i}\cdot D)
-neighborhood cover of the regular vertices of
H
.

Total Update Time.

We now bound the total update time of the algorithm. The update time is dominated by the update time of the algorithm from
C.2
, and the algorithm
\mbox{\sf{Alg}}_{2}
. The former has update time
O\left(\hat{c}^{i-1}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
, while the latter has update time
O\left(\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
. Since we can assume that
\hat{c}
is a sufficiently large constant, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
\left(\hat{c}^{i}\cdot\hat{W}^{1+\hat{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{\hat{c}/\epsilon}\right)
.

Appendix D
Proof of
Lemma

5.4

Throughout, we use a parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
. Since
1/(\log N)^{1/50}\leq\epsilon<1/400
, it is easy to verify that
1/(\log N^{\epsilon})^{1/24}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}<1/400
, and so in particular,
1/(\log(N^{0}(H)))^{1/24}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}<1/400
holds.
Note that, if
\mu\geq(N^{0}(H))^{1/10}
, then
Theorem

5.3
, with parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}
replacing
\epsilon
, provides an algorithm with the desired guarantees. We will assume from now on that
\mu<(N^{0}(H))^{1/10}
.

The proof is by induction on
i
.
The base case is
i=3
.
We assume that we are given as input a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E},D\right)
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, such that, if
N^{\prime}
denotes the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then
N^{\prime}\leq N^{3\epsilon}
.
Using the arguments from
Section

3.2.1
, at the cost of losing a factor
2
in the approximation ratio, we can assume that
D\leq 3N^{\prime}
.
Let
V^{\prime}
denote the set of all regular vertices that lie in graph
H
at te beginning of the algorithm. We start with
{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{H\right\}
, and then, over the course of
|V^{\prime}|
iterations, we create clusters
C_{v}=H
for all
v\in V^{\prime}
, by splitting them off from
H
. In the remainder of the algorithm, we will maintain the collection
{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{C_{v}\mid v\in V^{\prime}\right\}
of clusters. As
H
undergoes valid update operations, we perform similar updates in the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}
, as described in Section
3.2.2
. Once a regular vertex
v
is deleted from
V(H)
, we delete all edges and vertices from
C_{v}
.
Throughout the algorithm, we also set
\mbox{\sf{CoveringCluster}}(v)=C_{v}
for all
v\in V^{\prime}
. For each vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
, we initialize the algorithm from
Theorem

2.6
for maintaining a modified
ES-Tree
in graph
C_{v}
, with source vertex
v
and distance bound
D
, as the graph undergoes valid update operations; since supernode-splitting is a special case of vertex-splitting, every update that graph
C_{v}
undergoes is either edge-deletion, or isolated vertex-deletion, or vertex-splitting.
Whenever some vertex
x\in V(C_{v})
is added to the set
S^{*}
of vertices (in which case
\mbox{\sf dist}_{C_{v}}(x,v)>D
must hold), we delete vertex
x
with its all incident edges from
C_{v}
. We denote the algorithm for maintaining the
ES-Tree
in graph
C_{v}
by
{\mathcal{A}}(C_{v})
. Recall that the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(m^{*}\cdot D\cdot\log m^{*})
, where
m^{*}
is the total number of edges that ever belonged to graph
C_{v}
. Since
m^{*}\leq N^{\prime}\mu
, and
D\leq 3N^{\prime}
, we get that the total update time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(C_{v})
is bounded by
O((N^{\prime}\mu)^{3})
. Lastly, since
|V^{\prime}|\leq N^{\prime}
, the total update time of the whole algorithm is bounded by
O((N^{\prime})^{4}\mu^{3})\leq O(N^{\prime}\cdot N^{12\epsilon}\mu^{3})\leq N^{0}(H)\cdot N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon}\cdot\mu^{4}
, as required. It is easy to verify that lists
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}(x)
for vertices
x\in V(H)
and
\mbox{\sf{ClusterList}}(e)
for edges
e\in E(H)
can be maintained without increasing the asymptotic running time of the algorithm. Every regular vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
belongs to at most
N^{\prime}\leq N^{3\epsilon}\leq\Delta_{3}
clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}
over the course of the algorithm. Lastly, when a query
\mbox{\sf{short-path-query}}(C_{z},x,y)
arrives, where
x,y\in V(C_{z})
, we perform queries
\mbox{\sf{SSSP-query}}(x)
and
\mbox{\sf{SSSP-query}}(y)
in data structure
{\mathcal{A}}(C_{z})
, to obtain a path
P_{1}
of length at most
D
connecting
x
to
z
in time
O(|E(P_{1})|)
, and a path
P_{2}
of length at most
D
connecting
y
to
z
in time
O(|P_{2}|)
. By concatenating both paths, we obtain a path connecting
x
to
y
in cluster
C_{z}
, whose length is at most
2D\leq\alpha_{3}\cdot D
. The running time of the algorithm that responds to the query is
O(|E(P)|)
, and the algorithm achieves approximation factor
4\leq\alpha_{3}
.

We also need to consider separately the special case where
i=4
.
We assume that we are given as input a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, such that, if
N^{\prime}
denotes the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then
N^{\prime}\leq N^{4\epsilon}
. We also assume that
\mu\leq(N^{\prime})^{1/10}
.
Using the arguments from
Section

3.2.1
, at the cost of losing a factor
2
in the approximation, we can assume that
D\leq 3N^{\prime}
.
Denote
W=N^{3\epsilon}
and
\hat{W}^{\prime}=N^{4\epsilon}
. Clearly,

W<\hat{W}^{\prime}\leq W^{1.5}
holds.

Recall that the total update time of the algorithm for the case where
i=3
was bounded by
O((N^{0}(H^{\prime}))^{4}\cdot\mu^{3})
, where
N^{0}(H^{\prime})
is the number of regular vertices in the input graph
H^{\prime}
at the beginning of the algorithm. By setting
\delta=3
,
c^{\prime}=0
, and letting
c
be a large enough constant, this running time can be bounded by
N^{0}(H^{\prime})\cdot W^{\delta}\cdot\mu^{c}
(since
N^{0}(H^{\prime})\leq N^{3\epsilon}=W
in this case). Therefore,
5.1
holds for the parameter
W
that we just defined, with parameters
c^{\prime}=0
,
\delta=3
,
\alpha(N^{\prime}\mu)=\alpha_{3}
, and
\Delta(N^{\prime})=\Delta_{3}
. By applying
Theorem

5.2
with precision parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}
, we obtain an algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}_{4}
, for instance
{\mathcal{J}}
, that achieves approximation factor:

\begin{split}&\max\left\{2^{O(1/(\epsilon^{\prime})^{6})}\cdot\log\log N^{\prime},\frac{\alpha_{3}^{2}\cdot\log\log N^{\prime}}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{16}}\right\}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{24})}\cdot\log\log N^{\prime},\frac{\left((\log\log(N^{2}))^{\tilde{c}\cdot 2^{6}/\epsilon^{24}}\right)^{2}\cdot\log\log N^{\prime}}{\epsilon^{64}}\right\}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\left((\log\log(N^{2}))^{\tilde{c}\cdot 2^{6}/\epsilon^{24}}\right)^{4}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq(\log\log(N^{2}))^{\tilde{c}\cdot 2^{8}/\epsilon^{24}}=\alpha_{4}.\end{split}

The algorithm ensures that, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the number of clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, such that
v
ever belongs to
C
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
(N^{\prime})^{4\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq N^{4\epsilon^{4}}=\Delta_{4}
.

We now bound the total update time of the algorithm.
In order to bound the total update time of the algorithm, observe that
\frac{\hat{W}^{\prime}}{W}=N^{\epsilon}
, and that
W^{\delta}=N^{9\epsilon}
. Therefore, from
Theorem

5.2
, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(N^{\prime}\cdot N^{9\epsilon}\cdot N^{3\epsilon\cdot c\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\Delta_{3}^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\log N)^{3c}\right)+O\left(N^{\prime}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon\cdot\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot D^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\Delta_{3}\cdot N^{8\epsilon}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq N^{\prime}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot D^{3}\cdot\mu^{c}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq N^{0}(H)\cdot N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+4\tilde{c}\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\mu^{4\tilde{c}}\cdot D^{3},\end{split}

if
\tilde{c}
is sufficiently large
(we have used the fact that
\Delta_{3}=N^{3\epsilon}
, and, since
\epsilon\geq 1/(\log N)^{4}
,
N^{\epsilon}>\log N
holds.).

For the step of the induction, we consider an integer
4<i\leq q
. We assume that the claim holds for integer
i-1
, and prove it for
i
.
We assume that we are given as input a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}=\left(H,\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)},D\right)
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations with dynamic degree bound
\mu
, such that, if
N^{\prime}
denotes the number of regular vertices in
H
at the beginning of the algorithm, then
N^{(i-1)\epsilon}<N^{\prime}\leq N^{i\epsilon}
(if
N^{\prime}\leq N^{(i-1)\epsilon}
, then we can use the algorithm from the induction hypothesis). We also assume that
\mu\leq(N^{\prime})^{1/10}
.
Using the arguments from
Section

3.2.1
, at the cost of losing a factor
2
in the approximation, we can assume that
D\leq 3N^{\prime}
.
Denote
W=N^{(i-1)\epsilon}
and
\hat{W}^{\prime}=N^{\prime}
. Since
i>4
,

W<\hat{W}^{\prime}\leq W^{1.5}
holds. We also use a precision parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
, as before.

From the induction hypothesis, there is an algorithm that, given a valid input structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}
undergoing a sequence of valid update operations with the dynamic degree bound
\mu^{\prime}
, such that, if
N^{\prime\prime}
is the number of regular vertices in the initial graph, then
N^{\prime\prime}\leq W
holds, achieves approximation factor
\alpha_{i-1}
, and has total update time
at most:

\begin{split}N^{\prime\prime}\cdot N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+\tilde{c}(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}\cdot(\mu^{\prime})^{\tilde{c}(i-1)}\cdot D^{3}.\end{split}

The algorithm also ensures that every regular vertex belongs to at most
\Delta_{i-1}
clusters over the course of the algorithm.

We choose a parameter
\delta
, so that
N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+\tilde{c}(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}=W^{\delta}
. Since
W=N^{(i-1)\epsilon}
, it is easy to see that
\delta=\frac{\tilde{c}+\tilde{c}(i-1)\epsilon}{i-1}
.

By setting
c=\tilde{c}\cdot(i-1)
, we get that the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by
N^{\prime\prime}\cdot W^{\delta}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\mu^{\prime})^{c}
. Therefore,
5.1
holds for the parameter
W
that we just defined,
c^{\prime}=3
,
\alpha(N^{\prime\prime})=\alpha_{i-1}
, and
\Delta(N^{\prime\prime})=\Delta_{i-1}
,
and the parameters
\delta
and
c
as defined above. By using
Theorem

5.2
with precision parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}
, we obtain an algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, for instance
{\mathcal{J}}
, that achieves approximation factor:

\begin{split}&\max\left\{2^{O(1/(\epsilon^{\prime})^{6})}\cdot\log\log N,\frac{\alpha_{i-1}^{2}\cdot\log\log N}{(\epsilon^{\prime})^{16}}\right\}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\max\left\{2^{O(1/\epsilon^{24})}\cdot\log\log N,\frac{\left((\log\log(N^{2}))^{\tilde{c}\cdot 2^{2(i-1)}/\epsilon^{24}}\right)^{2}\cdot\log\log N}{\epsilon^{64}}\right\}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\left((\log\log(N^{2}))^{\tilde{c}\cdot 2^{2(i-1)}/\epsilon^{24}}\right)^{4}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq(\log\log(N^{2}))^{\tilde{c}\cdot 2^{2i}/\epsilon^{24}}=\alpha_{i}.\end{split}

The algorithm ensures that, for every regular vertex
v
of
H
, the number of clusters
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, such that
v
ever belongs to
C
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
(N^{\prime})^{4\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq N^{4\epsilon^{4}}=\Delta_{i}
.

It now remains to bound the total update time of the algorithm. Recall that
W^{\delta}=N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+\tilde{c}(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}
,
\frac{\hat{W}}{W}=N^{\epsilon}
,
W=N^{(i-1)\epsilon}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{4}
,
c^{\prime}=3
, and
c=\tilde{c}\cdot(i-1)
. Recall also that
i-1\leq 1/\epsilon
. From
Theorem

5.2
, the total update time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(N^{\prime}\cdot W^{\delta+c\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\Delta_{i-1}^{c}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\log D)^{c}\right)+O\left(N^{\prime}\cdot W^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot D^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{W}\right)^{8}\cdot\Delta_{i-1}\right)\\
&\leq O\left(N^{\prime}\cdot N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+\tilde{c}(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}\cdot N^{c(i-1)\epsilon^{5}}\cdot N^{4c\epsilon^{4}}\cdot\mu^{c}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\log N)^{c}\right)+O\left(N^{\prime}\cdot N^{O((i-1)\epsilon^{5})}\cdot D^{3}\cdot\mu^{4}\cdot N^{9\epsilon}\right)\\
&\leq O\left(N^{\prime}\cdot N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+\tilde{c}(i-1)\epsilon^{2}}\cdot N^{5\tilde{c}\epsilon^{3}}\cdot\mu^{\tilde{c}(i-1)}\cdot D^{3}\cdot(\log N)^{\tilde{c}(i-1)}\right)\\
&\leq N^{\prime}\cdot N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon+\tilde{c}i\epsilon^{2}}\mu^{\tilde{c}i}\cdot D^{3}.\end{split}

(We have used the facts that

\Delta_{i-1}=N^{4\epsilon^{4}}
, and, since
\epsilon\geq 1/(\log N)^{1/24}
,
\log N<N^{\epsilon^{4}}
holds. In particular,
(\log N)^{\tilde{c}(i-1)}\leq N^{\tilde{c}(i-1)\epsilon^{4}})\leq N^{\tilde{c}\epsilon^{3}}
.)

Appendix E
Proof of
6.4

Throughout the proof, when we refer to
copies
of a regular vertex
x\in V
, we only refer to copies of
x
that lie in the clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\cup\left\{H^{\prime}\right\}
; we ignore copies of
x
that belong to clusters of
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
.
Since, for all
1\leq j\leq r
, vertices may join set
S_{\geq j}
over the course of the algorithm, but they may never leave it,
it is enough to prove that, at the end of the algorithm,
|S_{\geq j}|\leq N^{1-j\hat{\epsilon}}
holds for all
1\leq j\leq r
. The proof is by induction on
j
.

The base is
j=1
. Consider any regular vertex
x\in V
. Note that a new copy of vertex
x
may only be created over the course of Phase 1 of a Flag-Lowering Operations, during one of the iterations when some terminal
t
was processed, via the
algorithm from

Lemma

2.1
. Let
1<i_{t}\leq\frac{2r+6}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
be the integer that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
returned. Recall that we have denoted
B_{t}=B_{C}(t,4(i_{t}-1)D)
and
B^{\prime}_{t}=B_{C}(t,4i_{t}D)
, where
C
is the cluster to which the Flag Lowering operation is applied. The algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
ensured that
|S_{0}\cap B^{\prime}_{t}|\leq|S_{0}\cap B_{t}|\cdot N^{\hat{\epsilon}}
. The vertices of
B_{t}
are then deleted from graph
H^{\prime}
, and the only vertices for which new copies are created are the vertices of
B^{\prime}_{t}\setminus B_{t}
. We assign, to every regular vertex
y\in B_{t}
, a charge of
N^{\hat{\epsilon}}
. Notice that the total charge to all regular vertices of
B_{t}
is at least as large as
|S_{0}\cap(B^{\prime}_{t}\setminus B_{t})|
, so the charge is at least as large as the number of new copies of vertices in
S_{0}
that were created. Since the vertices of
B_{t}
are deleted from graph
H^{\prime}
during the current iteration, they will never be charged again. Overall, we get that the total charge to all vertices of
V
, over the course of the algorithm, is bounded by
N^{1+\hat{\epsilon}}
. Note that a vertex
x
may only be added to set
S_{\geq 1}
when
n_{x}\geq N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}+1
, so at least
N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}
copies of vertex
x
have been created. Since the total number of copies of all vertices in
S_{0}
that are ever created is bounded by
N^{1+\hat{\epsilon}}
, we get that
|S_{\geq 1}|\leq\frac{N^{1+\hat{\epsilon}}}{N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}}\leq N^{1-\hat{\epsilon}}
holds.

Consider now some integer
j>1
, and assume that the claim holds for
j-1
. Consider some vertex
x\in V
, that was added to set
S_{\geq j}
at some time
\tau
during the algorithm’s execution. Let
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
be the time when vertex
x
was added to set
S_{\geq(j-1)}
. Then at time
\tau^{\prime}
,
n_{x}=(j-1)\cdot N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}
held, and at time
\tau
,
n_{x}=j\cdot N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}
held. Therefore, between time
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau
, vertex
x
belonged to class
S_{j-1}
, and during that time,
N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}
new copies of this vertex were created.

As before, new copies of regular vertices may only be created
over the course of Phase 1 of a Flag-Lowering operations, during one of the iterations when some terminal
t
was processed, via the
algorithm from

Lemma

2.1
. Let
1<i_{t}\leq\frac{2r+6}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
be the integer that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
returned. Recall that we have denoted
B_{t}=B_{C}(t,4(i_{t}-1)D)
and
B^{\prime}_{t}=B_{C}(t,4i_{t}D)
, where
C
is the cluster to which the Flag Lowering operation is applied. The algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
ensured that
|S_{j-1}\cap B^{\prime}_{t}|\leq|S_{j-1}\cap B_{t}|\cdot N^{\hat{\epsilon}}
. The vertices of
B_{t}
are then deleted from graph
H^{\prime}
, and the only vertices for which new copies are created are the vertices of
B^{\prime}_{t}\setminus B_{t}
. We assign, to every regular vertex
y\in B_{t}\cap S_{j-1}
, a charge of
N^{\hat{\epsilon}}
. Notice that the total charge to all regular vertices of
B_{t}\cap S_{j-1}
is at least as large as
|S_{j-1}\cap(B^{\prime}_{t}\setminus B_{t})|
, so the charge is at least as large as the number of new copies of vertices in
S_{j-1}
that were created. Since the vertices of
B_{t}\cap S_{j-1}
are deleted from graph
H^{\prime}
during the current iteration, they will never be charged again for the vertices of
S_{j-1}
. Therefore, a vertex that ever belonged to set
S_{j-1}
may only be charged at most once for creating new copies of vertices of
S_{j-1}
, and the amount of the charge is
N^{\hat{\epsilon}}
. Since, from the induction hypothesis, at the end of the algorithm,
|S_{\geq(j-1)}|\leq N^{1-(j-1)\hat{\epsilon}}
holds, the total number of copies of vertices of
S_{j-1}
that were ever created during the algorithm is bounded by
|S_{\geq(j-1)}|\cdot N^{\hat{\epsilon}}\leq J^{1-j\hat{\epsilon}+2\hat{\epsilon}}
. As discussed already, in order for a vertex of
S_{j-1}
to join set
S_{j}
, we need to create at least
N^{2\hat{\epsilon}}
new copies of that vertex. We conclude that the total number of vertices that ever belonged to set
S_{j}
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
N^{1-j\hat{\epsilon}}
. If vertex
v
belongs to
S_{\geq j}
at the end of the algorithm, then it must have belonged ot
S_{j}
at some time during the algorithm. Therefore, at the end of the algorithm,
|S_{\geq j}|\leq N^{1-j\hat{\epsilon}}
holds.
